Sequential Decision Problems with Weak Feedback
Arun Verma

Abstract
Many variants of sequential decision problems that are considered in the literature depend upon the type of feedback and the amount of information they reveal about the associated rewards. Most of the prior work studied the cases where feedback from actions reveals rewards associated with the actions. However, in many areas like crowd-sourcing, medical diagnosis, and adaptive resource allocation, feedback from actions may be weak, i.e., may not reveal any information about rewards at all. Without any information about rewards, it is not possible to learn which action is optimal. Clearly, learning an optimal action is only feasible in such cases if the problem structure is such that an optimal action can be identified without explicitly knowing the rewards. Our goal in this thesis is to study the class of problems where optimal action can be inferred without explicitly knowing the rewards. Specifically, we study Unsupervised Sequential Selection (USS), where rewards/losses for selected actions are never revealed, but the problem structure is amenable to identify the optimal actions. We also introduce a novel setup named Censored Semi-Bandits (CSB), where the reward observed from an action depends on the amount of resources allocated to it. The major part of this thesis focuses on the USS problem. In the USS problem, the loss associated with action cannot be inferred from observed feedback. Such cases arise in many real-life applications. For example, in a medical diagnosis, the patients’ true state may not be known; hence, the test’s effectiveness cannot be known. In crowd-sourcing systems, crowd-sourced workers’ expertise level is unknown; therefore, the quality of their work cannot be known. In such problems, the prediction from test/worker is observed, but one cannot ascertain their reliability due to the absence of ground truth. We show that by comparing the feedback obtained from different actions, one can find the optimal action for a class of USS problems when the ‘weak dominance’ property is satisfied by the problem. For this problem, we develop upper confidence bound and Thompson Sampling based algorithms with optimal performance guarantees. In this thesis, we introduce a new setup called the censored semi-bandits (CSB), where feedback observed from an action depends on the amount of resource allocated. The feedback gets ‘censored’ if enough resource is not allocated. In the CSB setup, a learner allocates resources among different activities (actions) in every round and receives censored loss from each action as feedback. The goal is to learn a policy for resource allocation that minimizes the cumulative loss. The loss at each time step depends on two unknown parameters, one specific to the action but independent of the allocated resources, and the other depends on the amount of resource allocated. More specifically, the loss equals zero if the action’s resource allocation exceeds a constant (but unknown) threshold that can be dependent on the action. The CSB setup finds applications in many resource allocation problems like police patrolling, traffic regulations and enforcement, poaching control, advertisement budget allocation, stochastic network utility maximization, among many others. The last part of this thesis focuses on distributed learning in multi-player multi-armed bandits for identifying the best subset of actions. The setup is such that the reward is only observed for those actions which are played by only one player. These problems find applications in wireless ad hoc networks and cognitive radios to find the best communication channels. Our contribution in this thesis is to address the above described sequential decision problems by exploiting specific structures these problems exhibit. For each of these setups with weak feedback, we develop provably optimal algorithms. Finally, we validate their empirical performance on different problem instances derived from synthetic and real datasets. Keywords: Stochastic Multi-Armed Bandits, Unsupervised Online Learning, Censored Feedback, Upper Confidence Bound, Thompson Sampling, Contextual Bandits, Generalized Linear Models, Multi-Play Multi-Armed Bandits, Combinatorial Semi-Bandits, Stochastic Network Utility Maximization, Pure Exploration, Multi-Player Multi-Armed Bandits

Chapter 1
Introduction

Many real-life systems involve actions being applied sequentially, with each action resulting in a certain reward. The goal is to apply an action from a set of available actions that results in maximum reward (or minimum cost) in each round. However, which action is the best in a given round, may not be known. In such systems, the ‘goodness’ of action has to be inferred from the results of past actions. For example, in the medical diagnosis of an ailment, a doctor prescribes a treatment from a set of available treatments, and by observing their effectiveness, narrows down on the most appropriate treatment
(
Thompson 1933
)
. Such a decision-making process is commonly known as online learning in literature, where the goal is to learn the best action as quickly as possible.

Online machine learning is a branch of machine learning where actions are made sequentially using past feedback, and associated rewards of actions are observed. As opposed to batch learning where all the data are available beforehand, in online machine learning, an instance gets revealed in each round, and after selecting an action, feedback is observed. The feedback could reveal how good the selected action is or may not provide any information at all. The goal is to identify the best action to play in each round. In the batch learning methods, the entire data are available in one go, and the best predictor is obtained using all the data and used for prediction on any new instance. In online learning methods, learning is incremental: as more and more feedback is available, the action to apply in the next instance is dynamically updated using all the past feedback.

Several variants of batch learning methods have been studied in the past: supervised, unsupervised, and active learning. In supervised learning methods, true labels for all data points are known, whereas, in unsupervised methods, they are not known
(
Friedman et al. 2001
;
Shalev-Shwartz and Ben-David 2014
)
. In the active learning (a special case of semi-supervised learning where the algorithm has a small labeled set of training instances with a larger unlabeled set), the labels are not available by default but can be acquired if necessary
(
Settles 2010
)
. For example, obtaining labels could be expensive, in which case labels for the most ’important’ data points can be acquired on payment or with lots of human effort. All three variants in the batch setting are well-studied in literature.

Many variants of online machine learning are considered in the literature, depending on the type of feedback and the amount of information they reveal about the rewards. The multi-armed bandits and the expert setting
(
Auer et al. 2002
;
Bubeck et al. 2012
)
are well-studied problems where feedback provides direct information about the rewards. In the multi-armed bandit setting, feedback observed from an action reveals only the reward associated with that action. However, in the expert setting, the feedback observed from an action reveals reward associated with the action played as well as all the other actions. The settings that span in between these two extreme cases are also studied, namely, bandits with side-information
(
Mannor and Shamir 2011
;
Alon et al. 2013
;
Alon et al. 2015
;
Wu et al. 2015
)
.

The online learning methods studied in the literature work under the assumption that feedback from an action provides information about the reward of that action, though the amount of information revealed could vary from one action to another. It is also possible that the feedback from actions is indirectly tied to the rewards. This setting is referred as partial monitoring setting
(
Cesa-Bianchi et al. 2006
;
Bartók and
Szepesvári 2012a
;
Bartók et al. 2014
)
. It includes all the previously described online learning setups as special cases. A summary of the various online learning setups discussed is shown in below Figure
1.1
.

Figure 1.1
:
Classification of various online learning settings.

The finite partial monitoring setting
(
Bartók et al. 2014
)
is characterized by a pair of
K\times O
matrices, the reward matrix
R
, and the feedback matrix
F
. In each round
t
, the learner selects an action
I_{t}\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
and simultaneously, the environment selects an outcome
J_{t}\in\{1,2,\ldots,O\}
. Then, the learner observes entry
F_{I_{t},J_{t}}
as feedback and receives instantaneous reward
R_{I_{t},J_{t}}
, which is not revealed to him. The feedback can be thought of as masked information about the outcome of
J_{t}
. Sometimes,
F_{I_{t},J_{t}}
may uniquely determine the outcome; otherwise, the feedback may give only partial or no information about the outcome. The learner’s performance is measured in terms of regret, i.e., the total reward
\sum_{t=1}^{T}R_{I_{t},J_{t}}
of the learner is compared with the total reward of best-fixed action in hindsight. Generally, the regret grows with the number of rounds
T
. The learner must have sub-linear regret, which means that the learner’s average per-round reward approaches the average per-round reward of the best action in hindsight.

The partial monitoring setting is the most general online machine learning setting, but most of the earlier work on partial monitoring is restricted to cases where feedback from the actions allows the learner to fully identify the rewards of the actions. However, in many areas like crowd-sourcing, medical diagnosis, security management, resource allocation, and many others, feedback from actions may not even be sufficient to fully identify their rewards. In this thesis, the algorithms that identify optimal actions without requiring explicit reward values are developed. Broadly, the aim is to identify the scenarios where the reward values cannot be identified and then develop algorithms to learn optimal action by exploiting the problem structure. We categorized such problem as
s
equential
d
ecision problems with
w
eak
f
eedback (SDWF).

Our main objective in this thesis is to identify scenarios when it is possible to act optimally in SDWF. Clearly, without any information about rewards, one cannot learn which action is good in general. So, the specific problem structure has to be exploited to learn good actions. We propose to identify a set of necessary and sufficient conditions that lead to the learnability of optimal actions. We then identify classes of problems that satisfy these conditions and prepare a common framework for such classes to study partial monitoring problems with weak feedback. For the class of SDWF problems that can be learned, we develop learning algorithms. The goal is to develop provably optimal algorithms that are also easy to implement in practice. The performance of the developed algorithms is evaluated on synthetic and real datasets drawn from various application fields and compared with natural benchmarks.

In this thesis, the following classes of SDWF problems are studied: Uncensored Sequential Selection, Censored Semi-Bandits, and Collaborative Learning in Multi-Player Multi-Armed Bandits. In subsequent, these classes of SDWF problems are briefly presented.

Unsupervised Sequential Selection

The problems involving the SDWF arise naturally in areas like security, medical diagnosis, and crowd-sourcing, where we may not benefit from knowing the ground-truth associated with the task. For example, in medical diagnosis, patients may not reveal the outcome of treatment due to privacy reasons. Hence, the treatment’s effectiveness may not be known. In crowd-sourcing systems, crowd-sourced workers may not reveal their expertise levels, and hence their effectiveness is not known. We named this class of problems as ‘Unsupervised Sequential Selection’ (USS).

The main difficulty in the study of the USS problem is the lack of reward information. Moreover, the techniques developed for the partial monitoring problem, where rewards are identifiable from feedback, are not applicable to the USS setup. However, one would still like to act optimally. We develop algorithms that learn to select the optimal action when rewards are not fully identifiable from the feedback. Specifically, we exploit the problem structure to learn the optimal action rather than rely on the availability of the reward information.

Censored Semi-Bandits

Sequential allocation problems with a censored feedback structure have received significant interest in recent times
(
Abernethy et al. 2016
)
. Censoring occurs naturally in several applications such as police patrolling of opportunistic crimes, supplier selection, budget allocation, and several others. In all these applications, an allocation to a set of options (allocation of price to items, allocation of the patrol to locations, etc.) is made by a learner, and then the response to the allocation (purchase, crime, etc.) is observed. If a positive response is observed (crime occurs, a purchase is made), the learner receives information about the goodness of allocation. However, if a negative response (censored) is observed (no crime, no purchase, etc.), the learner cannot decide if it is because of a good allocation or because of an inherent low propensity of a response. The goal then is to make repeated allocations and learn the best possible allocation strategy. We model these problems as stochastic semi-bandits and refer to this class of problems as ‘Censored Semi-Bandits’ (CSB).

In the CSB problem, the amount of reward information revealed to the learner via feedback depends on the resource allocation. Hence the reward is only partially observable from feedback. We develop algorithms that first estimate the threshold so that we fully observe the reward from feedback and then find the best resource allocation.

Distributed Learning in Multi-Player Multi-Armed Bandits

We consider a communication network consisting of multiple users and multiple channels where each user wishes to use one of the available channels. A user can transmit on any of the channels. To keep the model more general, users cannot communicate directly between them, and any information about the other users can be obtained only by collisions. In particular, if the user transmits on a channel, it gets a sample of the gain of the channel only if no other user transmits on that channel. Otherwise, it only gets to know that at least one other user is transmitted on that channel. We assume that the expected gain of each of the channels is distinct. Even though users cannot communicate and have to make decisions in a distributed fashion, we consider a non-strategic setting where users have a common goal to maximize the total gain in the network, i.e., to achieve an optimal allocation, which is realized when all the users occupy non-overlapping channels in the top channels. Here the top channels refer to the set of channels with the highest expected gain. As the goal is to achieve optimal network allocation, we assume that each user is satisfied if she gets one of the top channels when no other better channel is free. Our aim in this work is to find a distributed strategy that minimizes the time by which the users reach an optimal allocation while keeping the regret and number of collisions as small as possible. In this thesis, this setup is modeled as ‘Multi-Player Multi-Armed Bandits’ (MP-MAB).

The reward information is the same as feedback in the MP-MAB problem unless two players select the same arm. We propose distributed strategies that assign arms to players and then learn in a distributed fashion by using all players for collecting information about the arms with a minimal number of collisions.

Organization of Thesis

In
Chapter

2
, we introduce Unsupervised Sensor Selection problem. We provide an optimal algorithm for solving the USS problem under certain conditions. We study the contextual version of the USS problem in
Chapter

3
, where the optimal action could be task-dependent. In
Chapter

4
, we proposed a novel framework for resource allocation problems using a variant of semi-bandits named censored semi-bandits. We use censored semi-bandits for solving the stochastic network utility maximization problem. We showcase the application of the CSB setup for network utility maximization. In
Chapter

5
, we set up the channels selection problem in the communication network as a stochastic multi-player multi-armed bandits. We developed distributed strategies without centralized communication that achieve constant regret with a high probability. Since we studied the different sequential decision problems in this thesis, the related literature is given in the respective chapters.

Chapter 2
Unsupervised Sequential Selection

In this chapter, we study
U
nsupervised
S
equential
S
election
(USS) problem. The USS problem is a variant of the stochastic multi-armed bandits problem, where the loss of an arm can not be inferred from the observed feedback. In the USS setup, arms are associated with fixed costs and are ordered, forming a cascade. The learner selects an arm in each round and observes the feedback from arms up to the selected arm. The learner’s goal is to find the arm that minimizes the expected total loss. The total loss is the sum of the cost incurred for selecting the arm and the stochastic loss associated with the selected arm. The problem is challenging because, without knowing the mean loss, one cannot compute the total loss for the selected arm. Clearly, learning is feasible only if the optimal arm can be inferred from the problem structure. It is observed that this happens if the problem satisfies the ‘Weak Dominance’ (WD) property. We set up the USS problem as a stochastic partial monitoring problem and develop algorithms with sub-linear regret under the WD property. We argue that our algorithms are optimal and evaluate its performance on problem instances generated from synthetic and real-world datasets.

2.1
Unsupervised Sequential Selection (USS)

In many applications, one has to trade-off between accuracy and cost. For example, for detecting some event, it is not only the accuracy of a test/sensor that matters, but the associated cost is important as well. Also, one may have to predict labels of instances for which ground-truth cannot be obtained. In such scenarios, feedback about the correctness of predictions remains unknown. Such problems arise naturally in medical diagnosis, crowd-sourcing, security system
(
Hanawal et al. 2017
)
, and online features selection in unsupervised setting. In the medical diagnosis problem, either the true state of the patients may not be known or the patients may not reveal the outcome of treatment due to privacy concerns; hence, the test’s effectiveness cannot be known. Whereas in the crowd-sourcing systems, the expertise level of self-listed-agents (workers) is unknown; therefore, the quality of their work cannot be known. In these prediction problems, we can observe prediction from test/worker, but we cannot ascertain their reliability due to the absence of ground truth.

In many of the real-world situations like those found in medical diagnosis, airport security, and manufacturing, a set of tests or classifiers is used to monitor patients, people, and products. Tests have cost with the more informative ones resulting in higher monetary costs and higher latency. Thus, they are often organized as a cascade
(
Chen et al. 2012
;
Trapeznikov and Saligrama 2013
)
, so that a new input is first probed by an inexpensive test then more expensive one. We refer to such cascaded systems as
Unsupervised Sequential Selection
(USS) problem
1
1

1

Note that the unsupervised sequential selection problem is referred to as the unsupervised sensor selection problem in the prior work
(
Hanawal et al. 2017
)
.
, where an arm represents a test/ worker. A learner’s goal in the USS problem is to select the most cost-effective arm so that the overall system maintains high accuracy at low average costs.

Clearly, without the knowledge of the ground-truth, one cannot find the optimal arm as their losses cannot be computed. In the USS setup, the structure of the problem needs to be exploited, and it is shown that under certain conditions, namely strong dominance (
\mathrm{SD}
) and weak dominance (
\mathrm{WD}
), learning is possible. The
\mathrm{SD}
property requires the loss of an arm to stochastically dominate the losses of other arms with lower costs in the cascade. Specifically, it assumes that if an arm’s prediction is correct, then all the arms that follow this arm in the cascade also have correct predictions.

Under the
\mathrm{SD}
property,
Hanawal et al. 2017
established that USS problem is equivalent to a multi-armed bandit with side observations and exploit the equivalence to give an algorithm with sub-linear regret. The
\mathrm{SD}
property is quite strong and posits that disagreement probability of the predictions of two arms is equal to the difference in error rates. This property implies that we can measure losses by measuring disagreement probabilities leading to a direct multi-armed bandit (MAB) reduction and analysis.

The
\mathrm{WD}
property relaxes strict stochastic ordering on predictions and allows errors on some instances from better arms. It is argued that the set of instances satisfying the
\mathrm{WD}
property is maximally learnable, and any further relaxation of this property renders the problems unlearnable. The reduction techniques used under
\mathrm{SD}
property does not apply/extend to
\mathrm{WD}
property. For this case, a heuristic algorithm without any performance guarantee is given in
Hanawal et al. 2017
. Our work bridges this gap. Specifically, we propose algorithms that use the notion of weak dominance
(
Hanawal et al. 2017
)
that helps to find optimal arm using observed disagreements between arms. We then validate the performance of these algorithms on several problem instances derived from synthetic and real datasets. Our contributions of this chapter is summarized as follows:

•

Hanawal et al. 2017
assume that arms are ordered, i.e., their accuracy improve with their index, and used this fact in their algorithms. We relax this assumption in
Section

2.3.2
where the arms can have an arbitrary order. For this setup, we show that the same
\mathrm{WD}
property determines the learnability. Hence, we demonstrate that the requirement of arms being ordered by error rate in the USS problem is unnecessary and as long as the
\mathrm{WD}
property is satisfied the USS problem is learnable.

•

We develop an UCB based algorithm named
USS-UCB
that has sub-linear regret under
\mathrm{WD}
property. We characterize regret in terms of how ‘well’ the problem instances satisfy the
\mathrm{WD}
property and then provide a bound that holds uniformly for all
\mathrm{WD}
instances.

•

We give problem independent bounds on the regret of
USS-UCB
. We show that it is of order
T^{2/3}
under
\mathrm{WD}
property and improves to
T^{1/2}
under
\mathrm{SD}
property. We establish that the bounds are optimal using results from partial monitoring in
Section

2.4
.

•

We develop a Thompson Sampling based algorithm named
USS-TS
for the USS problem.
USS-TS
uses a one-sided test to find the optimal arm, whereas the
USS-UCB
uses a two-sided test to identify the optimal arm. The one-sided test leads to a simpler algorithm.

•

In
Section

2.5
, we characterize the regret of
USS-TS
in terms of how well the problem instance satisfies the
\mathrm{WD}
property and show that it has sub-linear regret under
\mathrm{WD}
property. We also give problem independent regret bound and establish that the regret bounds are near-optimal.

•

We demonstrate empirical performance of our algorithms on both synthetic and real datasets in
Section

2.6
. The experimental results show that regret of
USS-UCB
and
USS-TS
is always lower than the heuristic algorithm in
Hanawal et al. 2017
. Further, our experimental results show that regret of
USS-TS
is always lower than
USS-UCB
.

2.1.1
Related Work

Several works consider the problem similar to the USS setup in either batch, or online settings
(
Trapeznikov and Saligrama 2013
;
Seldin et al. 2014
)
.
However, they all require that the label of each data point is available or the reward is obtained for each action.

Zolghadr et al. 2013
considers that the labels are available on payment.
Greiner et al. 2002
and
Póczos et al. 2009
consider costs associated with tests. However, they assume that loss/reward associated with the players’ action is revealed. In contrast, in our setting, the labels are not revealed at any point and are thus completely unsupervised, and the cost in our setup is related to sensing cost and not that of acquiring a label.

Platanios et al. 2014
;
Platanios et al. 2016
;
Platanios et al. 2017
consider the problem of estimating accuracies of the multiple binary classifiers with unlabeled data. Most of these works make strong assumptions such as independence given the labels, knowledge of the true distribution of the labels.

Platanios et al. 2014
proposed logistic regression based methods using the classifiers’ agreement rates over unlabeled data,
Platanios et al. 2016
extend this work to use graphical models, and
Platanios et al. 2017
proposes method using probabilistic logic. Further,
Platanios et al. 2017
also use weighted majority vote for label prediction. All this is in the batch setting and differs from our online setup.

In the crowd-sourcing problems, various methods have been proposed to estimate unknown skill-level of crowd-workers from the noisy labels they provide (
Bonald and Combes 2017
;
Kleindessner and Awasthi 2018
). These methods assume that all workers are having the same cost and aggregate the predictions on a given dataset for estimating the accuracy of each worker. Unlike our problem setup, these methods are not online.

Our work is closely related to the stochastic partial monitoring setting

(
Cesa-Bianchi et al. 2006
;
Bartók and
Szepesvári 2012a
;
Bartók et al. 2014
;
Wu et al. 2015
)
, where the feedback from actions is indirectly tied to the rewards. In our setting, we exploit the problem structure to learn an optimal arm without explicitly knowing the loss associated with each action.

2.2
Problem Setting

We consider a stochastic
K
-armed bandits problem. The set of arms is denoted by
[K]
where
[K]\doteq\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
. In round
t
, the environment generates a task
x_{t}
independently and corresponding
(K+1)
-dimensional binary vector
\left(Y_{t},\{Y_{t}^{i}\}_{i\in[K]}\right)
. The variable
Y_{t}
denotes the best binary feedback for round
t
, which is hidden from the learner. The vector
\left(\{Y_{t}^{i}\}_{i\in[K]}\right)\in\{0,1\}^{K}
represents observed feedback at time
t
, where
Y_{t}^{i}
denote the feedback
2
2

2

In the USS setup, an arm
i
could represent a classifier. After using the first
i
classifiers, the final label can be a function of labels predicted by the first
i
classifiers,
i\in[K]
.
observed after playing arm
i
. We denote the cost for using arm
i\in[K]
as
c_{i}\geq 0
that is known to learner and the same for all rounds.
Fig.

2.1
depicts the USS setup.

Figure 2.1
:
Cascaded Unsupervised Sequential Selection (USS) setup. In round
t
,
x_{t}
represents the task,
Y_{t}
is the hidden state of the instance, and
Y_{t}^{1},Y_{t}^{2}\ldots Y_{t}^{K}
are feedback observed from the arms.
\gamma_{i}
denotes error rate of the arm
i
and
c_{i}
denotes the cost of using the arm
i
.

In the USS setup, the arms are assumed to be ordered and form a cascade. When the learner selects an arm
i\in[K]
, the feedback from all arms till arm
i
in the cascade is observed. The expected loss of playing the arm
i
is denoted as
\gamma_{i}\doteq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y\right\}}\right]=\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y\right\}
, where
\mathds{1}_{\left\{A\right\}}
denotes indicator of event
A
. The
expected total cost
incurred by playing arm
i
is defined as
\gamma_{i}+\lambda_{i}C_{i}
, where
C_{i}\doteq c_{1}+\ldots+c_{i}
and
\lambda_{i}
is a trade-off parameter that normalizes the loss and the incurred cost of playing arm
i
. The interaction between the environment and a learner is given in Algorithm
1
.

Algorithm 1

Learning with USS instance
(\boldsymbol{Q},\boldsymbol{c})

For each round
t
:

1.

Environment
chooses a vector
(Y_{t},\{Y_{t}^{i}\}_{i\in[K]})\sim\boldsymbol{Q}
.

2.

Learner
selects an arm
I_{t}\in[K]
to stop in cascade.

3.

Feedback and Loss:
The learner observes feedback
(Y_{t}^{1},Y_{t}^{2},\ldots,Y_{t}^{I_{t}})
and incurs a total loss
\mathds{1}_{\left\{Y^{I_{t}}\neq Y_{t}\right\}}+\lambda_{I_{t}}C_{I_{t}}
.

Since the best binary feedback are hidden from the learner, the expected loss of an arm cannot be inferred from the observed feedback. We thus have a version of the stochastic partial monitoring problem, and we refer to it as
u
nsupervised
s
equential
s
election (USS) problem. Let
\boldsymbol{Q}
be the unknown joint distribution of
(Y,Y^{1},Y^{2}\ldots,Y^{K})
. Henceforth we identify an USS instance as
P\doteq(\boldsymbol{Q},\boldsymbol{c})
where
\boldsymbol{c}\doteq(c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{K})
is the known cost vector of arms. We denote the collection of all USS instances as
\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
. For instance
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
, the optimal arm is given by

{i^{\star}}\in\max\left\{\arg\!\min_{i\in[K]}\left(\gamma_{i}+\lambda_{i}C_{i}\right)\right\}

(2.1)

where the ‘max’ operator selects the arm with the largest index among the minimizers. The choice of
{i^{\star}}
in
Eq.

2.1
is risk-averse as we prefer the arm with the lowest error among the optimal arms.

The learner’s goal is to learn a policy that finds an arm such that the cumulative expected loss is minimized. Specifically, for
T
rounds, we measure the performance of a policy that selects an arm
I_{t}
in round
t
in terms of regret given by

{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\gamma_{I_{t}}+\lambda_{I_{t}}C_{I_{t}}-\left(\gamma_{i^{\star}}+\lambda_{i^{\star}}C_{{i^{\star}}}\right)\right).

(2.2)

A good policy should have sub-linear regret, i.e.,

\lim\limits_{T\rightarrow\infty}{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}/T=0
. The sub-linear regret implies that the learner collects almost as much reward in expectation in the long run as an oracle that knew the optimal arm from the first round.
We say that a problem instance
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
is learnable if there exists a policy with sub-linear regret.

2.3
Conditions for Learning Optimal Arm

The purpose of this section is to introduce the notions of strong and weak dominance from the work of
Hanawal et al. 2017
. While
Hanawal et al. 2017
studied learning under strong dominance, we will focus on weak dominance in this chapter. We also modify the definition of weak dominance of
Hanawal et al. 2017
to correct an oversight of them. Next, we define the strong and weak dominance property of the USS problem instance that makes the learning of the optimal arm possible.

Definition 1

(Strong Dominance
(\mathrm{SD})

(
Hanawal et al. 2017
)
)
.

A problem instance is said to satisfy
\mathrm{SD}
property if

Y^{i}=Y\mbox{ for some }i\in[K]\implies Y^{j}=Y,~~\forall j>i.

We represent the set of all instances in
\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
that satisfy
\mathrm{SD}
property by
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}
.

The
\mathrm{SD}
property implies that if the feedback of an arm is same as the true reward, then the feedback of all the arms in the subsequent stages of the cascade is also same as the true reward.
Hanawal et al. 2017
show that the set of all instances satisfying
\mathrm{SD}
property is learnable by mapping such instances to stochastic multi-armed bandits problem with side information
(
Wu et al. 2015
)
. A weaker version of the
\mathrm{SD}
property is defined as follows:

Definition 2

(Weak Dominance
(\mathrm{WD})

(
Hanawal et al. 2017
)
)
.

Let
i^{\star}
denote the optimal arm. Then an instance
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
is said to satisfy weak dominance property if

\rho\doteq\min_{j>i^{\star}}\frac{C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}}{\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{j}\neq Y^{i^{\star}}\right\}}>1.

(2.3)

We denote the set of all instances in
\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
that satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
property by
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
.

The
\mathrm{WD}
property implies that for all sub-optimal arms that come after the optimal arm in the cascade, the cost difference is more than their disagreement probability.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}=\left\{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}:P\mbox{ satisfies $\mathrm{WD}$ condition}\right\}
denote the set of instances satisfying the
\mathrm{WD}
property.
Hanawal et al. 2017
claimed that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
is learnable. However, their definition allowed
\rho\geq 1
. As it turns out, permitting
\rho=1
can prevent
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
from being learnable:

Proposition 1
.

The set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}^{\prime}=\{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}\,:\,\rho\geq 1\}
is not learnable.

Proof.

Let
C_{2}-C_{1}=1/4
. Theorem 19 of
Hanawal et al. 2017
constructs instances
P,P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}^{\prime}
such that the optimal decision for
P
is arm
1
, for
P^{\prime}
is arm
2
. The sub-optimality gap on instance
P
is
1/4
, while on instance
P^{\prime}
is
\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
is a tunable parameter. At the same time
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{1}\neq Y^{2}\right\}=1/4
in
P
and
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{1}\neq Y^{2}\right\}=1/4+\varepsilon
in
P^{\prime}
. Theorem 17 of
Hanawal et al. 2017
implies that a sound algorithm must check
1/4=C_{2}-C_{1}\geq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{1}\neq Y^{2}\right\}
. However, no finite amount of data is sufficient to decide this: In particular, one can show that if an algorithm on
P
achieves sub-linear regret, then it must suffer linear regret on
P^{\prime}
for
\varepsilon>0
small enough. Hence, all algorithms will suffer linear regret on some instance in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}^{\prime}
.
∎

The following theorem is obtained directly from Theorem
14
and Theorem
19
in
Hanawal et al. 2017
after excluding the case
\rho=1
in their proofs.

Theorem 1
.

The set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
is a maximal learnable set.

Since the set of problems satisfying the
\mathrm{WD}
property is maximally learnable, any relaxation of
\mathrm{WD}
property makes the problem unlearnable. In the following equation, we use an alternative characterization of the
\mathrm{WD}
property, given as

\xi\doteq\min_{j>{i^{\star}}}\left\{C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}-\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\right\}>0.

(2.4)

After multiplying LHS of
Eq.

2.3
by
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{j}\neq Y^{i^{\star}}\right\}
and then subtracting both sides by
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{j}\neq Y^{i^{\star}}\right\}
, we get LHS of
Eq.

2.4
. Observe that
\rho>1
if and only if
\xi>0
. The larger the value of
\xi
, ‘stronger’ is the
\mathrm{WD}
property, and easier to identify an optimal arm. We later characterize the regret upper bound of our algorithms in terms of
\xi
.

2.3.1
Optimal Arm Selection

Without loss of generality, we set
\lambda_{i}=1
for all
i\in[K]
as their value can be absorbed into the costs. Since
{i^{\star}}=\max\big\{\arg\min\limits_{i\in[K]}\left(\gamma_{i}+C_{i}\right)\big\}
, it must satisfy following equation:

\displaystyle\forall j<{i^{\star}}\,:\,C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}\leq\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i^{\star}}\,,

(2.5a)

\displaystyle\forall j>{i^{\star}}\,:\,C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}>\gamma_{i^{\star}}-\gamma_{j}\,.

(2.5b)

As the loss of an arm is not observed, the above equations can not lead to a sound arm selection criteria. We thus have to relate the unobservable quantities in terms of the quantities that can be observed. In our setup, we can compare the feedback of two arms, which can be used to estimate their disagreement probability. For notation convenience, we define
p_{ij}\doteq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}
. The value of
p_{ij}
can be estimated as it is observable. We use the following result from
Hanawal et al. 2017
that relates the differences in the unobserved error rates in terms of their observable disagreement probability.

Proposition 2

(Proposition 3 in
Hanawal et al. 2017
)
.

For any two arms
i
and
j
,
\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}=p_{ij}-2\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}=Y,Y^{j}\neq Y\right\}
.

Now, using
2
, we can replace
Eq.

2.5a
by

\forall j<{i^{\star}}\,:\,C_{{i^{\star}}}-C_{j}\leq p_{ji^{\star}},

(2.6)

which only has observable quantities. For
j>{i^{\star}}
, we can replace
Eq.

2.5b
by using the
\mathrm{WD}
property as follows:

\forall j>{i^{\star}}\,:\,C_{j}-C_{{i^{\star}}}>p_{i^{\star}j}.

(2.7)

Using
Eq.

2.6
and
Eq.

2.7
, we define the selection criteria based on the following sets:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{l}=\Big\{i:\forall j<i,C_{i}-C_{j}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\Big\}\cup\{1\},

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{h}=\Big\{i:\forall j>i,C_{j}-C_{i}>\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\Big\}\cup\{K\}.

(2.9)

Our next result gives the optimal arm for a problem instance.

Lemma 1
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
. Let
\mathcal{B}\doteq\mathcal{B}^{l}\cap\mathcal{B}^{h}
. Then
\mathcal{B}
contains the optimal arm.

The detailed proof of
1
and all other missing proofs of this chapter appear in
Section

2.7
.

Remark 1
.

The
\mathrm{WD}
property holds trivially for the problem instances that satisfy
\mathrm{SD}
property as the difference of mean losses is the same as the disagreement probability between two arms due to
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t},Y_{t}^{j}\neq Y_{t}\right\}=0
for
j>i
. Also, by definition, the
\mathrm{WD}
property holds for all problem instances where the last arm of the cascade is an optimal arm.

2.3.2
Unknown Ordering of Arms

The error rates of arms are unknown in the USS setup and cannot be estimated due to unavailability of ground-truth. Thus, it may happen that we do not know whether error rate of arms in the cascade is decreasing or not. In this section, we remove the requirement that arms are arranged in the decreasing order of their error rates and allow them to be arranged in an arbitrary order that is unknown. We denote the set of USS instances with unknown ordering of arms by their error-rates as
\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}^{\prime}
. The rest of the setup remains same. We show that even with this relaxation,
\mathrm{WD}
property defined earlier, continues to characterize the learnability of the USS problem. We begin with the following observation.

Lemma 2
.

Let
i^{\star}
be an optimal arm. Then, error rate of any arm
j<i^{\star}
is higher than that of
i^{\star}
.

Proof.

We have
\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i^{\star}}\geq C_{i^{\star}}-C_{i}
for all
j\in[K]
. For
j<i^{\star}
,
C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}\geq 0
as costs are increasing with arms. Hence
\gamma_{j}\geq\gamma_{i^{\star}}
.
∎

The following corollary directly follows from
2
.

Corollary 1
.

For any
i,j\in[K]
,
\max\{0,\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}
.

The following two propositions provide the conditions on arm costs that allows comparison of their total costs based on disagreement probabilities.

Proposition 3
.

Let
i<j
. Assume

C_{j}-C_{i}\notin\left(\max\{0,\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}\},\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\right].

(2.10)

Then,
C_{j}-C_{i}>\max\{0,\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}\}
iff
C_{j}-C_{i}>\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{j}\neq Y^{i}\right\}
.

Proposition 4
.

Let
i>j
. Assume

C_{i}-C_{j}\notin\left(\max\{0,\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}\},\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\right].

(2.11)

Then,
C_{i}-C_{j}\leq\max\{0,\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}\}
iff
C_{j}-C_{i}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}
.

From
2
, for any
j<i^{*}
we have
\max\{0,\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i^{\star}}\}=\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i^{\star}}
.
3
and
4
then suggests that the value of
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}
are sufficient to select the optimal arm if the arms costs satisfy (
Eq.

2.10
) for all
j>i^{\star}
and (
Eq.

2.11
) for all
j<i^{\star}
. Since the values of
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}
can be estimated for all
i,j\in[K],
we can establish the following result.

Proposition 5
.

Let
i^{\star}
be an optimal arm. Any problem instance
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}^{\prime}
is learnable if

\forall\;j>i^{\star}\;\;C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}\notin\left(\max\{0,\gamma_{i^{\star}}-\gamma_{j}\},\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\right].

Notice that for
j>i^{\star}
,
C_{j}-C_{i}^{\star}\geq 0
and
C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}\geq\gamma_{i^{\star}}-\gamma_{j}
. Hence, the learnability condition reduces to
\forall\;j>i^{\star},C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}>\Pr\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\}
, i.e., same as the WD condition. Hence, we have the following result.

Theorem 2
.

The set
\{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}^{\prime}:\rho(\theta)>1\}
is learnable.

Proof.

Using equation (
2.5b
) and the fact that the costs of using an arm is increasing in the cascade, we have
C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}>\max\{0,\gamma_{i^{\star}}-\gamma_{j}\}
. The max function is used because arbitrary ordering of arms by error-rates can lead to
\gamma_{i^{\star}}-\gamma_{j}
being negative whereas
C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}
is a non-negative quantity. Therefore,

\displaystyle\forall j>i^{\star},\;C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}>\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}

(2.12)

It implies that
\mathrm{WD}
property holds for problem instance even with the arbitrary ordering of arms by their error-rates. This relationship ensures learnability of such USS problems.
∎

2.4
Upper Confidence Bound based Algorithm for USS problem

In bandit problems, the upper confidence bound (UCB)
(
Auer et al. 2002
;
Garivier and Cappé 2011
)
is highly effective for dealing with the trade-off between exploration and exploitation. Using UCB idea, we develop an algorithm, named
USS-UCB
, that utilizes the sets (
2.8
) and (
2.9
) and looks for an index that belongs to both. Since the value of disagreement probabilities,
\left(p_{ij}\doteq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\right)
are unknown (but fixed and observable), they are replaced by their optimistic empirical estimates at round
t
, denoted by
\hat{p}_{ij}(t)+\Psi_{ij}(t)
, where
\hat{p}_{ij}(t)
is empirical estimate of
p_{ij}
and
\Psi_{ij}(t)
is the confidence term associated with
\hat{p}_{ij}(t)
as in UCB algorithm. The new sets for selection criteria are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{\hat{B}}_{t}^{l}=\{i:\forall j<i,C_{i}-C_{j}\leq\hat{p}_{ji}(t)+\Psi_{ji}(t)\}\cup\{1\},

(2.13a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{\hat{B}}_{t}^{h}=\{i:\forall j>i;C_{j}-C_{i}>\hat{p}_{ij}(t)+\Psi_{ij}(t)\}\cup\{K\}.

(2.13b)

From the definition, it is easy to verify that
\hat{p}_{ij}(t)=\hat{p}_{ji}(t)
and
\Psi_{ij}(t)=\Psi_{ji}(t)
for any
(i,j)
pair. Therefore, it is enough for algorithm to only keep track of
\hat{p}_{ij}(t)
and
\Psi_{ji}(t)
for
i<j
.

Remark 2
.

It might be tempting to use lower confidence, i.e.,
\hat{p}_{ij}(t)-\Psi_{ij}(t)
term instead of the upper confidence term in (
2.13b
). However, such a change can make the algorithm converge to a sub-optimal arm. A detailed discussion is given in
Section

2.7
.

2.4.1
Algorithm:
USS-UCB

The pseudo code of Algorithm is given in
USS-UCB
and it works as follows. It takes
\alpha
as an input that trades-off between exploration and exploitation. In the first round, it selects arm
K
and initializes the value of number of comparisons and counter of disagreements for each pair
(i,j),i<j
, denoted
\mathcal{N}_{ij}(1)
and
\mathcal{D}_{ij}(1)
, respectively. In each subsequent round, the algorithm computes estimate for the disagreement probability
(\hat{p}_{ij}(t))
and the associated confidence
(\Psi_{ij}(t))
. Then the values of
\hat{p}_{ij}(t)
and
\Psi_{ij}(t)
are used for computing sets
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{l}
and
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{h}
which are then used to select the arm. Specifically, the algorithm selects a arm
I_{t}
that satisfies the conditions given in (
2.13a
) and (
2.13b
).

USS-UCB

UCB based Algorithm for
USS
under
WD
property

1:

Input:

\alpha>0.5

2:

Select arm
I_{1}=K
and observe
Y^{1}_{1},\dots,Y^{I_{1}}_{1}

3:

Set
\mathcal{D}_{ij}(1)\leftarrow\mathds{1}_{\left\{Y^{i}_{1}\neq Y^{j}_{1}\right\}},\;\mathcal{N}_{ij}(1)\leftarrow 1\;\;\forall i<j\leq I_{1}

4:

for

t=2,3,...

do

5:

\hat{p}_{ij}(t)\leftarrow\frac{\mathcal{D}_{ij}(t-1)}{\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t-1)}\;\;\forall i<j\leq K

6:

\Psi_{ij}(t)\leftarrow\sqrt{\frac{\alpha\log f(t)}{\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t-1)}}\;\;\forall i<j\leq K

7:

Compute
\mathcal{\hat{B}}_{t}^{l}
and
\mathcal{\hat{B}}_{t}^{h}
as given in (
2.13a
) and (
2.13b
)

8:

\mathcal{\hat{B}}_{t}:=\mathcal{\hat{B}}_{t}^{l}\cap\mathcal{\hat{B}}_{t}^{h}

9:

I_{t}\leftarrow\min\big\{\mathcal{\hat{B}}_{t}\cup\{K\}\big\}

10:

Select arm
I_{t}
and observe
Y^{1}_{t},\dots,Y^{I_{t}}_{t}

11:

\mathcal{D}_{ij}(t)\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{ij}(t-1)+\mathds{1}_{\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}\right\}}\;\;\forall i<j\leq I_{t}

12:

\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t)\leftarrow\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t-1)+1\;\;\forall i<j\leq I_{t}

13:

end

for

Since initial estimates for
p_{ij}
are not good enough,
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{t}
can be empty. In such a case, the algorithm selects the arm
K
. After selection of arm
I_{t}
,
Y^{j}_{t},j\in[I_{t}]
are observed which are then used to update the
\mathcal{D}_{ij}(t)
and
\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t)
in the algorithm.

2.4.2
Regret Analysis

The following notations and definition are useful in subsequent proofs. For the optimal arm
i^{\star}
and each
j\in[K]
, let

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}:=C_{j}+\gamma_{j}-(C_{i^{\star}}+\gamma_{i^{\star}}),

(2.14)

\displaystyle\kappa_{j}:=

\displaystyle p_{i^{\star}j}-(\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i^{\star}}),\;\;\text{ if }j<i^{\star}

(2.15a)

\displaystyle\kappa_{j}:=

\displaystyle p_{i^{\star}j}-(\gamma_{i^{\star}}-\gamma_{j}),\;\;\text{ if }j>i^{\star}

(2.15b)

\displaystyle\xi_{j}:=

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}+\kappa_{j},\;\;\text{ if }j<i^{\star}

(2.16a)

\displaystyle\xi_{j}:=

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}-\kappa_{j},\;\;\text{ if }j>i^{\star}

(2.16b)

Notice that the values of
\kappa_{j}
and
\xi_{j}
for all
j\in[K]
are positive under the
\mathrm{WD}
property. Their relations are depicted in Figure
2.2
.

Figure 2.2
:
Relationship among
\Delta_{j},\;\kappa_{j}
and
\xi_{j}
for a problem instance that satisfies WD property.

Let
N_{j}(T)
denote the number of times arm
j
is selected until round
T
. The following proposition gives the mean number of times a sub-optimal arm is selected.

Proposition 6
.

Let
f(t)
be a positive valued increasing function such that
C=\lim\limits_{T\rightarrow\infty}\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\dfrac{1}{f(t)^{2\alpha}}<\infty
in
USS-UCB
. For any
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
, the mean number of times a arm
j\neq i^{\star}
is selected, is bounded as follows:

•

for any
j<i^{\star}

\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\leq\dfrac{C}{2\xi_{j}^{2}},

•

and for any
j>i^{\star}

\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\leq 1+\frac{1}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\left(\alpha\log f(T)+\sqrt{\frac{\pi\alpha\log f(T)}{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\right).

Notice that the mean number of times a arm
j<i^{\star}
is selected, is finite. The regret bounds follows by noting that
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]=\sum_{j<i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\Delta_{j}+\sum_{j>i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]

\Delta_{j}
. Formally, we have the following regret bound.

Theorem 3
.

Let
f(t)
be set as in
6
. Then, for any
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
, the expected regret of
USS-UCB
in
T
rounds is bounded as below:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]\leq\sum\limits_{j<i^{\star}}\dfrac{\Delta_{j}C}{2\xi_{j}^{2}}+\sum\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\Delta_{j}\Bigg[1\;+\frac{1}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\Bigg(\alpha\log f(T)+\sqrt{\frac{\pi\alpha\log f(T)}{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\Bigg)\Bigg].

Proof.

Let
I_{t}
is the arm selected by algorithm at round
t
. Then expected regret
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]
of
USS-UCB
for
T
rounds is:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}r_{t}\hskip-1.13809pt\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\left(C_{I_{j}}+\gamma_{I_{t}}-\left(C_{i^{\star}}+\gamma_{i^{\star}}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{j\neq i^{\star}}\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}}\Delta_{j}\right]=\sum\limits_{j\neq i^{\star}}\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\Delta_{j}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j<i^{\star}}\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}\Delta_{j}+\sum\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}\Delta_{j}.

Since
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}{T}\right]
, using
6
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]\leq\sum\limits_{j<i^{\star}}\dfrac{\Delta_{j}C}{2\xi_{j}^{2}}+\sum\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\Delta_{j}\Bigg(1\;+\frac{1}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\Bigg(\alpha\log f(T)+\sqrt{\frac{\pi\alpha\log f(T)}{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\Bigg)\Bigg).

∎

Corollary 2
.

Let
\alpha=1
and
f(t)=t
in
3
. Then, expected regret of
USS-UCB
for any
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
in
T
rounds is of
O\left(\sum\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\frac{\Delta_{j}\log T}{\xi^{2}}\right)
.

Proof.

The regret contribution of arms whose index is smaller than the optimal arm is constant. With this fact and
\xi=\min\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\xi_{j}
, the proof follows from
3
with
\alpha=1
and
f(t)=t
.
∎

Remark 3
.

The function
f(t)
and
\alpha
control the exploration in
USS-UCB
. The larger the value, the more will be the exploration. The constraint given in
6
ensures that there is no linear term in the regret given in
3
due to inefficient exploration.

Corollary 3
.

Let technical conditions stated in
2
hold. Then expected regret of
USS-UCB
for any
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}
in
T
rounds is of
O\left(\sum\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\frac{\log T}{\xi}\right)
.

Proof.

Since
|\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i^{\star}}|=p_{i^{\star}j}
for
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}
,
\kappa_{j}=0,\forall j\in[K]\Rightarrow\xi_{j}=\Delta_{j}
. Rest follows from Corollary
2
.
∎

We next present problem independent bounds on the expected regret of
USS-UCB
.

Theorem 4
.

Let
f(t)
be set as in
6
. The expected regret of
USS-UCB
in
T
rounds

•

for any instance in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
is bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]\leq 3\left(3\alpha K\log f(T)\right)^{1/3}T^{2/3}.

•

for any instance in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}
is bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]\leq 4\left(\alpha KT\log f(T)\right)^{1/2}.

Corollary 4
.

Let technical conditions stated in
2
hold. The expected regret of
USS-UCB
on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}
is
\tilde{O}(T^{1/2})
and on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
it is
\tilde{O}(T^{2/3})
, where
\tilde{O}
hides logarithmic terms.

The proof of Theorem
4
can be found in
Section

2.7
. We note that the above uniform bounds do not contradict Theorem
19
in
Hanawal et al. 2017
which claimed non-existence of uniform bounds. The
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
condition considered in
Hanawal et al. 2017
incorrectly includes the class of instances satisfying
\rho=1
which renders
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
not learnable, whereas in our definition of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
these instances are excluded and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
is learnable.

Discussion on optimality of
USS-UCB
:

Any partial monitoring problem can be classified as an ‘easy’, ‘hard’ or ‘hopeless’ problem if it has expected regret bounds of the order
\Theta(T^{1/2}),\Theta(T^{2/3})
or
\Theta(T)
, respectively, and there exists no other class in between
Bartók et al. 2014
. The class
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}
is regret equivalent to a stochastic multi-armed bandit with side observations
Hanawal et al. 2017
, for which regret scales as
\Theta(T^{1/2})
, hence
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}
resides in the easy class and our bound on it is optimal. Since
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}\supsetneq\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
is not easy, and also
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
is learnable, it cannot be hopeless. Therefore, the class
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
is hard. We thus conclude that the regret bound of
USS-UCB
is near-optimal in
T
. However, optimality concerning other leading constants (in terms of
K
) is to be explored further.

2.5
Thompson Sampling based Algorithm for USS problem

Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) based methods are useful for dealing with the trade-off between exploration and exploitation in bandit problems
(
Auer et al. 2002
;
Garivier and Cappé 2011
)
. UCB has been widely used for solving various sequential decision-making problems. On the other hand, Thompson Sampling (TS)
(
Agrawal and Goyal 2012
;
Kaufmann et al. 2012
;
Agrawal and Goyal 2013
)
is an online algorithm based on Bayesian updates. TS selects an arm to play according to its probability of being the best arm, and it is shown that TS is empirically superior then UCB based algorithms for various MAB problems
(
Chapelle and Li 2011
)
. TS also achieves lower bound for MAB when rewards of arms have Bernoulli distribution, as shown by
Kaufmann et al. 2012
.

In this section, we propose a TS based algorithm for the USS problem and show that it is a near-optimal algorithm. Using
Eq.

2.6
and
Eq.

2.7
, our next result gives the optimal arm in the USS problem that only uses one-sided test (
Eq.

2.7
) for the arm’s selection.

Lemma 3
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
and
\mathcal{B}=\left\{i:\forall j>i,C_{j}-C_{i}>p_{ij}\right\}\cup\{K\}
. Then the arm
I_{t}=\min(\mathcal{B})
is the optimal arm for the problem instance
P
.

Proof.

Let
{i^{\star}}
be an optimal arm for the problem instance
P
. Since
p_{i^{\star}j}\doteq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}
, we have
\forall j<{i^{\star}}:\,C_{{i^{\star}}}-C_{j}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\implies C_{{i^{\star}}}-C_{j}\ngtr\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\implies j\notin\mathcal{B},\forall j<{i^{\star}}
. If any sub-optimal arm
h\in\mathcal{B}
then the index of arm
h
must be larger than the index of optimal arm
{i^{\star}}
in the cascade. Hence the element of the set
\mathcal{B}
in round
t
is given as follows:

\mathcal{B}=\{{i^{\star}},h_{1},\ldots,h_{t},K\},

where
{i^{\star}}<h_{1}<\cdots<h_{t}<K
. By construction of set
\mathcal{B}
, the minimum indexed arm in set
\mathcal{B}
is the optimal arm.
∎

2.5.1
Algorithm:
USS-TS

We develop a Thompson Sampling based algorithm, named
USS-TS
, that uses
3
to select optimal arm. The algorithm works as follows: It sets the prior distribution of disagreement probability for each pair of arms as the Beta distribution, Beta
(1,1)
, which is the same as Uniform distribution on
[0,1]
. The variable
S_{ij}
represents the number of rounds when a disagreement is observed between arm
i
and
j
. Whereas, the variable
F_{ij}
represents the number of rounds when an agreement is observed. The variables
S_{ij}^{(t)}
and
F_{ij}^{(t)}
denote the values of
S_{ij}
and
F_{ij}
at the beginning of round
t
.

USS-TS

Thompson Sampling based Algorithm for Unsupervised Sequential Selection

1:

Set
\forall 1\leq i<j\leq K:\mathcal{S}_{ij}^{(1)}\leftarrow 1,\mathcal{F}_{ij}^{(1)}\leftarrow 1

2:

for

t=1,2,\ldots

do

3:

Set
i=1
and
I_{t}=0

4:

while

I_{t}=0

do

5:

Play arm
i

6:

\forall j\in[i+1,K]:
compute
\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(t)}\leftarrow\mbox{Beta}(\mathcal{S}_{ij}^{(t)},\mathcal{F}_{ij}^{(t)})

7:

If
\forall j\in[i+1,K]:C_{j}-C_{i}>\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(t)}
or
i=K
then set
I_{t}=i
else set
i=i+1

8:

end

while

9:

Select arm
I_{t}
and observe
Y_{t}^{1},Y_{t}^{2},\dots,Y_{t}^{I_{t}}

10:

\forall 1\leq i<j\leq I_{t}:
update
\mathcal{S}_{ij}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{ij}^{(t)}+\mathds{1}_{\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}\right\}},\mathcal{F}_{ij}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\mathcal{F}_{ij}^{(t)}+\mathds{1}_{\left\{Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t}^{j}\right\}}

11:

end

for

In round
t
, the learner plays the arm
i=1
and then observe its feedback. For each
(i,j)
pair, a sample
\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(t)}
is independently drawn from Beta
(S_{ij}^{(t)},F_{ij}^{(t)})
. Then algorithm checks whether the arm
i
is the best arm using
Eq.

2.7
with
\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(t)}
in place of
p_{ij}^{(t)}
. If the arm
i
is not the best, then the algorithm plays the next arm, and the same process is repeated. If the arm
i
is the best arm for the round
t
, then the algorithm stops at arm
I_{t}=i
in the round
t
.

After selecting arm
I_{t}
, the feedback from arms
1,\ldots,I_{t}
are observed, which is used to update the values of
S_{ij}^{(t+1)}
and
F_{ij}^{(t+1)}
. The same process is repeated in the subsequent rounds.

Remark 4
.

USS-TS
is adapted for the USS problem from the Thompson Sampling algorithm for stochastic multi-armed bandits. However, the feedback structure and the way arms are selected in the USS setup differ from that in the stochastic multi-armed bandits.

2.5.2
Regret Analysis

The following definitions and results are useful in subsequent proof arguments.

Definition 3

(Action Preference (
\succ_{t}
))
.

USS-TS
prefers the arm
i
over arm
j
in round
t
if:

\displaystyle i\succ_{t}j\doteq

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{ji}^{(t)}\geq C_{i}-C_{j}

if
j<i

(2.17a)

\displaystyle i\succ_{t}j\doteq

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(t)}<C_{j}-C_{i}

if
j>i

(2.17b)

Definition 4

(Transitivity Property)
.

If
i\succ_{t}j
and
j\succ_{t}k
then
i\succ_{t}k
.

Definition 5
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{t}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the history of selected arms and observations at the beginning of the time
t
and given as follows:

\mathcal{H}_{t}\doteq\left\{I_{s},\left\{Y_{s}^{i}\right\}_{i\leq I_{s}},s=1,\ldots,t-1\right\},

where
I_{s}
denotes the arm selected and set
\left\{Y_{s}^{i}\right\}_{i\leq I_{s}}
denotes the observations from arm
1
to
I_{s}
in the round
s
. Define
\mathcal{H}_{1}\doteq\{\}.

Fact 1

(Beta-Binomial equality, Fact 1 in
Agrawal and Goyal 2012
)
.

Let
F_{\alpha,\beta}^{beta}(y)
be the cumulative distribution function (cdf) of the beta distribution with integer parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
. Let
F_{n,p}^{B}(\cdot)
be the cdf of the binomial distribution with parameters
n
and
p
. Then,

F_{\alpha,\beta}^{beta}(y)=1-F_{\alpha+\beta-1,y}^{B}(\alpha-1).

Lemma 4

(Lemma 2 in
Agrawal and Goyal 2013
)
.

Let
n\geq 0
and
\hat{\mu}_{n}
be the empirical average of
n
samples from Bernoulli(
\mu
). Let
x<\mu
and
q_{n}(x)\doteq 1-F_{n\hat{\mu}_{n}+1,n(1-\hat{\mu}_{n})+1}^{beta}(x)
be the probability that the posterior sample from the Beta distribution with its parameter
n\hat{\mu}_{n}+1,n(1-\hat{\mu}_{n})+1
exceeds
x
. Then,

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{q_{n}(x)}-1\right]\leq\begin{cases}\frac{3}{\Delta(x)}&\mbox{if }n<8/\Delta(x)\\
\Theta\left(\exp^{-\frac{n\Delta(x)^{2}}{2}}+\frac{\exp^{-{nd(x,\mu)}}}{(n+1)\Delta(x)^{2}}+\frac{1}{\exp^{\frac{n\Delta(x)^{2}}{4}}-1}\right)&\mbox{if }n\geq 8/\Delta(x),\end{cases}

where
\Delta(x)\doteq\mu-x
and
d(x,\mu)\doteq x\log\left(\frac{x}{\mu}\right)+(1-x)\log\left(\frac{1-x}{1-\mu}\right)
.

Recall that
p_{i^{\star}j}
is the disagreement probability between arm
i^{\star}
and
j
and
\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}
is the sample of
p_{i^{\star}j}
using Beta distribution with the
t
samples.
Next, we bound the probability by which
USS-TS
selects the sub-optimal arm whose index is smaller than the optimal arm.

Definition 6
.

For any
j<i^{\star}
, define
q_{j,t}
as the probability

q_{j,t}\doteq\mathbb{P}\left\{\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\geq p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}.

Lemma 5
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
and satisfies the transitivity property. If
j<i^{\star}
then the probability by which
USS-TS
selects any sub-optimal arm
j
over the optimal arm is given by

\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\leq\frac{(1-q_{j,t})}{q_{j,t}}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}\geq i^{\star}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}.

Lemma 6
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
and satisfies the transitivity property. If
s
be the number of times the sub-optimal arm
j
is selected by
USS-TS
then, for any
j<i^{\star}
,

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}\right\}\leq\frac{24}{\xi_{j}^{2}}+\sum_{s\geq 8/\xi_{j}}\Theta\left(\exp^{-{s\xi_{j}^{2}}/{2}}+\frac{\exp^{-{sd(p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j},p_{i^{\star}j})}}}{(s+1)\xi_{j}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\exp^{{s\xi_{j}^{2}}/{4}}-1}\right).

Proof.

(sketch)

Using
5
and property of conditional expectations, we can have
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}\right\}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left\{jI_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\right]
. By using some simple algebraic manipulations on quantity
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\right]
with
4
, we can get the above stated upper bound.
∎

Our next result is useful to bound the probability by which
USS-TS
prefers the sub-optimal arms whose index is larger than the optimal arm.

Lemma 7
.

Let
\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}
be the empirical estimate of
p_{i^{\star}j}
and
j>{i^{\star}}
. Then, for any
x_{j}>p_{i^{\star}j}
and
y_{j}>x_{j}
,

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j}\right\}\leq\frac{\ln T}{d(x_{j},y_{j})}+1.

Lemma 8
.

For any
x_{j}>p_{i^{\star}j}
,

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>x_{j}\right\}\leq\frac{1}{d(x_{j},p_{i^{\star}j})}.

Proof.

(sketch)

This result is easily proved by using Chernoff-Hoeffding bound. See details in
Section

2.7
.
∎

Lemma 9
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
. For any
\varepsilon>0
and
j>i^{\star}
,

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}i^{\star},j>{i^{\star}}\right\}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\frac{\ln T}{d(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})}+O\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right).

Proof.

(sketch)

Let
p_{i^{\star}j}<x_{j}<y_{j}<p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j}
where
j>{i^{\star}}
. Then, it can be easily shown that
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}i^{\star},j>{i^{\star}}\right\}\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j}\right\}+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>x_{j}\right\}.
The upper bound on first term of right hand side quantity is given by
7
and the upper bound of the second term of right hand side quantity is given by
8
. Then, for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
with suitable values of
x_{j}
and
y_{j}
, we can get the above stated upper bound.
∎

Let
\Delta_{j}=C_{j}+\gamma_{j}-(C_{i^{\star}}+\gamma_{i^{\star}})
be the sub-optimality gap for arm
j
. Now we state the problem dependent regret upper bound of
USS-TS
.

Theorem 5

(Problem Dependent Bound)
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
and satisfies the transitivity property. If
\varepsilon>0
then, the expected regret of
USS-TS
in
T
rounds is bounded by

{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}\leq\sum_{j>i^{\star}}\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\ln T}{d(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})}\Delta_{j}+O\left(\frac{K-i^{\star}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right),

Proof.

(sketch)

Let
M_{j}(T)
is the number of times arm
j
is selected by
USS-TS
. Then, the regret of
USS-TS
is given by
{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}=\sum_{j\in[K]}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]\Delta_{j}=\sum_{j\in[K]}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}}\right]\Delta_{j}=\sum_{j\in[K]}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}\Delta_{j}
. We divide the regret into two parts and it can be re-written as
{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}\leq\sum_{j<i^{\star}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<i^{\star}\right\}\Delta_{j}+\sum_{j>i^{\star}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j>i^{\star}\right\}\Delta_{j}
. The first part of the regret is upper bounded by using
6
. For the second part, when arm
I_{t}>{i^{\star}}
is selected, then there exists at least one arm
k>{i^{\star}}
, which must be preferred over
{i^{\star}}
. Using transitivity property and a recursive argument, we can show that the selected arm is preferred over the optimal arm. Hence,
\sum_{j>i^{\star}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j>i^{\star}\right\}\Delta_{j}
can be upper bounded by
\sum_{j>i^{\star}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}

\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}{i^{\star}},j>i^{\star}\right\}\Delta_{j}
. We can upper bound
\sum_{j>i^{\star}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}{i^{\star}},j>i^{\star}\right\}\Delta_{j}
by using
9
to get the above stated regret upper bound for
USS-TS
.
∎

Next we present problem independent bounds on the regret of
USS-TS
.

Theorem 6

(Problem Independent Bound)
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
and satisfies the transitivity property. Then the expected regret of
USS-TS
in
T
rounds

•

for any instance in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}
is bounded as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}\leq O\left(\sqrt{KT\ln T}\right).

•

for any instance in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
is bounded as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}\leq O\left(\left(K\ln T\right)^{1/3}T^{2/3}\right).

Proof.

(sketch)

To get the above problem independent regret upper bound, we maximize the problem-dependent regret of
USS-TS
with respect to the value of
\xi_{j}
.
∎

Corollary 5
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
and satisfies the transitivity property. Then the expected regret of
USS-TS
on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}
is
\tilde{O}(T^{1/2})
and on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
it is
\tilde{O}(T^{2/3})
, where
\tilde{O}
hides
K
and the logarithmic terms that are having
T
in them.

Discussion on optimality of
USS-TS
:

As discussed in
Section

2.4
, the stochastic partial monitoring problems can be classified as an ‘easy,’ ‘hard,’ or ‘hopeless’ problem with expected regret bounds of the order
\Theta(T^{1/2}),\Theta(T^{2/3})
, or
\Theta(T)
, respectively. And there exists no other class of problems in between
(
Bartók et al. 2014
)
. After matching the regret bounds, we conclude that the regret bound of
USS-TS
is also near-optimal in
T
up to a logarithmic term.

2.6
Experiments

In this section, we evaluate the performance of proposed algorithms on different problem instances derived from synthetic and two ‘real’ datasets: PIMA Indians Diabetes
(
Kaggle 2016
)
and Heart Disease (Cleveland)
(
Detrano 1998
;
Dheeru and Karra Taniskidou 2017
)
. In our experiments, each arm is represented by a classifier that is arranged in order of their decreasing misclassification error, i.e., error-rate for each dataset. The cost of using a classifier is assigned based on its error-rate – smaller the error-rate higher the cost. The case where arms’ error-rate need not to decrease in the cascade is also considered.

2.6.1
Datasets

Synthetic Dataset:

We generate synthetic Bernoulli Symmetric Channel (BSC) dataset
(
Hanawal et al. 2017
)
as follows: The true binary feedback
Y_{t}
is generated from i.i.d. Bernoulli random variable with mean
0.7
. The problem instance used in the experiment has three arms. We fix feedback as true binary feedback for the first arm with probability
0.6
, second arm with probability
0.7
, and third arm with probability
0.8
. To ensure strong dominance, we impose the condition during data generation. When the feedback of arm
1
matches the true binary feedback, we introduce error up to 10% to the feedback of arm
2
and
3
. We use five problem instances of the BSC dataset by varying the cumulative cost of playing the arms as given in
Table

.

An arm
i
represents a classifier whose prediction is treated as the feedback of the arm

. The disagreement label for
(i,j)

and

. In Heart Disease dataset, each sample has

6

6
features, utilizes the thalach, exang and oldpeak features, and the 3rd classifier uses all the features. In PIMA Indians Diabetes dataset, each sample has

features as input. These features include patient profile. The 2nd classifier, in addition to the
6
features, utilizes the feature on the glucose tolerance test, and the 3rd classifier uses all the previous features and the feature that gives values of insulin test. The PIMA Indians Diabetes dataset has
768
samples, whereas the Heart Disease dataset has only
297
samples. As
10000
rounds are used in our experiments, we select a sample from the original dataset in a round-robin fashion and give it as input to the algorithm. The details about the different costs used in five problem instances of the real datasets are given in

Verifying
\mathrm{WD}
property:

The error-rate associated with each arm is known to us as given in
Table

2.1
and
Table

2.2
(but note that the error-rates are unknown to the algorithm); hence we can find an optimal arm for a given problem instance. After knowing optimal arm,
\mathrm{WD}
property is verified by using the disagreement probability estimates after
10000
rounds.

2.6.2
Experiment Results

We fix the time horizon to
10000
in all experiments and repeat each experiment
100
times. The average regret is presented with a
95
% confidence interval. The vertical line on each plot shows the confidence interval.

Comparison between different Algorithms:

In our first experiment, we compare the performance of
USS-UCB
(with value of
\alpha=0.5
) and
USS-TS
with Algorithm 2 of
Hanawal et al. 2017
with value of
\alpha=1.5
(as used in the paper) on Heart Disease and PIMA Indians Diabetes datasets. As expected,
USS-TS
outperforms other algorithms with large margins as shown in
Fig.

2.3(a)
(PIMA Indians Diabetes dataset) and
Fig.

2.3(b)
(Heart Disease dataset).

:
Comparing regret of
USS-UCB
and

with Algorithm 2
(
Hanawal et al. 2017
)
for real datasets (
Fig.

2.3(a)
and
Fig.

2.3(b)
).

Regret versus Time Horizon:
The
regret
of
USS-TS
versus
Time Horizon
plots for the different problem instances derived from BSC Dataset and two real datasets are shown in
Fig.

2.5(a)
and
Fig.

2.4
respectively. These plots verify that any instance that satisfies
\mathrm{WD}
property has sub-linear regret. Note that the regret of
USS-TS
is linear for the Instance
5
as it does not satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
property.

Heart Disease Dataset.

Figure 2.4
:
Regret of
USS-TS
for different problem instances derived from real datasets.

Learnability v/s
\mathrm{WD}
Property:
We experiment with different problem instances of the BSC dataset to know the relationship between regret of
USS-TS
and
\mathrm{WD}
property. We fixed an optimal arm and vary the cumulative cost of using arms in such a way that we pass from the case where
\mathrm{WD}
property does not hold (
\xi\leq 0
or
C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}\in(\gamma_{i^{\star}}-\gamma_{j},p_{i^{\star}j}]
for any
j>i^{\star}
where
\xi:=\min_{j>i^{\star}}\xi_{j}
) to the situation where
\mathrm{WD}
property holds
(\xi>0
).
When
\mathrm{WD}
property does not hold for any problem instance,
USS-TS
treats a sub-optimal arm as the optimal arm. In such problem instances, as
C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}
increases, the regret will also increase due to selection of sub-optimal arm by
USS-TS
until
\mathrm{WD}
property does not satisfy for that problem instance. When
\mathrm{WD}
property does not satisfy for a problem instance then
C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}\in(\gamma_{i^{\star}}-\gamma_{j},p_{i^{\star}j}]
holds in such cases, hence, it is easy to verify that
\xi
can not be smaller than
-\max(p_{i^{\star}j}-(\gamma_{i^{\star}}-\gamma_{j}))
.

(a)

Synthetic BSC Dataset.

(b)

Synthetic BSC Dataset.

Figure 2.5
:
Regret of
USS-TS
for different problem instances derived from synthetic BSC datasets. Regret behavior of
USS-TS
versus WD property for BSC Dataset is shown in
Fig.

2.5(b)
.

We consider the problem instances with the minimum possible value of
\xi
for which problem instance satisfies
\mathrm{WD}
property. Then we increase the value of
\xi
by increasing the cumulative cost of the arm. The regret versus
\xi
plots for BSC Dataset is shown in
Fig.

2.5(b)
. It can be observed that there is a transition at
\xi=0
. Through our experiments, we show that the stronger the
\mathrm{WD}
property (large value of
\xi
) for the problem instance, easier it is to identify the optimal arm and, hence the less regret is incurred by
USS-TS
.

Proof.

Let
i^{\star}
be an optimal sensor.
Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{l}:=\big\{i:i\in[2,K];\,\forall j<i\,\,\ni\,\,C_{i}-C_{j}\leq\mathbb{P}\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\}\big\}\cup\;\{1\}

(2.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{l}_{-1}:=\mathcal{B}^{l}\setminus\{1\}

(2.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{h}:=\big\{i:i\in[1,K-1];\,\forall j>i\,\,\ni\,\,C_{j}-C_{i}>\mathbb{P}\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\}\big\}\cup\;\{K\}

(2.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{h}_{-K}:=\mathcal{B}^{h}\setminus\{K\}

(2.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{B}^{l}\cap\mathcal{B}^{h}

(2.22)

Consider following three cases:

I.

1<i^{\star}<K

II.

i^{\star}=1

III.

i^{\star}=K

Case I:

1<i^{\star}<K

As
i^{\star}
is an optimal sensor therefore
\forall j>i^{\star}:\,C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}>\mathbb{P}\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\}\Rightarrow C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}\nleq\mathbb{P}\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\}\Rightarrow\forall j>i^{\star}\notin\mathcal{B}^{l}_{-1}
. If any sensor
l\in\mathcal{B}^{l}_{-1}
then
l\leq i^{\star}
i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{l}_{-1}=\{l_{1},l_{2},\ldots,l_{m},i^{\star}\}~~~~\mbox{ where }1<l_{1}<\cdots<l_{m}<i^{\star}

(2.24)

Similarly,
\forall j<i^{\star}:\,C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}\leq\mathbb{P}\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\}\Rightarrow C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}\ngtr\mathbb{P}\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\}\Rightarrow\forall j<i^{\star}\notin\mathcal{B}^{h}_{-K}
. If any sensor
h\in\mathcal{B}^{h}_{-K}
then
h\geq i^{\star}
i.e.,

\hskip-2.84526pt\mathcal{B}^{h}_{-K}=\{i^{\star},h_{1},\ldots,h_{n}\}\mbox{ where }i^{\star}<h_{1}<\cdots<h_{n}<K

(2.25)

\mathcal{B}^{h}=\mathcal{B}^{h}_{-K}\cup\{K\}=\{i^{\star},h_{1},h_{2},\ldots,h_{n},K\}

(2.26)

From (
2.22
), (

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}^{l}\cap\mathcal{B}^{h}

\displaystyle=\{1,l_{1},l_{2},\ldots,i^{\star}\}\cap\{i^{\star},h_{1},h_{2},\ldots,h_{k},K\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=\{i^{\star}\}

(2.27)

\mathcal{B}^{l}_{-1}=\varphi

\mathcal{B}^{l}=\{1\}

2.26

\mathcal{B}^{h}=\{1,h_{1},h_{2},\ldots,h_{n},K\}
that implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}=\{1\}\Rightarrow\mathcal{B}=\{i^{\star}\}

),(

∎

The following definition is convenient for the proof arguments.

Definition 7

(Action Preference (
\succ_{t}
))
.

The sensor
i
is optimistically preferred over sensor
j
in round
t
if:

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pti\succ_{t}j:=

\displaystyle C_{i}-C_{j}\leq\hat{p}_{ji}(t)+\Psi_{ji}(t)\;\text{if }j<i

(2.30a)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pti\succ_{t}j:=

\displaystyle C_{j}-C_{i}>\hat{p}_{ij}(t)+\Psi_{ij}(t)\;\text{if }j>i

(2.30b)

Discussion of Remark
2

The algorithm can converge to a sub-optimal sensor when we replace the term
\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}+\Psi_{i^{\star}j}(t)
in (
2.13b
) by
\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}-\Psi_{i^{\star}j}(t)
. To verify this claim, assume algorithm selects sub-optimal sensor
j
in around
t
and
j<i^{\star}
then,

\displaystyle C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}

\displaystyle>\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}-\Psi_{i^{\star}j}(t)

Since sensor
i^{\star}
is not used then there is no update in
\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}(t+1)
but by definition
\Psi_{i^{\star}j}(t+1)>\Psi_{i^{\star}j}(t)
therefore,

\displaystyle C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}

\displaystyle>\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}(t+1)-\Psi_{i^{\star}j}(t+1).

Hence sub-optimal sensor will always be preferred over the optimal sensor in the subsequent rounds. This can be avoided by using UC term in (
2.13b
) because,

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}(t+1)+\Psi_{i^{\star}j}(t+1)>\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}+\Psi_{i^{\star}j}(t)

The sub-optimal sensor
j
will not be preferred after sufficient
n
rounds,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\;

\displaystyle C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}<\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}(t+n)+\Psi_{i^{\star}j}(t+n).

(2.31)

As using LC term can make the decisions stuck to sub-optimal sensor, UC term is used in (
2.13b
).

Missing proofs from
Section

2.4

We first recall the standard Hoeffding’s inequality
(
Hoeffding 1963
, Theorem 2)
that we use in the proof.

Theorem 7
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be independent random variables with common range
[0,1]
,
\mu=\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}\right]
, and
\hat{\mu}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}X_{t}
. Then for all
\varepsilon\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{\mu}_{n}-\mu\leq-\varepsilon\right\}\leq e^{-2n\varepsilon^{2}}

(2.32a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{\mu}_{n}-\mu\geq\varepsilon\right\}\leq e^{-2n\varepsilon^{2}}

(2.32b)

We need the following lemmas to prove the Proposition
6
.

Lemma 10
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{erf}(x)=\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-t^{2}}dt=\int\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}}dx

(2.33)

Proof.

Leibniz’s rule for
0<g(x)\leq h(x)<\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\int_{g(x)}^{h(x)}

\displaystyle f(x,t)dt=f(x,h(x))\frac{dh(x)}{dx}-f(x,g(x))\frac{dg(x)}{dx}+\int_{g(x)}^{h(x)}\frac{\partial f(x,t)}{\partial x}dt

Leibniz’s integral rule without any common variable,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\int_{g(x)}^{h(x)}f(t)dt=f(h(x))\frac{dh(x)}{dx}-f(g(x))\frac{dg(x)}{dx}

Using Leibniz’s rule in (
2.33
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-t^{2}}dt=\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}}\frac{dx}{dx}-1\frac{d0}{dx}=\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\;

\displaystyle{d}\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-t^{2}}dt=\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}}dx

Integrating both side,

\displaystyle\int{d}\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-t^{2}}dt=\int\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}}dx

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-t^{2}}dt=\int\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}}dx

∎

Lemma 11
.

Let
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
t_{c}=b\sqrt{at}\pm\sqrt{acd}
. Then

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{e}^{-t_{c}^{2}}dt

\displaystyle=\mp\dfrac{\sqrt{{\pi}cd}\operatorname{erf}(t_{c})}{\sqrt{a}b^{2}}-\dfrac{\mathrm{e}^{-t_{c}^{2}}}{ab^{2}}+C

\displaystyle\mbox{where }\operatorname{erf}(x)=\dfrac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}}dx~~~~~\operatorname{(using\;Lemma\;\ref{lem:erf_integral})}

Proof.

Let
x=t_{c}\Rightarrow x=b\sqrt{at}\pm\sqrt{acd}
. Then,

\displaystyle t=\frac{(x\mp\sqrt{acd})^{2}}{ab^{2}}

Now differentiate
x
w.r.t.
t
,

\displaystyle\frac{dx}{dt}=\frac{b\sqrt{a}}{2\sqrt{t}}\Rightarrow dt=\frac{2\sqrt{t}}{b\sqrt{a}}dx=\frac{2(x\mp\sqrt{acd})}{ab^{2}}dx

By changing the variable from
t
to
x
in given integral,

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{e}^{-t_{c}^{2}}dt

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}}\frac{2(x\mp\sqrt{acd})}{ab^{2}}dx

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\int\mathrm{e}^{-t_{c}^{2}}dt

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{ab^{2}}\int x\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}}dx\mp\frac{2\sqrt{cd}}{\sqrt{a}b^{2}}\int\mathrm{e}^{-x^{2}}dx

(2.34)

As
\int\mathrm{e}^{-cx^{2}}dx=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{4c}}\operatorname{erf}(\sqrt{c}x)+C
and
\int x\mathrm{e}^{-cx^{2}}dx=-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-cx^{2}}}{2c}+C
, then (
2.34
) with
c=1
is,

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-cx^{2}}}{ab^{2}}\mp\frac{\sqrt{\pi cd}\operatorname{erf}(x)}{\sqrt{a}b^{2}}+C

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\int\mathrm{e}^{-t_{c}^{2}}dt

\displaystyle=\mp\frac{\sqrt{\pi cd}\operatorname{erf}(t_{c})}{\sqrt{a}b^{2}}-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-t_{c}^{2}}}{ab^{2}}+C

∎

Lemma 12
.

Let
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
t_{0}=cdb^{-2}
. Then

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{e}^{-(b\sqrt{at}-\sqrt{acd})^{2}}dt=\dfrac{\sqrt{{\pi}cd}}{\sqrt{a}b^{2}}+\dfrac{1}{ab^{2}}

Proof.

Using Lemma
11
with
t_{c}=b\sqrt{at}-\sqrt{acd}
.

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t_{c}^{2}}dt

\displaystyle=\left.\left(\dfrac{\sqrt{{\pi}cd}\operatorname{erf}(t_{c})}{\sqrt{a}b^{2}}-\dfrac{\mathrm{e}^{-t_{c}^{2}}}{ab^{2}}\right)\right|_{t_{0}}^{\infty}

Since
t_{0}=cdb^{-2},\;t_{c}=0
for
t=t_{0},\;\operatorname{erf}(0)=0
and
\operatorname{erf}(\infty)=1
, we get

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t_{c}^{2}}dt

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\sqrt{{\pi}cd}}{\sqrt{a}b^{2}}+\dfrac{1}{ab^{2}}=\frac{1}{b^{2}}\left(\sqrt{\dfrac{{\pi}cd}{a}}+\dfrac{1}{a}\right)

∎

Lemma 13
.

Let
b,c,d\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
be a sequence of independent random variables,
\hat{\mu}_{t}=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{s=1}^{t}X_{s}
, and
