\mu=\mathbb{E}\left[X_{t}\right]
where
X_{t}\in[0,1],\;\forall t
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{\mu}_{t}-\mu\geq b-\sqrt{\frac{cd}{t}}\right\}\leq 1+\dfrac{1}{b^{2}}\left(cd+\sqrt{\frac{{\pi}cd}{2}}+\dfrac{1}{2}\right)

Proof.

Assume
t_{0}=\left\lceil cdb^{-2}\right\rceil
and
t_{0}<<n
. We divide sum of the interest into two parts as:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{\mu}_{t}-\mu\geq b-\sqrt{\frac{cd}{t}}\right\}=\sum_{t=1}^{t_{0}}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{\mu}_{t}-\mu\geq b-\sqrt{\frac{cd}{t}}\hskip-0.99594pt\right\}+\sum_{t={t_{0}}}^{n}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{\mu}_{t}-\mu\geq b-\sqrt{\frac{cd}{t}}\hskip-0.99594pt\right\}

As
\mathbb{P}\left\{\text{any event}\right\}\leq 1
and for
t>t_{0}
,
b-\sqrt{\frac{cd}{t}}>0
. Using Hoeffding’s inequality (
2.32b
), we get

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{\mu}_{t}-\mu\geq b-\sqrt{\frac{cd}{t}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lceil t_{0}\right\rceil+\sum_{t=\left\lceil t_{0}\right\rceil}^{n}\mathrm{e}^{-2\left(b\sqrt{t}-\sqrt{cd}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\dfrac{cd}{b^{2}}+\int^{\infty}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{e}^{-\left(b\sqrt{2t}-\sqrt{2cd}\right)^{2}}dt

Now using Lemma
12
with
a=2
,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{\mu}_{t}-\mu\geq b-\sqrt{\frac{cd}{t}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\dfrac{1}{b^{2}}\left(cd+\sqrt{\frac{{\pi}cd}{2}}+\dfrac{1}{2}\right)

∎

See
6

Proof.

Assume
N_{T}(j)
be the number of times sensor
j
is selected till
T
rounds and
I_{t}
be the sensor selected by algorithm at round
t
. Then mean number of pulls for any arm
j
is:

\mathbb{E}\left[N_{T}(j)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}}\right]=\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}

We prove the proposition by considering the case
j<i^{\star}
and
j>i^{\star}
separately.

•

Case I:

j<i^{\star}

If sensor
j
is preferred over
i^{\star}
at round
t
then,

\displaystyle C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}>\hat{p}_{ji^{\star}}+\Psi_{ji^{\star}}(t)

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{from \ref{def_prefer_h}}\right)

It is easy to verify that
C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}=p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j}
. By definition,
\hat{p}_{ji^{\star}}=\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}
and
\Psi_{ji^{\star}}(t)=\Psi_{i^{\star}j}(t)
,

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}+\Psi_{i^{\star}j}(t)<p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j}

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{from \ref{def_delta}, \ref{def_kappa_l} and \ref{def_xi_l}}\right)

If algorithm selects sensor
j
in round
t
then it is preferred over an optimal sensor in that round, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j\succ_{t}i^{\star}\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}i^{\star}\right\}=\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha\log{f(t)}}{\mathcal{N}_{i^{\star}j}(t-1)}}<p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j}\right\}

As
\mathcal{N}_{i^{\star}j}(t-1)
is a random variable, Hoeffding’s inequality (
2.32a
) cannot be directly used here. Let
\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j,s}
denote the value of
\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}
when
\mathcal{N}_{i^{\star}j}(t-1)=s
. Then, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{t}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j,s}+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha\log f(t)}{s}}\leq p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{t}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j,s}-p_{i^{\star}j}\leq-\left(\xi_{j}+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha\log f(t)}{s}}\right)\right\}

Now using Hoeffding’s inequality (
2.32a
),

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-2s\left(\xi_{j}+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha\log f(t)}{s}}\right)^{2}}\leq\sum_{s=1}^{t}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-2\xi_{j}^{2}s}\;\mathrm{e}^{-2\alpha\log f(t)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\dfrac{\mathrm{e}^{-2\xi_{j}^{2}s}}{f(t)^{2\alpha}}ds\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\dfrac{\mathrm{e}^{-2\xi_{j}^{2}s}}{f(t)^{2\alpha}}ds

\displaystyle\leq\left.\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-2\xi_{j}^{2}s}}{-2f(t)^{2\alpha}\xi_{j}^{2}}\right)\right|_{0}^{\infty}=\dfrac{1}{2\xi_{j}^{2}f(t)^{2\alpha}}

The mean number of time a sub-optimal sensor selected in
T
rounds is:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[N_{T}(j)\right]=\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\dfrac{1}{2\xi_{j}^{2}f(t)^{2\alpha}}\leq\dfrac{1}{2\xi_{j}^{2}}\sum\limits_{t=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{1}{f(t)^{2\alpha}}=\dfrac{C}{2\xi_{j}^{2}}.

•

Case II:

j>i^{\star}

If sensor
j
is preferred over
i^{\star}
at round
t
then,

\displaystyle C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}\leq\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}+\Psi_{i^{\star}j}(t)

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{\hfill from \ref{def_prefer_l}}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\;\;

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}+\Psi_{i^{\star}j}(t)\geq p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j}

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{from \ref{def_delta}, \ref{def_kappa_h} and \ref{def_xi_h}}\right)

The mean number of time a sub-optimal sensor selected in
T
rounds is given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[N_{T}(j)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}=\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}i^{\star},I_{t}=j\right\}\leq\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}i^{\star}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha\log{f(t)}}{\mathcal{N}_{i^{\star}j}(t-1)}}\geq p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j},\;I_{t}=j\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha\log{f(T)}}{\mathcal{N}_{i^{\star}j}(t-1)}}\geq p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j},\;I_{t}=j\right\}

As
\mathbb{P}\left\{A\cap B\right\}\leq\min\left\{\mathbb{P}\left\{A\right\},\mathbb{P}\left\{B\right\}\right\}\Rightarrow\mathbb{P}\left\{A\cap B\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{A\right\}
or
\mathbb{P}\left\{A\cap B\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{B\right\}
, we get

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{s=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j,s}+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha\log{f(T)}}{s}}\geq p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{s=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j,s}-p_{i^{\star}j}\geq\xi_{j}-\sqrt{\frac{\alpha\log{f(T)}}{s}}\right\}.

Using Lemma
13
with
b=\xi_{j}
,
c=\alpha
,
d=\log{f(T)}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[N_{T}(j)\right]

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{1}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\left(\alpha\log f(T)+\sqrt{\frac{\pi\alpha\log f(T)}{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\right).

∎

See
4

Proof.

Let
N_{j}(T)
is the number of times sensor
j
selected in
T
rounds. Then expected cumulative regret of
USS-UCB
for any instance
\theta\in\Theta_{\mathrm{SA}}
is:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j\neq i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\Delta_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j<i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\Delta_{j}+\sum\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\Delta_{j}

Now, using the fact that for
j<i^{\star}
,
\Delta_{j}=\xi_{j}-\kappa_{j}
and for
j>i^{\star}
,
\Delta_{j}=\xi_{j}+\kappa_{j}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j<i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right](\xi_{j}-\kappa_{j})+\sum\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right](\xi_{j}+\kappa_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{j<i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\xi_{j}+\sum\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right](\xi_{j}+\kappa_{j})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\sum\limits_{j<i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\xi_{j}}_{\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}^{1}_{T}\right]}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right](\xi_{j}+\beta)}_{\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}^{2}_{T}\right]},

(2.35)

where

\forall j,\;\kappa_{j}\leq\beta\;\;\begin{cases}=0\quad\mbox{if }P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}\\
\leq 1\quad\mbox{if }P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}\mbox{ and sensors are ordered by their error-rate}\\
\leq 2\quad\mbox{if }P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}\mbox{ and sensors are arbitrarily ordered by their error-rates}\end{cases}

(2.36)

In the following, we set
\xi=\min\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\xi_{j}
. Now we consider the case
\xi\geq 1
and
\xi<1
separately.

•

Case I:
\xi\geq 1\Leftrightarrow\forall j>i^{\star},\;\xi_{j}\geq 1

Using Proposition
6
to upper bound
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}^{2}_{T}\right]
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}^{2}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\Bigg(1\;+\frac{1}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\Bigg(\alpha\log f(T)\;+\sqrt{\frac{\pi\alpha\log f(T)}{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\Bigg)\Bigg)(\xi_{j}+\beta)

\displaystyle\leq K\Bigg((\xi_{j}+2)+\Bigg(\alpha\log f(T)+\sqrt{\frac{\pi\alpha\log f(T)}{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\Bigg)\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{j}}+\frac{\beta}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\right)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}^{2}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq K(\xi_{j}+2)+K\Bigg(\alpha\log f(T)+\sqrt{\frac{\pi\alpha\log f(T)}{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\Bigg)\left(\frac{1}{\xi}+\frac{\beta}{\xi^{2}}\right)

(2.37)

For
\xi\geq 1
,
\left(\frac{1}{\xi}+\frac{\beta}{\xi^{2}}\right)\leq\beta+1
. Further, by definition
\forall j>i^{\star},\;\;\xi_{j}=C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}-\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}
, one can easily verify that
\max\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\xi_{j}\leq C_{K}-C_{1}
as
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\geq 0
. Assume
C_{K}-C_{1}\leq C_{1}^{K}
, then (
2.37
) can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}^{2}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq K(C_{1}^{K}+2)+(\beta+1)K\Bigg(\alpha\log f(T)+\sqrt{\frac{\pi\alpha\log f(T)}{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(2C_{1}^{K}+\beta+5)K}{2}+(\beta+1)K\Bigg(\alpha\log f(T)+\sqrt{\frac{\pi\alpha\log f(T)}{2}}\Bigg)

(2.38)

For any
\xi^{\prime}
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}^{1}\right]
can written as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}^{1}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}>\xi_{j}\\
j<i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\xi_{j}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}\\
j<i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\xi_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{j<i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\xi^{\prime}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}\\
j<i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\frac{C}{2\xi_{j}^{2}}\xi_{j}~~~~~\mbox{(using Proposition \ref{prop:meanpulls})}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}^{1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq T\xi^{\prime}+\frac{CK}{2\xi^{\prime}}~~~~~\left(\mbox{since }\sum\limits_{j<i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\leq T\right)

(2.39)

From definition,
\forall j<i^{\star},\;\xi_{j}=\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}-(C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j})
. As sensors are ordered by increasing cost, one can verify that
\xi_{j}<1
for
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
. With this fact, by combining (
2.38
) and (
2.39
), (
2.35
) can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]\leq T\xi^{\prime}+\frac{CK}{2\xi^{\prime}}+\frac{(2C_{1}^{K}+\beta+5)K}{2}+3K\Bigg(\alpha\log f(T)+\sqrt{\frac{\pi\alpha\log f(T)}{2}}\Bigg)

Choose
\xi^{\prime}=\sqrt{\frac{CK}{T}}
which maximize the upper bound and we get,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]\leq\sqrt{2CKT}+\frac{(2C_{1}^{K}+\beta+5)K}{2}+3K\Bigg(\alpha\log f(T)+\sqrt{\frac{\pi\alpha\log f(T)}{2}}\Bigg)

(2.40)

•

Case II:
\xi<1

Assume
T\geq T_{0}
for
j>i^{\star}
such that

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\left(\alpha\log f(T)+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha\pi\log f(T)}{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\leq\frac{2\alpha\log f(T)}{\xi_{j}^{2}}

(2.41)

For
\alpha=1
and
T_{0}=56
(
2.41
) holds for all
T\geq T_{0}
. Let
0<\xi^{\prime}<\xi
. Then
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}^{2}_{T}\right]
can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}>\xi_{j}\\
j<i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\xi_{j}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}\\
j<i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\xi_{j}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}>\xi_{j}\\
j>i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right](\xi_{j}+\beta)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}\\
j>i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right](\xi_{j}+\beta)

Since
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}>\xi_{j}\\
j<i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{j}(T)\right]\leq T
and for every
j>i^{\star},\;\xi_{j}>\xi^{\prime}
. Using Proposition
6
and (
2.41
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq T\xi^{\prime}+\sum\limits_{\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}}\frac{C}{2\xi_{j}^{2}}\xi_{j}+\sum\limits_{\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}}\frac{2\alpha\log f(T)}{\xi_{j}^{2}}(\xi_{j}+\beta)

\displaystyle\leq T\xi^{\prime}+\frac{CK}{2\xi^{\prime}}+2\alpha K\log f(T)\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{\prime}}+\frac{\beta}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)

As
C=\lim\limits_{T\rightarrow\infty}\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}\frac{1}{t^{2\alpha}}
, one can verify that for
T_{0}=56
and
\alpha=1
,
C<2\alpha\log f(T)
holds. Using this fact,

\displaystyle\leq T\xi^{\prime}+4\alpha K\log f(T)\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{\prime}}+\frac{\beta}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq T\xi^{\prime}+4\alpha K\log f(T)\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{\prime}}+\frac{\beta}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)

(2.42)

We first consider
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
class of problems. For
\xi^{\prime}<1
and
\beta\leq 2
, we have
\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{\prime}}+\frac{\beta}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{\beta+1}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}\leq\frac{3}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq T\xi^{\prime}+\frac{12\alpha K\log f(T)}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}

Choose
\xi^{\prime}=\left(\frac{24\alpha K\log f(T)}{T}\right)^{1/3}
which maximize above upper bound and we get,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(24\alpha K\log f(T)\right)^{1/3}T^{2/3}+\frac{\left(24\alpha K\log f(T)\right)^{1/3}}{2}T^{2/3}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(3\alpha K\log f(T)\right)^{1/3}T^{2/3}+\left(3\alpha K\log f(T)\right)^{1/3}T^{2/3}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 3\left(3\alpha K\log f(T)\right)^{1/3}T^{2/3}

(2.43)

As
C<2\alpha\log f(T)
and
K<<T
, it is clear that upper bound in (
2.43
) is worse than (
2.42
). Hence it completes our proof for the case when any problem instance belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
.

Now we consider any problem instance
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}
. For any
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}\Rightarrow\forall j\in[K],\;\kappa_{j}=0\Rightarrow\beta=0
. Hence (
2.42
) can be written as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq T\xi^{\prime}+\frac{4\alpha K\log f(T)}{\xi^{\prime}}

Choose
\xi^{\prime}=\left(\frac{4\alpha K\log f(T)}{T}\right)^{1/2}
which maximize above upper bound and we get,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(4\alpha KT\log f(T)\right)^{1/2}=4\left(\alpha KT\log f(T)\right)^{1/2}

(2.44)

As
C<2\alpha\log f(T)
and
K<<T
then upper bound of expected regret in (
2.42
) is
3\left(\alpha KT\log f(T)\right)^{1/2}
which is better than (
2.44
). It complete proof for second part of Theorem
4
.
∎

See
3

Proof.

Assume that
C_{j}-C_{i}>\max\{0,\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}\}
. Since
C_{j}-C_{i}\notin\left(\max\{0,\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}\},\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\right]
, we get
C_{j}-C_{i}>\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{j}\neq Y^{i}\right\}
.

The other direction follows by noting that
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{j}\neq Y^{i}\right\}\geq\max\{0,\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}\}
.
∎

See
4

Proof.

Assume that
C_{i}-C_{j}\leq\max\{0,\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}\}
. Since
\max\{0,\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}
, we get
C_{j}-C_{i}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}
.

The condition
C_{j}-C_{i}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}
along with
C_{i}-C_{j}\notin\left(\max\{0,\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}\},\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\right]
implies the other direction, i.e.,
C_{i}-C_{j}\leq\max\{0,\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}\}
.
∎

See
5

Proof.

From Proposition
3
and
4
, if the optimal sensor satisfies
for
j>i^{\star}

C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}\notin\left(\max\{0,\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}\},\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\right]

and for
j<i^{\star}

C_{i^{*}}-C_{j}\notin\left(\max\{0,\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}\},\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\right],

Then, for
j>i^{\star},C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}>\gamma_{i^{\star}}-\gamma_{j}
iff
C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}>\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}

and for

j<i^{\star},C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}\leq\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i^{\star}}
iff
C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}
. Hence we can use
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}\neq Y^{j}\right\}
as a proxy for
\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}
to make decision about the optimal arm.

Now notice that for
j<i^{\star}
,
C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}\leq\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i^{\star}}\leq\max\{0,\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i^{\star}}\}
(from Lemma
2
). Hence for
j<i^{\star}
the condition

C_{i^{*}}-C_{j}\notin\left(\max\{0,\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}\},\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\right]

is satisfied. Then, the condition

C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}}\notin\left(\max\{0,\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}\},\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i^{\star}}\neq Y^{j}\right\}\right]

for
j>i^{\star}
is sufficient for learnability.
∎

Missing proofs from
Section

2.5

We use the following results in our proofs.

Fact 2

(Chernoff bound for Bernoulli distributed random variables)
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be i.i.d. Bernoulli distributed random variables. Let
\hat{\mu}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
and
\mu=\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}\right]
. Then, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1-\mu)
,

\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{\mu}_{n}\geq\mu+\varepsilon\right\}\leq\exp\left(-d(\mu+\varepsilon,\mu)n\right),

and, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\mu)
,

\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{\mu}_{n}\leq\mu-\varepsilon\right\}\leq\exp\left(-d(\mu-\varepsilon,\mu)n\right),

where
d(x,\mu)=x\log\left(\frac{x}{\mu}\right)+(1-x)\log\left(\frac{1-x}{1-\mu}\right)
.

See Section 10.1 of Chapter 10 of book ‘Bandit Algorithms’
(
Lattimore and
Szepesvári 2020a
)
for proof.

Fact 3

(Pinsker’s Inequality for Bernoulli distributed random variables)
.

For
p,q\in(0,1)
, the KL divergence between two Bernoulli distributions is bounded as:

d(p,q)\geq 2(p-q)^{2}.

Fact 4
.

Let
x>0
and
D>0
. Then, for any
a\in(0,1)
,

\frac{1}{\exp^{Dx}-1}\leq\begin{cases}\frac{\exp^{-Dx}}{1-a}&\left(x\geq\ln\left(1/a\right)/D\right)\\
\frac{1}{Dx}&\left(x<\ln\left(1/a\right)/D\right).\end{cases}

Further, we have,

\sum_{x=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\exp^{Dx}-1}\leq\Theta\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}+\frac{1}{D}\right).

Proof.

Using
\exp^{y}\geq y+1
(by Taylor Series expansion), we have
\frac{1}{\exp^{Dx}-1}\leq\frac{1}{Dx}
as
\exp^{Dx}-1\geq Dx
. We can re-write,
\frac{1}{\exp^{Dx}-1}=\frac{\exp^{-Dx}}{1-\exp^{-Dx}}
. Since
\exp^{-Dx}
is strictly decreasing function for all
Dx>0
, it is easy to check that
\exp^{-Dx}\leq a
holds for any
x\geq\ln\left(1/a\right)/D
and
a\in(0,1)
. Hence,
\frac{\exp^{-Dx}}{1-\exp^{-Dx}}\leq\frac{\exp^{-Dx}}{1-a}
for all
x\geq\ln\left(1/a\right)/D
.

Now we will prove the second part,

\displaystyle\sum_{x=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\exp^{Dx}-1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\ln(1/a)}{D^{2}}+\sum_{x\geq\ln\left(1/a\right)/D}^{n}\frac{\exp^{-Dx}}{1-a}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\ln(1/a)}{D^{2}}+\frac{1}{(1-a)}\int_{x=0}^{\infty}\exp^{-Dx}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{\ln(1/a)}{D^{2}}+\frac{1}{(1-a)}\left.\left(\frac{\exp^{-Dx}}{-D}\right)\right|_{x=0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{\ln(1/a)}{D^{2}}+\frac{1}{(1-a)}\left(0-\frac{\exp^{0}}{-D}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\ln(1/a)}{D^{2}}+\frac{1}{(1-a)D}

\displaystyle\implies\sum_{x=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\exp^{Dx}-1}

\displaystyle\leq\Theta\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}+\frac{1}{D}\right).

∎

Fact 5
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
0<x<y<z<1
. If
d(y,z)=d(x,z)/(1+\varepsilon)
then

y-x\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{d(x,z)}{\ln\left(\frac{z(1-x)}{x(1-z)}\right)}.

Proof.

By definition

\displaystyle d(p,q)

\displaystyle=p\ln\frac{p}{q}+(1-p)\ln\left(\frac{1-p}{1-q}\right)

\displaystyle=\ln\left(\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{p}\left(\frac{1-p}{1-q}\right)^{1-p}\right)

\displaystyle=\ln\left(\left(\frac{q(1-p)}{p(1-q)}\right)^{-p}\right)+\ln\left(\frac{1-p}{1-q}\right)

\displaystyle\implies d(p,q)

\displaystyle=-p\ln\left(\frac{q(1-p)}{p(1-q)}\right)+\ln\left(\frac{1-p}{1-q}\right).

Set
l(p,q)=\ln\left(\frac{q(1-p)}{p(1-q)}\right)
. Note that
l(p,\cdot)
is a strictly decreasing function of
p
and positive for all
p<q
. We can re-arrange above equation as

p\cdot l(p,q)=-d(p,q)+\ln\left(\frac{1-p}{1-q}\right).

Using above equation, we have

\displaystyle y\cdot l(y,z)-x\cdot l(x,z)

\displaystyle=-d(y,z)+\ln\left(\frac{1-y}{1-z}\right)+d(x,z)-\ln\left(\frac{1-x}{1-z}\right).

Using
d(y,z)=d(x,z)/(1+\varepsilon)
,

\displaystyle y\cdot l(y,z)-x\cdot l(x,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}d(x,z)+\ln\left(\frac{1-y}{1-x}\right).

After adding
y(l(x,z)-l(y,z))
both side, we have

\displaystyle(y-x)l(x,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}d(x,z)+\ln\left(\frac{1-y}{1-x}\right)+y(l(x,z)-l(y,z)).

Using
l(x,z)=\ln\left(\frac{z(1-x)}{x(1-z)}\right)
and
l(y,z)=\ln\left(\frac{z(1-y)}{y(1-z)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}d(x,z)+\ln\left(\frac{1-y}{1-x}\right)+y\ln\left(\frac{y(1-x)}{x(1-y)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}d(x,z)+\ln\left(\left(\frac{y(1-x)}{x(1-y)}\right)^{y}\cdot\frac{1-y}{1-x}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}d(x,z)+\ln\left(\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)^{y}\left(\frac{1-y}{1-x}\right)^{1-y}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}d(x,z)+d(y,x)

As
d(p,q)\geq 0
and dividing both side by
l(x,z)
,

\displaystyle\implies y-x

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{d(x,z)}{l(x,z)}.

Substituting value of
l(x,z)
in the above equation, we get

y-x\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{d(x,z)}{\ln\left(\frac{z(1-x)}{x(1-z)}\right)}.

∎

See
5

Proof.

If the sub-optimal arm
j
is selected then arm
j
is preferred over the arms whose indexed is larger than
j
(
3
). Hence we have

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}=

\displaystyle~\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}k,\forall k>j,j<i^{\star}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}k,\forall k\geq{i^{\star}},j<i^{\star}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}.

Since the feedback from an arm is independent of the feedback of other arms,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}i^{\star},j<i^{\star}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}k,\forall k>{i^{\star}},j<i^{\star}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}.

If arm
j
is preferred over the arm
i^{\star}
then
\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}<C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}
. As
C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}=p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j}
for
j<i^{\star}
,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\mathbb{P}\left\{\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}<p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}k,\forall k>{i^{\star}},j<i^{\star}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\left(1-\mathbb{P}\left\{\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\geq p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\right)\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}k,\forall k>{i^{\star}},j<i^{\star}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\leq

\displaystyle~(1-q_{j,t})\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}k,\forall k>{i^{\star}},j<i^{\star}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}.\mbox{\hskip 11.38109pt (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:q})}

(2.45)

Similarly, the probability of selecting an arm whose index is larger than the optimal arm can be lower bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}\geq i^{\star}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=i^{\star}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\geq\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=i^{\star},{i^{\star}}\succ_{t}j,j<{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{i^{\star}\succ_{t}k,\forall k>{i^{\star}},{i^{\star}}\succ_{t}j,j<{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\mbox{\hskip 11.38109pt (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:Bx})}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\left\{{i^{\star}}\succ_{t}j,j\succ_{t}k,\forall k>{i^{\star}},j<{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\mbox{\hskip 14.22636pt (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:trans_prop})}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{{i^{\star}}\succ_{t}j,j<{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}k,\forall k>{i^{\star}},j<{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}.

If arm
{i^{\star}}
is preferred over the arm
j
then
\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\geq C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}
. As
C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}=p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j}
for
j<i^{\star}
,

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\geq p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}k,\forall k>{i^{\star}},j<i^{\star}\right\}

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}\geq{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq q_{j,t}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}k,\forall k>{i^{\star}},j<i^{\star}\right\}.\mbox{\hskip 48.36967pt (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:q})}

(2.46)

Combining the
Eq.

2.45
and
Eq.

2.46
, we get

\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\leq\frac{(1-q_{j,t})}{q_{j,t}}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}\geq{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}.

∎

See
6

Proof.

Applying
5
and properties of conditional expectations, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\right].

As
q_{j,t}
is fixed given
\mathcal{H}_{t}
,

\displaystyle\implies\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{(1-q_{j,t})}{q_{j,t}}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}\geq i^{\star}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{(1-q_{j,t})}{q_{j,t}}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}\geq i^{\star}\right\}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right]\right].

Using law of iterated expectations,

\displaystyle\implies\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{(1-q_{j,t})}{q_{j,t}}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}\geq i^{\star}\right\}}\right].

(2.47)

Let
s_{m}
denote the time step at which the output of arm
i^{\star}
is observed for the
m^{th}
time for
m\geq 1
, and let
s_{0}=0
. For
j<i^{\star}
, whenever the output from arm
i^{\star}
is observed then the output from arm
j
is also observed due to the cascade structure. Note that
q_{j,t}=\mathbb{P}\left\{\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}
changes only when the distribution of
\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}
changes, that is, only on the time step when the feedback from arms
i^{\star}
and
j
are observed. It only happens when selected arm
I_{t}\geq{i^{\star}}
. Hence,
q_{j,t}
is the same at all time steps
t\in\{s_{m}+1,\ldots,s_{m+1}\}
for every
m
. Using this fact, we can decompose the right hand side term in
Eq.

2.47
as follows,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{(1-q_{j,t})}{q_{j,t}}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}\geq i^{\star}\right\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{(1-q_{j,s_{m}+1})}{q_{j,s_{m}+1}}\sum_{t=s_{m}+1}^{s_{m+1}}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}\geq i^{\star}\right\}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{(1-q_{j,s_{m}+1})}{q_{j,s_{m}+1}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{q_{j,s_{m}+1}}-1\right].

Using above bound in
Eq.

2.47
, we get

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}\right\}\leq\sum_{m=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{q_{j,s_{m}+1}}-1\right].

Substituting the bound from
4
with
\mu=p_{i^{\star}j},x=p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j},\Delta(x)=\xi_{j},
and
q_{n}(x)=q_{j,s_{m}}
, we obtain the following bound,

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<{i^{\star}}\right\}\leq\frac{24}{\xi_{j}^{2}}+\sum_{s\geq 8/\xi_{j}}\Theta\left(\exp^{-{s\xi_{j}^{2}}/{2}}+\frac{\exp^{-{sd(p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j},p_{i^{\star}j})}}}{(s+1)\xi_{j}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\exp^{{s\xi_{j}^{2}}/{4}}-1}\right).

∎

See
7

Proof.

Define
L_{j}(T)=\frac{\ln T}{d(x_{j},y_{j})}
. Let
N_{j}(t)
be the number of times the output from arm
j
is observed in
t
rounds. Then, the given probability term can be decomposed into two parts:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j},N_{j}(t)\leq L_{j}(T)\right\}+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j},N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq L_{j}(T)

\displaystyle+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j},N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)\right\}.

(2.48)

The first term of the above decomposition is bounded trivially by
L_{j}(T)
. To bound the second term, we demonstrate that if
N_{j}(t)
is large enough and event
\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j}
is satisfied, then the probability that the event
\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(t)}>y_{j}
happens, is small. Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j},N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j},N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)\right\}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j},N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)\right\}}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right]\right].

Since
N_{j}(t)
and
\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}
are determined by the history
\mathcal{H}_{t}
,

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}_{\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)\right\}}\mathbb{P}\left\{\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\right].

(2.49)

Now, by definition,
\mathcal{S}_{i^{\star}j}(t)=\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}N_{j}(t)
, and therefore,
\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}
is a Beta
(\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}N_{j}(t)+1,(1-\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)})N_{j}(t)+1)
distributed random variable. A Beta
(\alpha,\beta)
random variable is stochastically dominated by Beta
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
if
\alpha^{\prime}\geq\alpha,\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta
. Therefore, if
\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j}
, the distribution of
\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}
is stochastically dominated by Beta
(x_{j}N_{j}(t)+1,(1-x_{j})N_{j}(t))
. Therefore, given a history
\mathcal{H}_{t}
such that
\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j}
and
N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left\{\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}=1-F_{x_{j}N_{j}(t)+1,(1-x_{j})N_{j}(t)}^{beta}(y_{j}).

Now, using Beta-Binomial equality (
1
), we obtain that for any fixed
N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)
,

\displaystyle 1-F_{x_{j}N_{j}(t)+1,(1-x_{j})N_{j}(t)}^{beta}(y_{j})

\displaystyle=F_{N_{j}(t),y_{j}}^{B}(x_{j}N_{j}(t))

(using
1
)

Here
F_{N_{j}(t),y_{j}}^{B}(x_{j}N_{j}(t))
is the cdf of Binomial distribution with parameter
y_{j}
and
N_{j}(T)
observations. Let
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\prime}
be the number of successes observed in
N_{j}(T)
observations. Then,

\displaystyle 1-F_{x_{j}N_{j}(t)+1,(1-x_{j})N_{j}(t)}^{beta}(y_{j})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\prime}\leq x_{j}N_{j}(t)\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\prime}}{N_{j}(t)}\leq x_{j}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{y}_{j}\leq x_{j}\right\}\hskip 48.36967pt\mbox{(using $\hat{y}_{j}=\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\prime}/N_{j}(t)$)}

\displaystyle\leq\exp^{-N_{j}(t)d(x_{j},y_{j})}\hskip 28.45274pt\mbox{(using Chernoff-Hoeffding bound)}

\displaystyle\leq\exp^{-L_{j}(t)d(x_{j},y_{j})},\hskip 28.45274pt\mbox{(as $N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)$)}

which is smaller than
1/T
because
L_{j}(T)=\frac{\log(T)}{d(x_{j},y_{j})}
. Substituting, we get that for a history
\mathcal{H}_{t}
such that
\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j}
and
N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j}|\mathcal{H}_{t}\right\}\leq\frac{1}{T}.

For other history
\mathcal{H}_{t}
, the indicator term
\mathds{1}_{\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)\right\}}
in
Eq.

2.49
will be 0 as either event
\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j}
or event
N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)
is violated. Summing over
t
, this bounds the right hand side term in
Eq.

2.49
as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j},N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{\mathds{1}_{\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},N_{j}(t)>L_{j}(T)\right\}}}{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{1}{T}\right]

\displaystyle=1.

Replacing the second term in
Eq.

2.48
by its upper bound and
L_{j}(T)
with its value,

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j}\right\}\leq\frac{\ln T}{d(x_{j},y_{j})}+1.

∎

See
8

Proof.

Let
s_{m}
denote the time step at which the outputs of arm
i^{\star}
and
j
is observed for the
m^{th}
time for
m\geq 1
, and let
s_{0}=0
. Note that probability
\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>x_{j}\right\}
changes when the outputs from both arm
i^{\star}
and
j
are observed. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>x_{j}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}(s_{m+1})>x_{j}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}(s_{m+1})-p_{i^{\star}j}>x_{j}-p_{i^{\star}j}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=0}^{T-1}\exp^{-kd(p_{i^{\star}j}+x_{j}-p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j})}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{(using \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fact:chernoff}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{T-1}\exp^{-kd(x_{j},p_{i^{\star}j})}.

Using
\sum_{s\geq 0}\exp^{-sa}\leq 1/a
, we get

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>x_{j}\right\}\leq\frac{1}{d(x_{j},p_{i^{\star}j})}.

∎

See
9

Proof.

Let
p_{i^{\star}j}<x_{j}<y_{j}<p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j}
for any
j>i^{\star}
. Than,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}i^{\star},j>{i^{\star}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}\leq x_{j},\tilde{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>y_{j}\right\}+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{\hat{p}_{i^{\star}j}^{(t)}>x_{j}\right\}.

Using
8
and
7
, we have

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}i^{\star},j>{i^{\star}}\right\}\leq\frac{\ln T}{d(x_{j},y_{j})}+1+\frac{1}{d(x_{j},p_{i^{\star}j})}.

For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, we set
x_{j}\in(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})
such that
d(x_{j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})=d(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})/(1+\varepsilon)
, and set
y_{j}\in(x_{j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})
such that
d(x_{j},y_{j})=d(x_{j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})/(1+\varepsilon)=d(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})/(1+\varepsilon)^{2}
. Then this gives

\frac{\ln(T)}{d(x_{j},y_{j})}=(1+\varepsilon)^{2}\frac{\ln(T)}{d(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})}.

Using
5
, if
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
x_{j}\in(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})
, and
d(x_{j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})=d(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})/(1+\varepsilon)
then

x_{j}-p_{i^{\star}j}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}.\frac{d(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})}{\ln\left(\frac{(p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})(1-p_{i^{\star}j})}{p_{i^{\star}j}(1-p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j})}\right)}.

Using Pinsker’s Inequality (
3
),
1/d(x_{j},p_{i^{\star}j})\leq 1/2(x_{j}-p_{i^{\star}j})^{2}=O({1}/{\varepsilon^{2}})
where big-Oh is hiding functions of the
p_{i^{\star}j}
and
\xi_{j}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}{i^{\star}},j>i^{\star}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)^{2}\frac{\ln(T)}{d(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})}+O\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq(1+3\varepsilon)\frac{\ln(T)}{d(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})}+O\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\frac{\ln(T)}{d(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})}+O\left(\frac{1}{{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\right),

where
\varepsilon^{\prime}=3\varepsilon
and the big-Oh above hides
p_{i^{\star}j}
and
\xi_{j}
in addition to the absolute constants. Replacing
\varepsilon
by
\varepsilon^{\prime}
completes the proof.
∎

See
5

Proof.

Let
M_{j}(T)
is the number of times arm
j
is selected by
USS-TS
. Than, the regret is

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[K]}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]\Delta_{j}=\sum_{j\in[K]}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}}\right]\Delta_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[K]}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}}\right]\Delta_{j}=\sum_{j\in[K]}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j\right\}\Delta_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[K]}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j\neq{i^{\star}}\right\}\Delta_{j}

\displaystyle\implies{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j<i^{\star}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<i^{\star}\right\}\Delta_{j}+\sum_{j>i^{\star}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j>i^{\star}\right\}\Delta_{j}

(2.50)

First, we bound the first of term of summation. From
6
, we have

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<i^{\star}\right\}\leq\frac{24}{\xi_{j}^{2}}+\sum_{s\geq 8/\xi_{j}}\Theta\left(\exp^{-{s\xi_{j}^{2}}/{2}}+\frac{\exp^{-{sd(p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j},p_{i^{\star}j})}}}{(s+1)\xi_{j}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\exp^{{s\xi_{j}^{2}}/{4}}-1}\right).

Using
\sum_{s\geq 0}\exp^{-sa}\leq 1/a
,
d(p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j},p_{i^{\star}j})\leq 2\xi_{j}^{2}
(
3
), and
4
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<i^{\star}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{24}{\xi_{j}^{2}}+\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{j}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\xi_{j}^{4}}+\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{j}^{4}}+\frac{1}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\right)\right)\leq O(1).

(2.51)

If arm
I_{t}>{i^{\star}}
is selected then there exists at least one arm
k_{1}>{i^{\star}}
which must be preferred over
{i^{\star}}
. If the index of arm
k_{1}
is smaller than the selected arm, then there must be an arm
k_{2}>k_{1}
, which must be preferred over
k_{1}
. By transitivity property, arm
k_{2}
is also preferred over
{i^{\star}}
. If the index of arm
k_{2}
is still smaller of the selected arm, we can repeat the same argument. Eventually, we can find an arm
k^{\prime}
whose index is larger than the selected arm, and it is preferred over arm
k_{i},\ldots,k_{1},{i^{\star}}
. Note that the selected arm must be preferred over
k^{\prime}
; hence the selected arm is also preferred over
{i^{\star}}
. We can write it as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j>i^{\star}\right\}\Delta_{j}=

\displaystyle~\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j>i^{\star},k^{\prime}\succ_{t}k,k\succ_{t}{i^{\star}},k^{\prime}>j,k>{i^{\star}}\right\}\Delta_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j>i^{\star},k^{\prime}\succ_{t}{i^{\star}},k^{\prime}>j\right\}\Delta_{j}\mbox{\hskip 5.69054pt (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:trans_prop})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}k,\forall k>j,j>i^{\star},k^{\prime}\succ_{t}{i^{\star}},k^{\prime}>j\right\}\Delta_{j}\mbox{\hskip 2.84526pt(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:Bx})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}k,\forall k>j,j>i^{\star},j\succ_{t}{i^{\star}}\right\}\Delta_{j}\mbox{\hskip 5.69054pt (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:trans_prop})}

\displaystyle\implies\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j>i^{\star}\right\}\Delta_{j}\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}{i^{\star}},j>{i^{\star}}\right\}\Delta_{j}.

(2.52)

Using
9
to upper bound
\sum_{t=1}^{T}

\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}{i^{\star}},j>i^{\star}\right\}\Delta_{j}
and with
Eq.

2.51
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq O(1)+\sum_{j>i^{\star}}\left((1+\varepsilon)\frac{\ln(T)}{d(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})}+O\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\right)\Delta_{j}

\displaystyle\implies{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j>i^{\star}}\ \frac{(1+\varepsilon)\ln(T)}{d(p_{i^{\star}j},p_{i^{\star}j}+\xi_{j})}\Delta_{j}+O\left(\frac{K-i^{\star}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right).

∎

See
6

Proof.

Let
M_{j}(T)
is the number of times arm
j
preferred over the optimal arm in
T
rounds. From
6
, for any
j<i^{\star}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j<i^{\star}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{24}{\xi_{j}^{2}}+\sum_{s\geq 8/\xi_{j}}\Theta\left(\exp^{-{s\xi_{j}^{2}}/{2}}+\frac{\exp^{-{sd(p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j},p_{i^{\star}j})}}}{(s+1)\xi_{j}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\exp^{{s\xi_{j}^{2}}/{4}}-1}\right).

It is east to show that
\frac{\exp^{-{sd(p_{i^{\star}j}-\xi_{j},p_{i^{\star}j})}}}{(s+1)\xi_{j}^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{(s+1)\xi_{j}^{2}}
and
{\exp^{{s\xi_{j}^{2}}/{4}}-1}\geq{s\xi_{j}^{2}}/4
(as
\exp^{y}\geq y+1
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]\leq\frac{24}{\xi_{j}^{2}}+\sum_{s\geq 8/\xi_{j}}\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{j}^{2}}+\frac{1}{(s+1)\xi_{j}^{2}}+\frac{4}{s\xi_{j}^{2}}\right).

By using
\sum_{s\geq 0}\exp^{-sa}\leq 1/a
and
\sum_{s=1}^{T}(1/s)=\log T
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{24}{\xi_{j}^{2}}+\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{j}^{2}}+\frac{\ln T}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\right)\implies\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]\leq O\left(\frac{\ln T}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\right).

(2.53)

For any
j>i^{\star}
, using
7
and
8
with
Eq.

2.52
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{I_{t}=j,j>i^{\star}\right\}\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{j\succ_{t}{i^{\star}},j>i^{\star}\right\}\leq\frac{\ln T}{d(x_{j},y_{j})}+1+\frac{1}{d(x_{j},p_{i^{\star}j})}.

By setting
x_{j}=p_{i^{\star}j}+\frac{\xi_{j}}{3}
and
y_{j}=p_{i^{\star}j}+\frac{2\xi_{j}}{3}
, we have
d(x_{j},y_{j})\geq\frac{2\xi_{j}^{2}}{9}
and
d(x_{j},p_{i^{\star}j})\geq\frac{2\xi_{j}^{2}}{9}
(using
3
).

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9\ln T}{2\xi_{j}^{2}}+1+\frac{9}{2\xi_{j}^{2}}

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\frac{\ln T}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\right).

(2.54)

The regret of
USS-TS
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}=\sum\limits_{j\neq i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]\Delta_{j}=\sum\limits_{j<i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]\Delta_{j}+\sum\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]\Delta_{j}

Recall
\Delta_{j}=C_{j}+\gamma_{j}-(C_{i^{\star}}+\gamma_{i^{\star}})
and for any two arms
i
and
j
,
0\leq p_{ij}-(\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i^{\star}})\leq\beta
. By using Eq. (
2.16a
) for
j<i^{\star}
, we have
\Delta_{j}=\xi_{j}-(p_{i^{\star}j}-(\gamma_{i^{\star}}-\gamma_{j}))\implies\Delta_{j}\leq\xi_{j}
, and using Eq. (
2.16b
) for
j>i^{\star}
, we have
\Delta_{j}=\xi_{j}+(p_{i^{\star}j}-(\gamma_{i^{\star}}-\gamma_{j}))\implies\Delta_{j}\leq\xi_{j}+\beta
. Replacing
\Delta_{j}
,

\Rightarrow{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}\leq\sum\limits_{j<i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]\xi_{j}+\sum\limits_{j>i^{\star}}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right](\xi_{j}+\beta).

Let
0<\xi^{\prime}<1
. Then
{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}
can be written as:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}>\xi_{j}\\
j<i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]\xi_{j}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}\\
j<i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]\xi_{j}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}>\xi_{j}\\
j>i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right](\xi_{j}+\beta)+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}\\
j>i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right](\xi_{j}+\beta).

Using
\sum\limits_{\xi^{\prime}>\xi_{j}}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]\leq T
for any
j
such that
\xi^{\prime}>\xi_{j}
,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq T\xi^{\prime}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}\\
j<i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right]\xi_{j}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}\\
j>i^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{j}(T)\right](\xi_{j}+\beta).

Substituting the value of
{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}
from
Eq.

2.53
and
Eq.

2.54
,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq T\xi^{\prime}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}\\
j<i^{\star}\end{subarray}}O\left(\frac{\xi_{j}\ln T}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\right)+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}\\
j>i^{\star}\end{subarray}}O\left(\frac{(\xi_{j}+\beta)\ln T}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq T\xi^{\prime}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}\\
j<i^{\star}\end{subarray}}O\left(\frac{\ln T}{\xi_{j}}\right)+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi^{\prime}<\xi_{j}\\
j>i^{\star}\end{subarray}}O\left(\frac{\ln T}{\xi_{j}}+\frac{\beta\ln T}{\xi_{j}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq T\xi^{\prime}+O\left(\frac{K\ln T}{\xi^{\prime}}\right)+O\left(\frac{K\ln T}{\xi^{\prime}}+\frac{\beta K\ln T}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=T\xi^{\prime}+O\left(K\ln T\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{\prime}}+\frac{\beta}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)\right)

Let there exist a variable
\alpha
such that
O\left(K\ln T\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{\prime}}+\frac{\beta}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)\right)\leq\alpha K\ln T\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{\prime}}+\frac{\beta}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)
,

\displaystyle\implies{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq=T\xi^{\prime}+\alpha K\ln T\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{\prime}}+\frac{\beta}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}\right).

(2.55)

Consider
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
class of problems. As
\xi^{\prime}<1
and
\beta\leq 2
(as arms in the cascade may not be ordered by their error-rates, it is possible that
\gamma_{i}<\gamma_{j}
), we have
\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{\prime}}+\frac{\beta}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{\beta+1}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}\leq\frac{3}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}
,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq=T\xi^{\prime}+\frac{3\alpha K\ln T}{{\xi^{\prime}}^{2}}.

Choose
\xi^{\prime}=\left(\frac{6\alpha K\ln T}{T}\right)^{1/3}
which maximize above upper bound and we get,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(6\alpha K\ln T\right)^{1/3}T^{2/3}+\frac{\left(6\alpha K\ln T\right)^{1/3}}{2}T^{2/3}

\displaystyle\implies{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(6\alpha K\ln T\right)^{1/3}T^{2/3}=O\left(\left(K\ln T\right)^{1/3}T^{2/3}\right)

It completes our proof for the case when any problem instance belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{WD}}
.

Now we consider any problem instance
\theta\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}
. For any
\theta\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{SD}}\Rightarrow\forall j\in[K],\;p_{ij}=\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}\implies\beta=0
(Setting
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y^{i}=Y,Y^{j}\neq Y\right\}=0
for
j>i
in Proposition 3 of
Hanawal et al. 2017
). We can rewrite
Eq.

2.55
as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq T\xi^{\prime}+\frac{\alpha K\ln T}{{\xi^{\prime}}}.

Choose
\xi^{\prime}=\left(\frac{\alpha K\ln T}{T}\right)^{1/2}
which maximize above upper bound and we get,

\displaystyle\implies{\mathfrak{R}_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\alpha KT\ln T\right)^{1/2}=O\left(\sqrt{KT\ln T}\right)

This complete proof for second part of Theorem
6
.
∎

Chapter 3
Contextual Unsupervised Sequential Selection

In this chapter, we study
Contextual
U
nsupervised
S
equential
S
election
(USS), a variant of the stochastic contextual bandits problem where the loss incurred by the arm cannot be inferred from the observed feedback. In our setup, arms are associated with fixed costs and are ordered, forming a cascade. In each round, a context is presented, and the learner selects the arms sequentially till some depth. The total cost incurred by stopping at an arm is the sum of fixed costs of arms selected and the stochastic loss associated with the arm. The learner’s goal is to learn a decision rule that maps contexts to arms with the goal of minimizing the total expected loss. The problem is challenging as we are faced with an unsupervised setting as the total loss cannot be estimated. Clearly, learning is feasible only if the optimal arm can be inferred (explicitly or implicitly) from the problem structure. We observe that learning is still possible when the problem instance satisfies the so-called ‘Contextual Weak Dominance’
(\mathrm{CWD})
property. Under
\mathrm{CWD}
, we propose an algorithm for the contextual USS problem and demonstrate that it has sub-linear regret. Experiments on synthetic and real datasets validate our algorithm.

3.1
Contextual USS

Industrial systems, such as those found in medical, airport security, and manufacturing, utilize a suite of tests or classifiers for monitoring patients, people, and products. Tests have costs with the more intrusive and informative ones resulting in higher monetary costs and higher latency. For this reason, they are often organized as a classifier cascade
(
Chen et al. 2012
;
Trapeznikov and Saligrama 2013
;
Wang et al. 2015
)
, so that new input is first probed by an inexpensive test and then a more expensive one. The goal of a cascaded system is to resolve easy to handle examples early so that the overall system maintains high accuracy at low average costs.

Over time, due to environmental changes or test calibrations, sequential testing protocols (STP) may no longer be accurate, resulting in higher costs. While one can leverage off-line methods such as supervised training of cascades
(
Wang et al. 2015
)
, they require new annotated data collection. In many scenarios, new data cannot be collected in-situ, and system shutdown is not an option. In the absence of annotated data, we face a dilemma. While we can observe test outcomes, we cannot ascertain their reliability due to the absence of ground truth, necessitating
unsupervised sequential selection (USS)
methods, where an arm represents a test/classifier. Recent works
(
Hanawal et al. 2017
;
Verma et al. 2019b
;
Verma et al. 2020a
)
propose methods for solving the USS problem; however, they focus exclusively on the non-contextual setting, which in essence requires inputs (people, objects, or products) to be homogeneous, and as such, these methods are unrealistic since contexts (high vs. low risk) can guide the arm selection.

In this context, we propose the contextual USS. In our setup, inputs arrive sequentially, and the learner observes a continuous-valued context as input. While the learner knows the cost of each arm, he does not know the associated stochastic loss. Furthermore, the learner does not benefit from feedback obtained by his arm selection, in contrast to the conventional contextual bandit works
(
Beygelzimer et al. 2011
)
. Thus, while being agnostic to the true loss, the learner must sequentially choose the arm that leads to the smallest total loss, where the total loss is the sum of the cost of using an arm and the mean loss associated with the arm. As such, our proposed problem is a special case of the stochastic partial monitoring problem with contextual inputs
(
Lattimore and Szepesvári 2020b
, Chapter 37)
.
Most of the prior work on partial monitoring problem is restricted to cases where observed feedback can identify the losses for selected actions. However, in many areas like crowd-sourcing
(
Bonald and Combes 2017
;
Kleindessner and Awasthi 2018
)
, resource allocation
(
Verma et al. 2019a
)
, medical diagnosis
(
Verma et al. 2020b
)
, and many others, feedback from actions may not even be sufficient to identify the losses.

While we draw upon several concepts introduced in earlier work
(
Hanawal et al. 2017
)
, there are additional challenges in the contextual case due to the unsupervised nature of the problem. First, unlike vanilla-USS, the loss here is context-dependent. We propose notions of contextual weak dominance as a means to relate observed disagreements to differences in losses between any two arms. We then propose a parameterized Generalized Linear Model (GLM) to model the context-conditional disagreement probability between any two arms and validate the model empirically.

A fundamental technical challenge is in the estimation of disagreement probabilities uniformly across all contexts in the finite time while ensuring sufficient exploration between different arm selection protocols, required for honing in on the optimal selection strategy. In particular, since contexts are continuous-valued, and because we have no control over inputs, the contextual observations, in the finite time, may not persistently span the whole space, and estimates are often unreliable. To this end, we adapt techniques from parameterized contextual bandits
(
Chu et al. 2011
;
Li et al. 2017
)
for our unsupervised setting. We propose an algorithm based on the principle of optimism, namely, the larger indexed arm in cascade is chosen when uncertain. We show that our algorithm navigates the exploration-exploitation tradeoffs in different ways and lead to sub-linear cumulative regret. We then validate it on several problem instances derived from synthetic and real datasets.

3.1.1
Related Work

Stochastic Contextual multi-armed Bandits (SCB):
In each round, the learner observes the context and decides which arm, among a finite number of arms, to apply
(
Beygelzimer et al. 2011
)
. By playing an arm, the learner observes a stochastic reward that depends on the context and the arm selected. The most commonly studied model assumes that each arm is parameterized, and the mean reward of an arm is the inner product of the context and an unknown parameter associated with the arm. Contextual bandits have been applied to problems ranging from online advertising
(
Li et al. 2010
;
Chu et al. 2011
)
and recommendations
(
Langford and Zhang 2008
)
to clinical trials
(
Woodroofe 1979
)
and mobile health
(
Tewari and Murphy 2017
)
.

Generalized linear models (GLM)
assume that the mean reward is a non-linear link function of the inner product between the context vector and the unknown parameter vector
(
Filippi et al. 2010
;
Li et al. 2017
)
. GLMs are also useful models for the classification problems where rewards, in the context of online learning problems, could be binary
(
Zhang et al. 2016b
;
Jun et al. 2017
)
. A more challenging non-parameterized version of the stochastic contextual bandits is studied in
(
Agarwal et al. 2014
)
.

Another framework that is closely related to SCB is
stochastic linear bandits (SLB)

(
Auer 2002
;
Dani et al. 2008
;
Rusmevichientong and
Tsitsiklis 2010
;
Abbasi-Yadkori et al. 2011
)
. In this setup, the environment is parameterized, and there could be uncountably many arms (within some bounded radius), also referred to as decision set. The arms are characterized into their feature vectors, and the mean reward for playing an arm is given as the inner product of the parameter (unknown) and the feature vector associated with the arm. In situations where the decision set is allowed to vary in each round and are finite, SLBs are equivalent to SCBs, where feature vectors correspond to context-arm pairs
(
Li et al. 2010
;
Li et al. 2017
)
.
For our work, we leverage GLMs as models for disagreement probability between any two arms. While it is tempting to reduce contextual USS to SCBs, note that, unlike prior works, we do not observe loss for our action choices, and so conventional algorithms such as LinUCB and UCB-GLM
(
Li et al. 2010
;
Agarwal et al. 2014
;
Li et al. 2017
)
cannot be applied.

Most of the prior work
(
Hanawal et al. 2017
;
Verma et al. 2019b
;
Verma et al. 2020a
)
considered the problem of learning an optimal action but ignored the contextual information. In this work, we incorporated contextual information, which is readily available in many applications. Exploiting the
real-valued
contextual information (features) for improving the arm selection strategy is non-trivial due to the unsupervised nature of the problem where the standard analysis of contextual bandits does not apply. We made necessary modeling assumptions to leverage GLMs to parameterize the disagreement probability between two arms and extended the existing definitions to address the new setup’s learnability issues. However, the problem still requires new ideas and analysis methods to derive an efficient algorithm, which poses new technical challenges for analysis.

3.2
Problem Setting

We consider a stochastic contextual bandits problem with
K
arms. The set of arms is denoted as
[K]
where
[K]\doteq\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
. In each round
t
, the environment generates a vector
\left(X_{t},Y_{t},\{Y_{t}^{i}\}_{i\in[K]}\right)
. The vector
X_{t}
denotes the context in round
t
and forms an independent and identically distributed (IID) sequence drawn from a bounded set
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
according to an unknown but fixed distribution
\nu
. The binary reward for context
X_{t}
is denoted by
Y_{t}\in\{0,1\}
, which is hidden from the learner. The vector
\left(\{Y_{t}^{i}\}_{i\in[K]}\right)\in\{0,1\}^{K}
represents observed feedback at time
t
, where
Y_{t}^{i}
denotes the feedback observed after playing arm
i
with
X_{t}
as input
1
1

1

In our setup, an arm
i
could be a classifier that outputs label
Y^{i}
. The classifier’s input could be a context and any combinations of feedback observed from classifiers coming before the arm
i
in the cascade. For example, consider a case where each arm represents a crowd-sourced worker. After using the first
i
crowd-sourced workers, the final label can be a function of predicted labels of the first
i
crowd-sourced workers.
. We denote the cost for using arm
i
as
c_{i}\geq 0
that is known and the same for all contexts.

In contextual USS, the arms are assumed to be ordered and form a cascade. When the learner selects an arm
i\in[K]
, the feedback from all arms till arm
i
in the cascade are observed. The expected loss of playing the arm
i
for a given context
x_{t}
is denoted as
\gamma_{i}(x_{t})\doteq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}|X=x_{t}\right\}}\right]=\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}|X=x_{t}\right\}
, where
\mathds{1}_{\left\{A\right\}}
denotes indicator of event
A
. For soundness, we assume that the probability density function of context distribution is strictly positive on
\mathcal{X}
such that the conditional probabilities are well defined. The total expected loss incurred by playing arm
i
for context
x_{t}
is defined as
\gamma_{i}(x_{t})+\lambda_{i}C_{i}
, where
C_{i}\doteq c_{1}+\ldots+c_{i}
and
\lambda_{i}
is a trade-off parameter that normalizes the incurred cost and the loss of playing arm
i
.

Fig.

3.1
depicts the USS setup.

Figure 3.1
:
Cascaded Contextual Unsupervised Sequential Selection (USS) setup. In round
t
,
x_{t}
is the the context generated by environment,
Y_{t}
is the hidden state associated with the context, and
Y_{t}^{1},Y_{t}^{2}\ldots Y_{t}^{K}
are feedback observed from the arms.
\gamma_{i}
denotes error rate of the arm
i
and
c_{i}
denotes the cost of using the arm
i
.

Since the true rewards are hidden from the learner, the expected loss of an arm cannot be inferred from the observed feedback. We thus have a version of the stochastic partial monitoring problem
(
Cesa-Bianchi et al. 2006
;
Bartók and
Szepesvári 2012a
;
Bartók et al. 2014
)
, and we refer to it as contextual
u
nsupervised
s
equential
s
election (USS). Let
\boldsymbol{Q}
be the unknown joint distribution of
(X,Y,Y^{1},Y^{2}\ldots,Y^{K})
. Henceforth we identify a contextual USS instance as
P\doteq(\boldsymbol{Q},\boldsymbol{c})
where
\boldsymbol{c}\doteq(c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{K})
is the known cost vector of arms. We denote the collection of contextual USS instances as
\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
. For instance
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
, the optimal arm for a context
x_{t}
is given as follows:

{i^{\star}_{t}}\in\max\left\{\arg\!\min_{i\in[K]}\left(\gamma_{i}(x_{t})+\lambda_{i}C_{i}\right)\right\},

(3.1)

where the choice of
{i^{\star}_{t}}
is risk-averse as we prefer the arm with the lowest error among the optimal arms.
The interaction between the environment and a learner is given in Algorithm
4
.

Algorithm 4

Learning on contextual USS instance
(\boldsymbol{Q},\boldsymbol{c})

For each round
t
:

1.

Environment
chooses a vector
(X_{t},Y_{t},\{Y_{t}^{i}\}_{i\in[K]})\sim\boldsymbol{Q}
.

2.

Learner
observes a context
X_{t}=x_{t}
and selects an arm
I_{t}\in[K]
to stop in cascade.

3.

Feedback and Loss:
The learner observes feedback
(Y_{t}^{1},Y_{t}^{2},\ldots,Y_{t}^{I_{t}})
and incurs a total loss
\mathds{1}_{\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}|X=x_{t}\right\}}+\lambda_{I_{t}}C_{I_{t}}
.

The learner’s goal is to find an arm for each context such that the cumulative expected loss is minimized. Specifically, for
T
contexts, we measure the performance of a policy that selects an arm
I_{t}
for a context
x_{t}
in terms of regret given by

\mathfrak{R}_{T}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\gamma_{I_{t}}(x_{t})+\lambda_{I_{t}}C_{I_{t}}-\left(\gamma_{i^{\star}_{t}}(x_{t})+\lambda_{i^{\star}_{t}}C_{i^{\star}_{t}}\right)\right).

(3.2)

We seek policies that yield sub-linear regret, i.e.,
\mathfrak{R}_{T}/T\rightarrow 0
as
T\rightarrow\infty
. It implies that the learner collects almost as much reward in the long run as an oracle collects that knew the optimal arm for every context. We say that a problem instance
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
is learnable if there exists a policy such that
\lim\limits_{T\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{R}_{T}/T=0
.

In the sequel, we discuss the selection criteria for optimal arm for a given context and the conditions under which instances of
\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
are learnable.

3.2.1
Contextual Weak Dominance

Next, we introduce the contextual weak dominance property of a problem instance.

Definition 8

(Contextual Weak Dominance
(\mathrm{CWD})
)
.

Let
{i^{\star}_{t}}
denote optimal arm for context
x_{t}
. Then the context
x_{t}
is said to satisfy weak dominance
(\mathrm{WD})
property if

\forall j>{i^{\star}_{t}}:C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}_{t}}>\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i^{\star}_{t}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}.

(3.3)

A problem instance
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
is said to satisfy the
\mathrm{CWD}
property if all contexts of
P
satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
property. We denote the set of all instances in
\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
that satisfies
\mathrm{CWD}
property by
\mathcal{P}_{\text{CWD}}
.

In the following, we use an alternative characterization of the
\mathrm{CWD}
property, given as

\xi(x_{t})\doteq\min_{j>{i^{\star}_{t}}}\left\{C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}_{t}}-\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i^{\star}_{t}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}\right\}>0.

(3.4)

We define
\xi\doteq\inf_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\xi(x)
and assume that
\xi>0
. The larger the value of
\xi
, ‘stronger’ is the
\mathrm{CWD}
property, and easier it is to identify an optimal arm for given contexts. We later characterize the regret upper bounds of proposed algorithms in terms of
\xi
. We also discuss the case when a fraction of contexts satisfies
\mathrm{WD}
property in
Section

3.6
.

3.2.2
Selection Criteria for Optimal Arm

Without loss of generality, we set
\lambda_{i}=1
for all
i\in[K]
as their value can be absorbed into the costs. Since
{i^{\star}_{t}}=\max\big\{\arg\min\limits_{i\in[K]}\left(\gamma_{i}(x_{t})+C_{i}\right)\big\}
, it must satisfy following equation:

\displaystyle\forall j<{i^{\star}_{t}}\,:\,C_{i^{\star}_{t}}-C_{j}\leq\gamma_{j}(x_{t})-\gamma_{i^{\star}_{t}}(x_{t})\,,

(3.5a)

\displaystyle\forall j>{i^{\star}_{t}}\,:\,C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}_{t}}>\gamma_{i^{\star}_{t}}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t})\,.

(3.5b)

As the loss of an arm is not observed, the above equations can not lead to a sound arm selection criteria. We thus have to relate the unobservable quantities in terms of the quantities that can be observed. In our setup, we can compare the feedback of two arms, which can be used to estimate the disagreement probabilities between them. For notation convenience, we define
p_{ij}^{(t)}\doteq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}
for
i<j
. The value of
p_{ij}^{(t)}
can be estimated as it is observable.
Our next result bounds unobserved error rates differences in terms of their observable disagreement probabilities for a given context.

Lemma 14
.

For any
i
,
j
, and
x_{t}\in\mathcal{X}
,
\gamma_{i}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t})=p_{ij}^{(t)}-2\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t},Y_{t}^{j}\neq Y_{t}|X=x_{t}\right\}
.

The detailed proof of
14
and all other missing proofs appear in
Section

3.6
.

Now, using
14
, we can replace
Eq.

3.5a
by

\forall j<{i^{\star}_{t}}\,:\,C_{{i^{\star}_{t}}}-C_{j}\leq p_{ji^{\star}_{t}}^{(t)},

(3.6)

which only has observable quantities.
For
j>{i^{\star}_{t}}
, using the
\mathrm{CWD}
property, we replace
Eq.

3.5b
by

\forall j>{i^{\star}_{t}}\,:\,C_{j}-C_{{i^{\star}_{t}}}>p_{i^{\star}_{t}j}^{(t)}.

(3.7)

Using
Eq.

3.6
and
Eq.

3.7
, our next result gives the optimal arm for a given context
x_{t}
.

Lemma 15
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{CWD}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{t}=\left\{i:\forall j>i,C_{j}-C_{i}>p_{ij}^{(t)}\right\}\cup\{K\}
. Then the arm
I_{t}=\min(\mathcal{B}_{t})
is the optimal arm for a context
x_{t}
.

By construction, the optimal arm lies in set
\mathcal{B}_{t}
. Because of
Eq.

3.7
, any sub-optimal arm having smaller index than optimal arm do not satisfy
Eq.

3.7
, hence it can not be in set
\mathcal{B}_{t}
. Therefore, the smallest arm of set
\mathcal{B}_{t}
is the optimal arm.

Theorem 8
.

The set
\mathcal{P}_{\text{CWD}}
is maximal learnable.

The proof establishes that under the
\mathrm{CWD}
property, there exists a ‘sound’ arm selection policy that identifies the optimal arm for each context. The sound policy only uses conditional disagreement probabilities between pairs of arms that can be estimated from the feedback of arms.

3.3
Parameterization of Pairwise Disagreement Probability

Since the number of contexts could be much larger (can be infinite) than the learning horizon, in stochastic contextual bandits, a correlation structure is assumed between the reward (loss) and the contexts
(
Auer 2002
;
Li et al. 2010
;
Li et al. 2017
)
. It is often realized via parameterization of the arms such that expected rewards (or losses) observed from an arm depend on the unknown parameter. In our setting, we cannot observe a loss for any arm. Hence parameterization of an expected loss of the arms is not useful. However, we can obtain feedback of two arms for a given context and can compare them. For example, we can check whether two arms’ feedback agrees or disagrees for a given context. Thus, we assume a correlation structure on the disagreement probability for a pair of arms across the contexts and parameterize it using generalized linear models. For
i<j
and context
x_{t}
, the disagreement probability for
(i,j)
pair of arms is given via a function
\mu
as follows:

\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}=\mu(\Phi_{ij}(x_{t})^{\top}\theta_{ij}^{\star}),

(3.8)

where
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\Phi_{ij}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
is a feature map for some
d^{\prime}\geq d
,
2
2

2

Let
\mathbb{R}^{d_{ij}}
be the space where
Eq.

3.8
holds for
(i,j)
pair of arms, and
\Phi_{ij}
is the feature map that lift
x_{t}
from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
space to
\mathbb{R}^{d_{ij}}
space. For simplicity, we take
d^{\prime}=\max_{\forall i<j\leq K}d_{ij}
.
and
\theta_{ij}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
is the unknown parameter for
(i,j)
pair.

We assume the following assumptions on context distribution
\nu
and function
\mu
, which is standard in the GLM bandit literature
(
Filippi et al. 2010
;
Li et al. 2017
)
:

Assumption 1

(GLM)
.

•

For all
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
(i,j)
pairs,
\left\|\Phi_{ij}(x)\right\|_{2}\leq 1
.

•

\kappa\doteq\inf_{\left\|x\right\|_{2}\leq 1,\left\|\theta-\theta_{ij}^{\star}\right\|_{2}\leq 1}

\dot{\mu}(\Phi_{ij}(x)^{\top}\theta)>0
for all
(i,j)
pairs.

•

There exists a constant
\lambda_{\Sigma}>0
such that
\lambda_{min}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi_{ij}(X)\Phi_{ij}(X)^{\top}\right]\right)\geq\lambda_{\Sigma}
for all
(i,j)
pairs.

•

The function
\mu:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,1]
is continuously differentiable and Lipschitz with constant
k_{\mu}
.

For our setting, the function
\mu
is defined as
\mu(z)={1}/{(1+\mathrm{e}^{-z})}
, which is the logistic function. The logistic function is widely used function for binary classification model and has
k_{\mu}\leq 1/4
.

In contextual USS setup, we can compare the arms’ feedback and check whether they agree or not for a given context. These binary observations (agree or disagree) can be treated as noisy samples of the disagreement probability. The noise in the binary observation obtained by comparing the feedback of
(i,j)
pair of arms in round
t
, is given by

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{ij}^{(t)}=\begin{cases}1-\mu(\Phi_{ij}(x_{t})^{\top}\theta_{ij}^{\star}),&\text{with probability }\mu(\Phi_{ij}(x_{t})^{\top}\theta_{ij}^{\star})\\
-\mu(\Phi_{ij}(x_{t})^{\top}\theta_{ij}^{\star}),&\text{with probability }\left(1-\mu(\Phi_{ij}(x_{t})^{\top}\theta_{ij}^{\star})\right)\end{cases}

where
\varepsilon_{ij}^{(t)}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable with
\mathbb{E}\left[\varepsilon_{ij}^{(t)}|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]=0
. Here
\mathcal{F}_{t}
denotes sigma algebra generated by history
\left\{\left(X_{s},I_{s},\left\{Y_{s}^{i}\right\}_{i\in[I_{s}]}\right)\right\}_{s\in[t]}
till time
t
. Since
\varepsilon_{ij}^{(t)}
is a zero-mean shifted Bernoulli random variable,
\varepsilon_{ij}^{(t)}
satisfies the following sub-Gaussian condition with parameter
\sigma\in(0,1)
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(\lambda\varepsilon_{ij}^{(t)})|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}\right),\hskip 5.69054pt\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}.

Let
d_{ij}(t)\doteq\mathds{1}_{\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}}
be the disagreement indicator for a context
x_{t}
and
S_{ij}^{t}
be the set of indices of contexts for which disagreements are observed for
(i,j)
pair of arms till round
t
. In round
t
, we estimate
\theta_{ij}^{\star}
, denoted by
\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}
, using the following equation adapted from the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) used for GLM bandits
(
Li et al. 2017
)
:

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in S_{ij}^{t}}

\displaystyle\left(d_{ij}(s)-\mu(\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})^{\top}\theta)\right)\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})=0.

(3.9)

In the next section, we develop an algorithm that exploits
15
for selecting the optimal arm to each context. It replaces the terms
p_{ij}^{(t)}
in
15
by their optimistic estimates.

3.4
Algorithm for Contextual USS:
USS-PD

Our algorithm, named
USS-PD
, is based on the
optimism-in-the-face-of-uncertainty
(OFU) principle.
USS-PD
works as follows: it takes
\delta
and
m
as inputs, where
\delta
is the confidence in the estimated parameters and used for computing confidence bound for
\theta_{ij}^{\star}
as given by
17
. The choice of
m
ensures that with probability at least
(1-\delta)
, the sample correlation matrix
V_{ij}^{t}=\sum_{s\in S_{ij}^{t}}\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})^{\top}
for each
(i,j)
pair where
i<j
, is invertible. A high probability upper bound on
m
is computed using
16
. The algorithm collects feedback from all arms by selecting the arm
K
irrespective of the context received for first
m
rounds. After
m
rounds, the sample correlation matrix and the estimate of
\theta_{ij}^{\star}
are computed for each
(i,j)
pair.

For
t>m
, the learner receives a context
x_{t}
and plays the arm
i=1
and then observe its feedback. For each
(i,j)
pair and context
x_{t}
, the upper bound on disagreement probability
\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(t)}
is computed using
\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}
and confidence bonus
\alpha_{ij}^{t}\left\|\Phi_{ij}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{ij}^{t})^{-1}}
. Here the notation
\left\|x\right\|^{2}_{A}\doteq x^{\top}Ax
denotes the weighted
l_{2}
-norm of vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with respect to a positive definite matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. The confidence bonus has two terms. The first term
\alpha_{ij}^{t}
is a slowly increasing function in
t
whose value is specified in Lemma
17
, and the second term
\left\|\Phi_{ij}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{ij}^{t})^{-1}}
decreases to zero as
t
increases.

USS-PD

Algorithm for Contextual USS using Pairwise Disagreement

1:

Input:
Tuning parameters:
\delta\in(0,1)
and
m>0

2:

Select arm
K
for first
m
contexts

3:

\forall i<j\leq K:
set
{V}_{ij}^{m}\leftarrow\sum_{t=1}^{m}\Phi_{ij}(x_{t}){\Phi_{ij}(x_{t})}^{\top}
and update
\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{m}
by solving
Eq.

3.9

4:

for

t=m+1,m+2,\ldots

do

5:

Receive context
x_{t}
. Set
i=1
and
I_{t}=0

6:

while

I_{t}=0

do

7:

Play arm
i

8:

\forall j\in[i+1,K]:
compute
\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(t)}\leftarrow\mu\left(\Phi_{ij}(x_{t})^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t-1}+\alpha_{ij}^{t-1}\left\|\Phi_{ij}(x_{t})\right\|_{\left({V}_{ij}^{t-1}\right)^{-1}}\right)

9:

If
\forall j\in[i+1,K]:C_{j}-C_{i}>\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(t)}
or
i=K
then set
I_{t}=i
else set
i=i+1

10:

end

while

11:

Select arm
I_{t}
and observe
Y_{t}^{1},Y_{t}^{2},\dots,Y_{t}^{I_{t}}

12:

\forall i<j\leq I_{t}:
update
V_{ij}^{t}\leftarrow{V}_{ij}^{t-1}+\Phi_{ij}(x_{t}){\Phi_{ij}(x_{t})}^{\top}
and
\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}
by solving
Eq.

3.9

13:

end

for

After computing
\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(t)}
, the algorithm checks whether the arm
i
is the best arm using
Eq.

3.7
with
\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(t)}
in place of
p_{ij}^{(t)}
. If the arm
i
is not the best, then the algorithm plays the next arm, and then the same process is repeated. If the arm
i
is the best arm for context, then the algorithm stops at that arm with
I_{t}=i
for that context. After selecting arm
I_{t}
, the feedback from arms
1,\ldots,I_{t}
are observed. After that, the values of
V^{t}_{ij}
are updated, and
\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}
are re-estimated. The same process is repeated for subsequent contexts.

Remark 5
.

GLM bandits are well studied but require reward or loss information. In the USS setup, loss of selected arm can not be observed; hence finding the optimal arm is challenging. Due to binary disagreement,
USS-PD
uses the MLE estimator for
\theta_{ij}^{\star}
as used in GLM bandits
(
Filippi et al. 2010
;
Li et al. 2017
)
. However, the feedback structure and the way arms are selected in the USS setup differ from that in the GLM bandits. Further, our analysis needs carefully connecting the regret with the bad events that make
USS-PD
selects non-optimal arms.

Remark 6
.

We force the algorithm to explore until the correlation matrix
V_{ij}^{t}
is invertible for all
(i,j)
pairs. The invertibility can also be ensured by adding a regularization term
(
Abbasi-Yadkori et al. 2011
;
Zhang et al. 2016b
;
Jun et al. 2017
)
to avoid forced exploration. However, the analysis of
USS-PD
with regularization term still required to the non-regularized part of the sample correlation matrix becomes invertible. See
Section

3.6
for the algorithm and its analysis.

3.4.1
Regret Analysis of
USS-PD

The following definition is useful in our regret analysis.

Definition 9

(Arm Preference (
\succ_{t}
))
.

USS-PD
prefers an arm
i
over
j
for context
x_{t}
if

\displaystyle i\succ_{t}j\doteq

\displaystyle C_{i}-C_{j}<\tilde{p}_{ji}^{(t)},

if
j<i

(3.10a)

\displaystyle i\succ_{t}j\doteq

\displaystyle C_{j}-C_{i}>\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(t)},

if
j>i
.

(3.10b)

Our next result bounds the number of disagreement observations required from a pair of arms say
(i,j)
, such that the smallest eigenvalue of its sample correlation matrix
V_{ij}
matrices is larger than a fixed value. This result uses the standard results from random matrix theory
(
Vershynin 2012
)
.

Lemma 16
.

Let
{V}_{ij}^{t}=\sum_{s\in S_{ij}^{t}}\Phi_{ij}(x_{s}){\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})}^{\top}
,
\Sigma_{ij}=\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi_{ij}(X)\Phi_{ij}(X)^{\top}\right]
,
\Psi
and
\delta\in(0,1)
be two positive constants. Then, there exist positive universal constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that the minimum eigenvalue of
\lambda_{min}({V}_{ij}^{t})\geq\Psi
with probability at least
1-2\delta/K^{2}
, iff

|S_{ij}^{t}|\geq\left(\frac{C_{1}\sqrt{d^{\prime}}+C_{2}\sqrt{\log(K^{2}/2\delta)}}{\lambda_{min}(\Sigma_{ij})}\right)^{2}+\frac{2\Psi}{\lambda_{min}(\Sigma_{ij})}.

The next result is adapted to our setting from the confidence bounds for maximum likelihood estimator used in GLM bandits
(
Li et al. 2017
)
.

Lemma 17

(Confidence Ellipsoid)
.

Let
m
be such that
\lambda_{min}(V_{ij}^{m+1})\geq 1
for any pair
(i,j)
. Then the following event holds with probability at least
1-2\delta/K^{2}
for
USS-PD
:

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}-\theta_{ij}^{\star}\right\|_{V_{ij}^{t}}\leq\alpha_{ij}^{t},\;\forall t>m

where
\alpha_{ij}^{t}=\frac{2\sigma}{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{d^{\prime}}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{2t}{d^{\prime}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{K^{2}}{2\delta}\right)}
.

The regret analysis of GLM bandits hinges on bounding the instantaneous regret in each round, which is tied to the estimation error of the GLM parameters. Due to the unsupervised setting and cascade structure, this way of bounding regret does not work in our setup. Our analysis goes by bounding the number of pulls of the sub-optimal arms. However, unlike standard bandits, we have to distinguish whether the sub-optimal arm pulled by
USS-PD
is on the ‘left’ or ‘right’ of the optimal arm in the cascade. It requires our analysis to handle both the cases carefully. Since
USS-PD
uses a similar MLE estimator for parameter estimation as in GLM bandits, we only adapt their asymptotic normality results. Our next results give conditions when
USS-PD
prefers a sub-optimal arm for a context.

Lemma 18
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{CWD}}
. Then
USS-PD
prefers any sub-optimal arm
l<i^{\star}_{t}
for context
x_{t}
with probability at most
\delta/2
.

Lemma 19
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{CWD}}
. If
USS-PD
prefers a sub-optimal arm
h>i^{\star}_{t}
for context
x_{t}
then

2k_{\mu}\alpha_{i^{\star}_{t}h}^{t}>\xi_{i^{\star}_{t}h}(x_{t})\sqrt{\lambda_{min}(V_{i^{\star}_{t}h}^{t})}.

where
\xi_{{i^{\star}_{t}}h}=C_{h}-C_{i^{\star}_{t}}-p_{i^{\star}_{t}h}^{(t)}
and
\alpha_{ij}^{t}
is given by
17
.

Let
m\doteq C\lambda_{\Sigma}^{-2}\left(d^{\prime}+\log(k^{2}/2\delta)\right)+2\lambda_{\Sigma}^{-1}
, where
C>0
is the universal constant and
R_{max}\doteq\max_{i\in[K],x\in\mathcal{X}}

\left[C_{i}+\gamma_{i}(x)-\left(C_{i^{\star}}+\gamma_{i^{\star}}(x)\right)\right]
, where
i^{\star}
is the optimal arm for context
x
.
Now we state the regret upper bound of
USS-PD
.

Theorem 9

(Regret Upper Bound)
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{CWD}}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, Assumption 1 holds, and
\xi_{h}=\min\limits_{t\geq 1}\xi_{i^{\star}_{t}h}(x_{t})
. Then with probability at least
1-2\delta
, the regret of
USS-PD
for
T>m
contexts is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq R_{max}\Bigg[m+\sum_{h=2}^{K}\Bigg(\hskip-2.84526pt\Bigg(\frac{C_{1}\sqrt{d^{\prime}}+C_{2}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{K^{2}}{2\delta}\right)}}{\lambda_{\Sigma}}\Bigg)^{2}\hskip-5.69054pt+\frac{16}{\lambda_{\Sigma}}

\displaystyle\qquad\left(\frac{k_{\mu}\sigma}{\xi_{h}\kappa}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{d^{\prime}}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{2T}{d^{\prime}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{K^{2}}{2\delta}\right)\right)\Bigg)\Bigg].

Corollary 6
.

Let technical conditions stated in
9
hold. Then with probability at least
1-2\delta

\mathfrak{R}_{T}\leq O\left({Kd^{\prime}\log(T)}/{\xi^{2}}\right).

The regret of
USS-PD
for instance
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{CWD}}
is logarithmic in
T
