and grows linearly with
d^{\prime}
and
K
. The regret is inversely dependent on the value of
\xi\doteq\min\limits_{h\geq 2}\xi_{h}
(measure how well
\mathrm{CWD}
holds), which implies the problem instance with smaller
\xi
has more regret and vice-versa. The value of
\xi
is analogous to the minimum sub-optimality gap in the standard Multi-Armed Bandits setting. With a large context set,
\xi
can be small, and its inverse relation in the regret captures the difficulty of the USS problem.

3.5
Experiment

We evaluate the performance of
USS-PD
on different problem instances derived from synthetic and real datasets. In our experiments, the data samples are treated as contexts. The labels of contexts are known but are never revealed to the algorithm. We use the labels to train classifiers offline that act as arms. Arm
i
represents a logistic classifier with trained parameter
\theta_{i}
. A context (data sample)
x
is assigned label
1
from the
i
-th classifier with probability
\mu(x^{\top}\theta_{i})
and label
0
with probability
1-\mu(x^{\top}\theta_{i})
. The disagreement labels for
(i,j)
pair is computed using the labels of classifier
i
and
j
. To satisfy
Eq.

3.8
, we use the polynomial kernel of degree two for mapping context into higher-dimensional space. Unlike other kernels, the polynomial kernel uses a well-defined feature map to lift the contexts into fixed, higher-dimensional space.
The details of the used problem instances are as follows.

Synthetic Dataset:
We consider
3
-dimensional synthetic dataset with
5000
data samples. Each sample is represented by
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
, where the value of
x_{j}
is drawn uniformly at random from
(-1,1)
. A sample
x
is labeled
0
if the value of
(x_{1}+x_{1}x_{2}+x_{3}^{2})
is negative otherwise it is labeled
1
. We train five logistic classifiers on this synthetic dataset by varying the regularization parameter. We then assign a positive cost to each classifier and order them by their increasing cost. We vary the cost of using classifiers to get different problem instances (see details in
Section

3.6
).

Real Datasets:
We applied our algorithm on PIMA Indian Diabetes
(
Kaggle 2016
)
dataset. Each sample has
8
features related to the conditions of the patient. We split the features into three subsets and train a logistic classifier on each subset. We associate 1st classifier with the first
6
features as input. These features include patient history/profile. The 2nd classifier, in addition to the
6
features, utilizes the feature on the glucose tolerance test, and the 3rd classifier uses all the previous features and the feature that gives values of insulin test. Due to space constraints, the experiment results on Heart Disease dataset
(
Detrano 1998
;
Dheeru and Karra Taniskidou 2017
)
are given in
Section

3.6
.

3.5.1
Experiments Results

We compare the performance of
USS-PD
on four problem instances derived from the synthetic dataset. The instances vary based on the cost of arms. All contexts in Instance
1
do not satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
property; hence it suffers linear regret as shown in
Fig.

3.2(a)
. For the remaining instances, we set costs such that the value of
\xi
increasing from Instance
2
to
4
. As expected, the regret decreases from Instance
2
to
4
, as seen in
Fig.

3.2(a)
. We also compare
USS-PD
against an algorithm where the learner receives true labels as feedback. In particular, the learner knows whether the classifier’s output is correct or not and can estimate their error rates. We implement this ‘supervised’ setting by replacing disagreement probability in
Eq.

3.7
with estimated error rates. As expected, the regret with supervision has lower than the
USS-PD
regret (unsupervised) in
Fig.

3.2(b)
. It is qualitatively interesting because these plots demonstrate that, in typical cases, our unsupervised algorithm can eventually learn to perform as good as an algorithm with knowledge of true labels.

(a)

Synthetic dataset: Regret for different instance

(b)

Supervised Setting (PI 3 of Synthetic Dataset)

(c)

Total cost for PIMA Indian Diabetes dataset

Figure 3.2
:
Performance of
USS-PD
on different problem instances (PIs) derived from synthetic and real datasets.

We derive three problem instances from PIMA Indian Diabetes dataset by varying the costs of using classifies. Since all contexts of these problem instances do not satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
property (see details in
Section

3.6
), we used cumulative total expected cost as a performance measure, where the cumulative total expected cost is given by
\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\gamma_{I_{t}}(x_{t})+C_{I_{t}})
. We compare the performance of
USS-PD
with three baseline policies – the first baseline policy uses the third classifier irrespective of contexts, and it is denoted as policy ‘
I_{t}=3
’ (plays arm
3
in each round). The second baseline policy uses the second classifier for all contexts, and it is denoted as policy ‘
I_{t}=2
’. The third baseline policy is ‘Random,’ which selects an arm uniformly at random in each round. In all three problem instances, we observe that
USS-PD
performs better than the baselines, as shown in
Fig.

3.2(c)
.

We repeat each of the above experiments
100
times, and then the average regret is presented with a
95
% confidence interval. The vertical line on each plot shows the confidence interval.

Proof.

Using definition of
\gamma_{i}(x_{t})\doteq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}|X=x_{t}\right\}
, we get

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}|X=x_{t}\right\}-\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{j}\neq Y_{t}|X=x_{t}\right\}.

As the observed feedback is binary, if
Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t}^{j}
and
Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}
then
Y_{t}^{j}\neq Y_{t}
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t})

\displaystyle=\cancel{\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t},Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}}+\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t},Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle-\cancel{\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{j}\neq Y_{t},Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}}-\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{j}\neq Y_{t},Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}.

Adding and subtracting
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t},Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t},Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t},Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{j}\neq Y_{t},Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}-\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t},Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}.

If
Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}
and
Y_{t}^{j}\neq Y_{t}
then
Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t}
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}-\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t},Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t},Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}-2\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t},Y_{t}^{j}\neq Y_{t}|X=x_{t}\right\}.

\displaystyle\implies\gamma_{i}(x_{t}^{i})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t}^{j})

\displaystyle=p_{ij}^{(t)}-2\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}=Y_{t},Y_{t}^{j}\neq Y_{t}|X=x_{t}\right\}.

∎

See

Proof.

Let
i_{t}^{\star}
be an optimal arm for a context
x_{t}
. As
p_{ij}^{(t)}=\mathbb{P}\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\}
and
i_{t}^{\star}
is an optimal arm, we have
\forall j<i_{t}^{\star}:\,C_{i_{t}^{\star}}-C_{j}\leq\mathbb{P}\{Y_{t}^{i_{t}^{\star}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\}\implies C_{i_{t}^{\star}}-C_{j}\ngtr\mathbb{P}\{Y_{t}^{i_{t}^{\star}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\}\implies\forall j<i_{t}^{\star}\notin\mathcal{B}_{t}
. If any sub-optimal arm
h\in\mathcal{B}_{t}
then
h>i_{t}^{\star}
i.e.,

\mathcal{B}_{t}=\{i_{t}^{\star},h_{1},\ldots,h_{n},K\},

where
i_{t}^{\star}<h_{1}<\cdots<h_{n}<K
. By construction of set
\mathcal{B}_{t}
, the minimum indexed arm in set
\mathcal{B}_{t}
is only the optimal arm.
∎

We need the following results to proof of
8
.

Lemma 20
.

Let
i<j
and
x_{t}\in\mathcal{X}
be any context. Assume

C_{j}-C_{i}\notin\left(\gamma_{i}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t}),\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}\right].

(3.11)

Then,
C_{j}-C_{i}>\gamma_{i}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t})
iff
C_{j}-C_{i}>\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}
.

Proof.

Assume that
C_{j}-C_{i}>\gamma_{i}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t})
. As
C_{j}-C_{i}\notin\Big(\gamma_{i}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t}),

\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}\Big]
, we get
C_{j}-C_{i}>\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}
.
The proof of other direction follows by noting that
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}\geq\gamma_{i}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t})
.
∎

Lemma 21
.

Let
i>j
and
x_{t}\in\mathcal{X}
be any context. Assume

C_{i}-C_{j}\notin\left(\gamma_{j}(x_{t})-\gamma_{i}(x_{t}),\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}\right].

(3.12)

Then,
C_{i}-C_{j}\leq\gamma_{j}(x_{t})-\gamma_{i}(x_{t})
iff
C_{j}-C_{i}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}
.

Proof.

Let
C_{i}-C_{j}\leq\gamma_{j}(x_{t})-\gamma_{i}(x_{t})
. As
\gamma_{j}(x_{t})-\gamma_{i}(x_{t})\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}
, we get
C_{i}-C_{j}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}
.

The condition
C_{i}-C_{j}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}
along with
C_{i}-C_{j}\notin\Big(\gamma_{j}(x_{t})-\gamma_{i}(x_{t}),

\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}\Big]
implies the other direction, i.e.,
C_{i}-C_{j}\leq\gamma_{j}(x_{t})-\gamma_{i}(x_{t})
.
∎

Lemma 22
.

Let
{i^{\star}_{t}}
be an optimal arm for a context
x_{t}
. Any problem instance
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
is learnable if for every context in
P
following holds:

\forall j>{i^{\star}_{t}},\;C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}_{t}}>\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i^{\star}_{t}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}.

The proof of
22
follows from
20
and
21
.
Now we give proof for Theorem
8
.

See
8

Proof.

Let
{i^{\star}_{t}}
be an optimal arm for a context
x_{t}
. It is enough to prove that any problem instance
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{USS}}
is learnable if

\displaystyle\forall j>{i^{\star}_{t}},\;C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}_{t}}>\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i^{\star}_{t}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}.\hskip 11.38109pt\text{(definition of $\mathrm{CWD}$ property)}

From
20
and
21
, if the optimal arm satisfies following conditions,

\displaystyle\forall j>{i^{\star}_{t}},C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}_{t}}\notin\left(\gamma_{i^{\star}_{t}}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t}),\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i^{\star}_{t}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}\right]\text{ and}

\displaystyle\forall j<{i^{\star}_{t}},C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}\notin\left(\gamma_{j}(x_{t})-\gamma_{i^{\star}_{t}}(x_{t}),\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i^{\star}_{t}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}\right],

then, for
j>{i^{\star}_{t}},C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}_{t}}>\gamma_{i^{\star}_{t}}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x)
iff
C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}_{t}}>\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i^{\star}_{t}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}
and for
j<{i^{\star}_{t}},C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}\leq\gamma_{j}(x)-\gamma_{i^{\star}_{t}}(x_{t})
iff
C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}_{t}}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i^{\star}_{t}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}
. Hence we can use
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}
as a proxy for
\gamma_{i^{\star}_{t}}(x)-\gamma_{j}(x)
to make decision about the optimal arm.
Now notice that for
j<{i^{\star}_{t}}
,
C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}\leq\gamma_{j}(x)-\gamma_{i^{\star}_{t}}(x_{t})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\forall j<{i^{\star}_{t}},C_{i^{\star}}-C_{j}\notin\left(\gamma_{j}(x_{t})-\gamma_{i^{\star}_{t}}(x_{t}),\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i^{\star}_{t}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}\right]\text{ and}

\displaystyle\forall j>{i^{\star}_{t}},C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}_{t}}\notin\left(\gamma_{i^{\star}_{t}}(x_{t})-\gamma_{j}(x_{t}),\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i^{\star}_{t}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}\right]

(3.13)

are sufficient for learnability. Note that
Eq.

3.13
is equivalent to

\forall j>{i^{\star}_{t}},\;C_{j}-C_{i^{\star}_{t}}>\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i^{\star}_{t}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}.

(3.14)

Note that if
Eq.

3.14
does not hold, then knowing
\mathbb{P}\left\{Y_{t}^{i^{\star}_{t}}\neq Y_{t}^{j}|X=x_{t}\right\}
is not sufficient for finding the optimal arm.
∎

Regret decomposition when contexts satisfy Weak Dominance property with some known probability

Without knowing the disagreement probability, it is impossible to check whether a context satisfies
\mathrm{WD}
property or not. Hence we consider a case where a context can satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
property with some fixed probability. For such cases, we can decompose the regret into two parts: regret due to the contexts that satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
property and regret due to the contexts that do not satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
property. Note that the regret can be linear due to the contexts that do not satisfy the
\mathrm{WD}
condition.
Our next result gives the upper bound on the regret where the contexts satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
property with a known fixed probability.

Lemma 23
.

Let
\rho
be the probability of context that it does not satisfy the
\mathrm{WD}
property and
R_{max}
be the maximum regret incurred for any context. If
\mathfrak{R}_{T}
is the regret incurred when all contexts satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
property then, the regret incurred when contexts satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
with probability
(1-\rho)
is given by

\mathfrak{R}_{T}^{\prime}\leq(1-\rho)\mathfrak{R}_{T}+\rho R_{max}T.

Proof.

Let
\rho
be the probability of context that it does not satisfy the
\mathrm{WD}
property and
r_{t}(I_{t},i^{\star}_{t})
be the regret incurred for selecting sub-optimal arm
I_{t}
for the context
x_{t}
. Then the regret can be decomposed into two parts as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{T}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[\mathds{1}_{\left\{x_{t}\mbox{ satisfies }\mathrm{WD}\right\}}r_{t}(I_{t},i^{\star}_{t})+\mathds{1}_{\left\{x_{t}\mbox{ does not satisfy }\mathrm{WD}\right\}}r_{t}(I_{t},i^{\star}_{t})\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}_{\left\{x_{t}\mbox{ satisfies }\mathrm{WD}\right\}}r_{t}(I_{t},i^{\star}_{t})\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}_{\left\{x_{t}\mbox{ does not satisfy }\mathrm{WD}\right\}}r_{t}(I_{t},i^{\star}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{x_{t}\mbox{ satisfies }\mathrm{WD}\right\}r_{t}(I_{t},i^{\star}_{t})+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{x_{t}\mbox{ does not satisfy }\mathrm{WD}\right\}r_{t}(I_{t},i^{\star}_{t}).

(3.15)

First, we will bound the regret due to the contexts that do not satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
property (second term of
Eq.

3.15
). Note that the context that does not satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
property, the learner can not make the correct decision hence always incurs regret. Since the maximum regret is upper bounded by
R_{max}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{x_{t}\mbox{ does not satisfy }\mathrm{WD}\right\}r_{t}(I_{t},i^{\star}_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{x_{t}\mbox{ does not satisfy }\mathrm{WD}\right\}R_{max}

Since
\rho
is the probability of context that it does not satisfy the
\mathrm{WD}
property, we get

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\rho R_{max}

\displaystyle\implies\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{x_{t}\mbox{ does not satisfy }\mathrm{WD}\right\}r_{t}(I_{t},i^{\star}_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\rho R_{max}T.

(3.16)

Now we will bound the regret due to the contexts which satisfy
\mathrm{WD}
property (first term in
Eq.

3.15
). Since any context satisfies
\mathrm{WD}
with
1-\rho
probability, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left\{x_{t}\mbox{ satisfies }\mathrm{WD}\right\}r_{t}(I_{t},i^{\star}_{t})=\sum_{t=1}^{T}(1-\rho)r_{t}(I_{t},i^{\star}_{t})=(1-\rho)\sum_{t=1}^{T}r_{t}(I_{t},i^{\star}_{t}).

(3.17)

By assuming that all contexts are satisfying
\mathrm{WD}
property, we have regret
\mathfrak{R}_{T}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}r_{t}(I_{t},i^{\star}_{t})
. Using it with
Eq.

3.16
in
Eq.

3.17
, we get

\mathfrak{R}_{T}^{\prime}\leq(1-\rho)\mathfrak{R}_{T}+\rho R_{max}T.

∎

Missing proofs from
Section

3.4

See
16

Proof.

The result is adapted from
(
Li et al. 2017
, Proposition 1)
, which uses the standard random matrix theory result from
(
Vershynin 2012
, Theorem 5.39)
. We need to carefully construct the sample complexity bound for our case as the observations are only observed for a pair of arms.
∎

The following result is needed to prove
17
.

Lemma 24
.

Let
\overline{V}_{ij}^{t}=\lambda\mathrm{I}_{d^{\prime}}+a\sum_{s\in S_{ij}^{t}}\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})^{\top}
for any
(i,j)
pair of arms, and
n_{ij}^{t}=|S_{ij}^{t}|
. Then

det(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})\leq\left(\lambda+an_{ij}^{t}/d^{\prime}\right)^{d^{\prime}}.

Proof.

The proof is adapted from Lemma 10 of
Abbasi-Yadkori et al. 2011
. By using inequality of arithmetic and geometric means, we have
det(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})\leq(trace(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})/{d^{\prime}})^{d^{\prime}}
. As the trace of matrix is a linear mapping i.e.
trace(A+B)=trace(A)+trace(B)
, hence, we get

\displaystyle trace(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})

\displaystyle=trace(\lambda I_{d^{\prime}})+a\sum_{s\in S_{ij}^{t}}trace\left(\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda{d^{\prime}}+a\sum_{s\in S_{ij}^{t}}\left\|\Phi_{ij}(x)\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\lambda{d^{\prime}}+an_{ij}^{t}.\hskip 5.69054pt\left(\text{as $\left\|\Phi_{ij}(x)\right\|_{2}\leq 1$ and $n_{ij}^{t}=|S_{ij}^{t}|$}\right)

Using upper bound of
trace(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})
for bounding
det(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})
, we get

det(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})\leq(trace(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})/{d^{\prime}})^{d^{\prime}}\leq\left((\lambda{d^{\prime}}+an_{ij}^{t})/{d^{\prime}}\right)^{d^{\prime}}\leq\left(\lambda+an_{ij}^{t}/{d^{\prime}}\right)^{d^{\prime}}.

∎

See
17

Proof.

Let
\overline{V}_{ij}^{t}=\lambda\mathrm{I}_{d^{\prime}}+V_{ij}^{t}
. If
Eq.

3.9
is used for estimation of unknown parameter
\theta_{ij}^{\star}
then by using Eq. (26) and Lemma 8 of
Li et al. 2017
with
\lambda_{min}(V_{ij}^{m+1})\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}-\theta_{ij}^{\star}\right\|_{V_{ij}^{t}}\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}\left\|\sum_{s\in S_{ij}^{t}}\varepsilon_{s}\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})\right\|_{(V_{ij}^{t})^{-1}}\leq\frac{(1-\lambda)^{\frac{-1}{2}}}{\kappa}\left\|\sum_{s\in S_{ij}^{t}}\varepsilon_{s}\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})\right\|_{(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})^{-1}}.

Using upper bound of
\left\|\sum_{s\in S_{ij}^{t}}\varepsilon_{s}\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})\right\|_{(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})^{-1}}
as given in Theorem 1 of
Abbasi-Yadkori et al. 2011
where
\varepsilon_{s}
is
\sigma-
subGaussian random variable, the following inequality holds with at least probability
1-2\delta/K^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1-\lambda)^{\frac{-1}{2}}}{\kappa}\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}\log\left(\frac{det(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})^{1/2}det(\lambda\mathrm{I}_{d^{\prime}})^{-1/2}}{2\delta/K^{2}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma(1-\lambda)^{\frac{-1}{2}}}{\kappa}\sqrt{2\log\left(\frac{det(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})}{det(\lambda\mathrm{I}_{d^{\prime}})}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+2\log\left(\frac{K^{2}}{2\delta}\right)}.

Upper bounding
det(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})
with
a=1
,
\lambda=1/2
, and
n_{ij}^{t}\leq t
by using
24
, we get

\displaystyle\implies\left\|\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}-\theta_{ij}^{\star}\right\|_{V_{ij}^{t}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\sigma}{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{d^{\prime}}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{2n_{ij}^{t}}{d^{\prime}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{K^{2}}{2\delta}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\sigma}{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{d^{\prime}}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{2t}{d^{\prime}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{K^{2}}{2\delta}\right)}.

∎

See
18

Proof.

If sub-optimal arm
l<i^{\star}_{t}
is preferred by
USS-PD
then using Eq. (
3.10b
), we get

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\left\{l\succ_{t}i^{\star}_{t},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\left\{C_{i}-C_{l}>\tilde{p}_{li}^{(t)},I_{t}=l,i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}\leq\mathds{1}_{\left\{C_{i}-C_{l}>\tilde{p}_{li}^{(t)}\right\}}.\hskip 14.22636pt\text{(as $A\cap B\cap C\subseteq A$)}

Using
C_{i}-C_{l}=p_{li}(x_{t})-\xi_{li}(x_{t})
for
l<i
, we have

\displaystyle\implies\mathds{1}_{\left\{l\succ_{t}i^{\star}_{t},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\left\{p_{li}(x_{t})-\xi_{li}(x_{t})>\tilde{p}_{li}^{(t)}\right\}}=\mathds{1}_{\left\{p_{li}(x_{t})-\tilde{p}_{li}^{(t)}>\xi_{li}(x_{t})\right\}}.

Using definition of
p_{li}(x_{t})
and
\tilde{p}_{li}^{(t)}
,

\displaystyle\implies\mathds{1}_{\left\{l\succ_{t}i^{\star}_{t},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}=\mathds{1}_{\left\{\mu(\Phi_{li}(x_{t})^{\top}\theta_{li}^{\star})-\mu\left(\Phi_{li}(x_{t})^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{li}^{t}+\alpha_{li}^{t}\left\|\Phi_{li}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{li}^{t})^{-1}}\right)>\xi_{li}(x_{t})\right\}}.

Since
\mu(\cdot)
is an increasing function and using
\alpha_{li}^{t}
as defined in
17
,
\mu\left(\Phi_{li}(x_{t})^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{li}^{t}+\alpha_{li}^{t}\left\|\Phi_{li}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{li}^{t})^{-1}}\right)
is the upper bound on
\mu(\Phi_{li}(x_{t})^{\top}\theta_{li}^{\star})
for all
(l,i)
pairs with probability at least
1-\delta/2
. We show it as follows:

\displaystyle\Phi_{li}(x_{t})^{\top}\theta_{li}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{li}(x_{t})^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{li}^{t}+\Phi_{li}(x_{t})^{\top}(\theta_{li}^{\star}-\hat{\theta}_{li}^{t})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{li}(x_{t})^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{li}^{t}+\left\|\Phi_{li}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{li}^{t})^{-1}}\left\|\theta_{li}^{\star}-\hat{\theta}_{li}^{t}\right\|_{V_{li}^{t}}

\displaystyle\implies\Phi_{li}(x_{t})^{\top}\theta_{li}^{\star}

\displaystyle\leq\Phi_{li}(x_{t})^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{li}^{t}+\alpha_{li}^{t}\left\|\Phi_{li}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{li}^{t})^{-1}}.

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\left(\text{using }\left\|\theta_{li}^{\star}-\hat{\theta}_{li}^{t}\right\|_{V_{li}^{t}}\leq\alpha_{li}^{t}\right)

Since
\mu(\cdot)
is an increasing function,

\displaystyle\implies\mu(\Phi_{li}(x_{t})^{\top}\theta_{li}^{\star})

\displaystyle\leq\mu\left(\Phi_{li}(x_{t})^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{li}^{t}+\alpha_{li}^{t}\left\|\Phi_{li}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{li}^{t})^{-1}}\right).

Hence, any sub-optimal arm smaller than the optimal arm is selected by
USS-PD
with probability at most
\delta/2
. It completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

See
19

Proof.

If sub-optimal arm
h>i^{\star}_{t}
is preferred by
USS-PD
then using Eq. (
3.10a
), we get

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\left\{h\succ_{t}i,i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\left\{C_{h}-C_{i}<\tilde{p}_{ih}^{(t)},h\succ_{t}i^{\star}_{t},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}\leq\mathds{1}_{\left\{C_{h}-C_{i}<\tilde{p}_{ih}^{(t)}\right\}}.\hskip 14.22636pt\text{(as $A\cap B\cap C\subseteq A$)}

Using
C_{h}-C_{i}=p_{ih}(x_{t})+\xi_{ih}(x_{t})
for
h>i
, we get

\displaystyle\implies\mathds{1}_{\left\{h\succ_{t}i,i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\left\{p_{ih}(x_{t})+\xi_{ih}(x_{t})<\tilde{p}_{ih}^{(t)}\right\}}=\mathds{1}_{\left\{\tilde{p}_{ih}^{(t)}-p_{ih}(x_{t})>\xi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\}}.

Using definition of
p_{ih}(x_{t})
and
\tilde{p}_{ih}^{(t)}
,

\displaystyle\implies\mathds{1}_{\left\{h\succ_{t}i,i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\left\{\mu\left(\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})^{\top}\theta_{ih}^{\star}+\alpha_{ih}^{t}\left\|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{ih}^{t})^{-1}}\right)-\mu(\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{ih}^{t})>\xi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\}}.

As
\mu
is Lipschitz,
|\mu(z_{1})-\mu(z_{2})|\leq k_{\mu}|z_{1}-z_{2}|
where
k_{\mu}
is Lipschitz constant, we have

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{1}_{\left\{k_{\mu}|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})^{\top}\theta_{ih}^{\star}+\alpha_{ih}^{t}\left\|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{ih}^{t})^{-1}}-\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{ih}^{t}|>\xi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{1}_{\left\{k_{\mu}|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})^{\top}\theta_{ih}^{\star}-\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})^{\top}\hat{\theta}_{ih}^{t}|+k_{\mu}\alpha_{ih}^{t}\left\|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{ih}^{t})^{-1}}>\xi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\}}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\left\{k_{\mu}|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})^{\top}(\theta_{ih}^{\star}-\hat{\theta}_{ih}^{t})|+k_{\mu}\alpha_{ih}^{t}\left\|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{ih}^{t})^{-1}}>\xi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\}}.

Using Cauchy-Schwartz inequality and
\left\|x\right\|_{A}^{2}=x^{\top}Ax
, we get

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{1}_{\left\{k_{\mu}\left\|\Phi_{ih}\right\|_{(V_{ih}^{t})^{-1}}\left\|\theta_{ih}^{\star}-\hat{\theta}_{ih}^{t}\right\|_{V_{ih}^{t}}+k_{\mu}\alpha_{ih}^{t}\left\|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{ih}^{t})^{-1}}>\xi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\}}.

As
\left\|\theta_{ih}^{\star}-\hat{\theta}_{ih}^{t}\right\|_{V_{ih}^{t}}\leq\alpha_{ih}^{t}
, we get

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{1}_{\left\{k_{\mu}\alpha_{ih}^{t}\left\|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{ih}^{t})^{-1}}+k_{\mu}\alpha_{ih}^{t}\left\|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{ih}^{t})^{-1}}>\xi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\}}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\left\{2k_{\mu}\alpha_{ih}^{t}\left\|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{ih}^{t})^{-1}}>\xi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\}}.

As
\left\|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\|_{(V_{ih}^{t})^{-1}}\leq\left\|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\|_{2}/\sqrt{\lambda_{min}(V_{ih}^{t})}
where
\lambda_{min}(V_{ih}^{t})
is the smallest eigenvalue of matrix
V_{ih}^{t}
and
\left\|\Phi_{ih}(x_{t})\right\|_{2}\leq 1
, we get

\displaystyle\implies\mathds{1}_{\left\{h\succ_{t}i,i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{1}_{\left\{2k_{\mu}\alpha_{ih}^{t}>\xi_{ih}(x_{t})\sqrt{\lambda_{min}(V_{ih}^{t})}\right\}}.

(3.18)

The event on LHS is subset of event of RHS in
Eq.

3.18
. By changing
i
to
i^{\star}_{t}
completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

See
9

Proof.

The regret for
T
rounds in the Contextual USS problem is given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(C_{I_{t}}+\gamma_{I_{t}}(x_{t})-(C_{i^{\star}_{t}}+\gamma_{i^{\star}_{t}}(x_{t}))\right).

As
R_{max}
denote the maximum regret incurred for any context, we get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq R_{max}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}\neq i^{\star}_{t}\right\}}.

(3.19)

As
\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}\neq i^{\star}_{t}\right\}}
has two random quantities
I_{t}
and
i^{\star}_{t}
, we can re-write it as follows:

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}\neq i^{\star}_{t}\right\}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l<i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}=l,i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}+\sum_{h^{\prime}>i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}=h^{\prime},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}.

Note that if
USS-PD
selects
l<i^{\star}_{t}
then
l
must be preferred over
i^{\star}_{t}
whereas if
h^{\prime}>i^{\star}_{t}
is selected then there exists an arm
h>i^{\star}_{t}
which is preferred over
i^{\star}_{t}
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}\neq i^{\star}_{t}\right\}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l<i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{l\succ_{t}i^{\star}_{t},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}+\sum_{h^{\prime}>i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}=h^{\prime},h\succ_{t}{i^{\star}_{t}},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}\leq\sum_{l<i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{l\succ_{t}i^{\star}_{t},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}+\sum_{h>i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{h\succ_{t}{i^{\star}_{t}},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}.

Using above bound in
Eq.

3.19
, we get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq R_{max}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[\sum_{l<i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{l\succ_{t}i^{\star}_{t},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}+\sum_{h>i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{h\succ_{t}i^{\star}_{t},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}\right].

From
18
,
\mathds{1}_{\left\{l\succ_{t}i^{\star}_{t},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}=0
for any
l<i
with probability at least
1-\delta/2
, then the regret becomes

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq R_{max}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h>i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{h\succ_{t}i^{\star}_{t},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}=R_{max}\sum_{h>i}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}_{\left\{h\succ_{t}i^{\star}_{t},i^{\star}_{t}=i\right\}}\leq R_{max}\sum_{h=2}^{K}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}_{\left\{h\succ_{t}i^{\star}_{t},i^{\star}_{t}<h\right\}}.

Note that
\alpha_{ih}^{t}
is slowly increasing value with
t
that implies
\alpha_{ih}^{t}\leq\alpha_{ih}^{T}
for all
t\leq T
.
Using
16
with
\Psi=\left(\frac{2k_{\mu}\alpha_{ih}^{T}}{\xi_{ih}}\right)^{2}
,
\Sigma_{ih}=\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi_{ih}(X_{s})\Phi_{ih}(X_{s})^{T}\right]
where
s\in S_{ih}^{t}
, after

n_{ih}^{T}\doteq\left(\frac{C_{1}\sqrt{d^{\prime}}+C_{2}\sqrt{\log(K^{2}/2\delta)}}{\lambda_{min}(\Sigma_{ih})}\right)^{2}+\frac{2}{\lambda_{min}(\Sigma_{ih})}\left(\frac{2k_{\mu}\alpha_{ih}^{T}}{\xi_{ih}}\right)^{2}

observations for arm pair
(i,h)
the
\lambda_{min}(V_{ih}^{t})\geq\left(\frac{2k_{\mu}\alpha_{ih}^{T}}{\xi_{ih}}\right)^{2}
with probability at least
1-2\delta/K^{2}
. Therefore, after having
n_{ih}^{T}
observations, the sub-optimal arm
h(>i)
will not be preferred over optimal arm
i
with probability at least
1-2\delta/K^{2}
. Therefore, with probability at least
1-2\delta/K^{2}
, following equations also hold

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}=h,i^{\star}_{t}=i,|S_{ih}^{t}|\geq n_{ih}^{T}\right\}}=0\implies\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}=h,i^{\star}_{t}=i,h>i\right\}}\leq n_{ih}^{T}.

Due to the problem structure, whenever an arm
h
is selected, disagreement labels for all arm pair
(i,j)
where
i<j\leq h
are observed. Therefore, with probability at least
1-2\delta/K
(by union bound), the maximum number of times an arm
h
is selected when the optimal arm’s index is smaller than
h
is
n_{h}^{T}
such that

\displaystyle n_{h}^{T}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{C_{1}\sqrt{d^{\prime}}+C_{2}\sqrt{\log(K^{2}/2\delta)}}{\lambda_{\Sigma}}\right)^{2}+\frac{2}{\lambda_{\Sigma}}\left(\frac{2k_{\mu}\alpha_{T}}{\xi_{h}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{C_{1}\sqrt{d^{\prime}}+C_{2}\sqrt{\log(K^{2}/2\delta)}}{\lambda_{\Sigma}}\right)^{2}+\frac{8}{\lambda_{\Sigma}}\left(\frac{k_{\mu}\alpha_{T}}{\xi_{h}}\right)^{2}

where
\xi_{h}=\min\limits_{i<h,t\geq 1}\xi_{ih}(x_{t})
,
\lambda_{\Sigma}=\min\limits_{i<j\leq K}\lambda_{min}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi_{ij}(X_{s})\Phi_{ij}(X_{s})^{\top}\right]\right)
and
\alpha_{T}\geq\max\limits_{i<h}\alpha_{ih}^{T}
. By using union bound, we get following bound with probability at least
1-\delta/2K

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}=h,i^{\star}_{t}<h\right\}}\leq n_{h}^{T}.

(3.20)

From
Eq.

3.20
, using
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}_{\left\{I_{t}=h,i^{\star}_{t}<h\right\}}\leq n_{h}^{T}
and value of
n_{h}^{T}
, we get following upper bound on regret that holds with probability at least
1-\delta
by union bound

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{T}\leq R_{max}\sum_{h=2}^{K}n_{h}^{T}=R_{max}\sum_{h=2}^{K}\left(\left(\frac{C_{1}\sqrt{d^{\prime}}+C_{2}\sqrt{\log(K^{2}/2\delta)}}{\lambda_{\Sigma}}\right)^{2}+\frac{8}{\lambda_{\Sigma}}\left(\frac{k_{\mu}\alpha_{T}}{\xi_{h}}\right)^{2}\right).

Using
\alpha_{T}=\frac{2\sigma}{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{d^{\prime}}{2}\log\left(1+2T/{d^{\prime}}\right)+\log\left({K^{2}}/{2\delta}\right)}
from Lemma
17
that ensures parameter
\theta_{ij}^{\star}
bounds for all pairs
(i,j)
holds with probability at least
1-\delta/2K
(by union bound) for
T>m
where
m=C\lambda_{\Sigma}^{-2}\left(d+\log(k^{2}/2\delta)\right)+2\lambda_{\Sigma}^{-1}
such that
\lambda_{min}(V_{ij}^{m+1})\geq 1
for all pair
(i,j)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq R_{max}\left(m+\sum_{h=2}^{K}n_{h}^{T}\right)

\displaystyle\implies\mathfrak{R}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq R_{max}\Bigg[m+\sum_{h=2}^{K}\Bigg(\hskip-2.84526pt\Bigg(\frac{C_{1}\sqrt{d^{\prime}}+C_{2}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{K^{2}}{2\delta}\right)}}{\lambda_{\Sigma}}\Bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{16}{\lambda_{\Sigma}}\left(\frac{k_{\mu}\sigma}{\xi_{h}\kappa}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{d^{\prime}}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{2T}{d^{\prime}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{K^{2}}{2\delta}\right)\right)\Bigg)\Bigg].

∎

Algorithm with
\lambda\mathrm{I}_{d^{\prime}}
Initialization

USS-PD
uses forced exploration by selecting arm
K
until the correlation matrix
V_{ij}^{t}
is not invertible for all
(i,j)
pairs of arms. Further, the minimum eigenvalue of
V_{ij}^{t}
for all
(i,j)
pairs is needed to be larger than

so that bound given in
17
holds. Alternatively,
V_{ij}^{t}
can be initialized by adding a regularization term
(

;

;

)
to avoid forced exploration and then apply OFUL type analysis. We have given an algorithm named

⁢
λ

which uses regularization term
\lambda\mathrm{I}_{d^{\prime}}
. However, its analysis still needed the minimum eigenvalue of the non-regularized part of the correlation matrix to become larger than some positive value (depends on
\lambda
value), as shown in our next result.

Lemma 25
.

Let

,

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}-\theta_{ij}^{\star}\right\|_{\overline{V}_{ij}^{t}}\leq\beta_{ij}^{t},

where
\beta_{ij}^{t}=\frac{2\sigma}{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{d^{\prime}}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{n_{ij}^{t}}{d^{\prime}\lambda}\right)+\log\left(\frac{K^{2}}{2\delta}\right)}+2\lambda^{1/2}S
.

Proof.

By using
\left\|Z\right\|_{A+B}\leq\left\|Z\right\|_{A}+\left\|Z\right\|_{B}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}-\theta_{ij}^{\star}\right\|_{\overline{V}_{ij}^{t}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}-\theta_{ij}^{\star}\right\|_{V_{ij}^{t}}+\left\|\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}-\theta_{ij}^{\star}\right\|_{\lambda\mathrm{I}_{d^{\prime}}}.

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt(\mbox{as }\overline{V}_{ij}^{t}=\lambda\mathrm{I}_{d^{\prime}}+V_{ij}^{t})

When

3.9
is used for estimation of unknown parameter
\theta_{ij}^{\star}
then by using Eq. (26) and Eq. (27) of Lemma 8 of
Li et al. 2017
, we have

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}\left\|\sum_{s\in S_{ij}^{t}}\varepsilon_{s}\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})\right\|_{(V_{ij}^{t})^{-1}}+2\lambda^{1/2}S.

\displaystyle\hskip-142.26378pt(\mbox{as }\left\|\theta_{ij}\right\|_{2}\leq S)

Sherman Morrison formula gives
\left\|Z\right\|_{(V_{ij}^{t})^{-1}}\leq\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{ij}^{t})}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\|Z\right\|_{(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})^{-1}}
. Using it, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}-\theta_{ij}^{\star}\right\|_{\overline{V}_{ij}^{t}}\leq\frac{\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{ij}^{t})}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\kappa}\left\|\sum_{s\in S_{ij}^{t}}\varepsilon_{s}\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})\right\|_{(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})^{-1}}+2\lambda^{1/2}S.

Using upper bound on
\left\|\sum_{s\in S_{ij}^{t}}\varepsilon_{s}\Phi_{ij}(x_{s})\right\|_{(\overline{V_{ij}^{t}})^{-1}}
as given in Theorem 1 of
Abbasi-Yadkori et al. 2011
, where

is

subGaussian random variable and holds with probability at least
1-2\delta/K^{2}
, we get

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}-\theta_{ij}^{\star}\right\|_{\overline{V}_{ij}^{t}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{ij}^{t})}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\kappa}\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}\log\left(\frac{det(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})^{1/2}det(\lambda\mathrm{I}_{d^{\prime}})^{-1/2}}{2\delta/K^{2}}\right)}+2\lambda^{1/2}S

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{ij}^{t})}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\kappa}\sqrt{2\log\left(\frac{det(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})}{det(\lambda\mathrm{I}_{d^{\prime}})}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+2\log\left(\frac{K^{2}}{2\delta}\right)}+2\lambda^{1/2}S.

By using
24
to upper bound
det(\overline{V}_{ij}^{t})
, where
t>s
with
a=1
, and
n_{ij}^{t}\leq t
, we get

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{\theta}_{ij}^{t}-\theta_{ij}^{\star}\right\|_{\overline{V}_{ij}^{t}}\leq\frac{\sigma\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{ij}^{t})}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\kappa}\sqrt{{d^{\prime}}\log\left(1+\frac{n_{ij}^{t}}{d^{\prime}\lambda}\right)+2\log\left(\frac{K^{2}}{2\delta}\right)}+2\lambda^{1/2}S.

(3.21)

As
t>s
such that

, we have

Note that if

then

\lambda

\left[C_{i}+\gamma_{i}(x)-\left(C_{i^{\star}}+\gamma_{i^{\star}}(x)\right)\right]
