4.5.2
Comparison between Algorithms

We compare
CSB-SU
,
CSB-SK
, and state-of-the-art CSB-ST algorithm
(
Verma et al. 2019a
)
for the CSB problems with the same threshold. Our algorithms outperforms CSB-ST for instance I and II as shown in
Fig.

4.6(a)
and
Fig.

4.6(b)
, respectively. Even though
CSB-SK
and CSB-ST use binary search for threshold estimation as compared to linear search in
CSB-SU
, there waiting delay with the overestimate of threshold leads to more rounds spend for the threshold estimation in considered CSB problems as compare to
CSB-SU
. Therefore,
CSB-SU
has the smallest regret than the other two algorithms.

(a)

Comparison for Instance I

(b)

Comparison for Instance II

Figure 4.6
:
Comparing regret of
CSB-SU
,
CSB-SK
, and CSB-ST
(
Verma et al. 2019a
)
.

We compare
CSB-MK
, CSB-DK (
CSB-MK
with
n=K
),
CSB-DU
, and state-of-the-art CSB-DT algorithm
(
Verma et al. 2019a
)
for the CSB problems with different thresholds.
CSB-MK
and CSB-DT also uses binary search to estimate the threshold for each arm. These algorithms use the same threshold estimate for the fixed number of rounds, which depends upon the value of
\varepsilon
and
\delta
. The smaller the value of
\varepsilon
, the more these algorithms wait for observing a loss and incur more regret as well. On the other hand,
CSB-DU
uses a linear search to estimate the threshold and does not need to know
\varepsilon
and
\delta
. As expected
CSB-MK
and CSB-DK outperform CSB-DT as shown in
Fig.

4.7(b)
. Whereas the performance of
CSB-MK
matches with CSB-DK in
Fig.

4.7(a)
as only two arms have the same threshold in Instance III. Since
CSB-DU
uses a linear search for threshold estimation, it needs more rounds to estimate allocation equivalent when the threshold has a larger search region than the algorithms that use binary search. Therefore,
CSB-DU
incurs more regret.

(a)

Comparison for Instance III

(b)

Comparison for Instance IV

Figure 4.7
:
Comparing regret of
CSB-MK
, CSB-DK (assuming all thresholds are different which is equivalent to
CSB-MK
with
n=K
),
CSB-DU
and CSB-DT
(
Verma et al. 2019a
)
.

Computation complexity of 0-1 Knapsack with fractional weight and value

Even though
KP(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\theta},Q)
is an NP-Hard problem; it can be solved by a pseudo-polynomial time algorithm
3
3

3

The running time of pseudo-polynomial time algorithm is a polynomial in the numeric value of the input whereas the running time of polynomial-time algorithms is polynomial of the length of the input.
using dynamic programming with the time complexity of O
(KQ)
. But such an algorithm for
KP(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\theta},Q)
works when the value and weight of items are integers. In the case of
\mu_{i}
and
\theta_{i}
are fractions, they need to be converted in integers with the desired accuracy by multiplying by large value
S
. The time complexity of solving
KP(S\boldsymbol{\mu},S\boldsymbol{\theta},SQ)
is O
(KSQ)
as a new capacity of Knapsack is
SQ
. Therefore, the time complexity of solving
KP(S\boldsymbol{\mu},S\boldsymbol{\theta},SQ)
in each of the
T
rounds is O
(TKSQ)
.
Since solving the
0
-
1
Knapsack problem is computationally expensive, we can solve it after
N
rounds as the empirical mean losses do not change drastically in consecutive rounds in practice (except initial rounds). We have used
S=10^{4}
and
N=20
in our experiments involving the different thresholds.

4.6
CSB for Stochastic Network Utility Maximization

In this section, we study the application of CSB for the Stochastic
Network Utility Maximization
problem. Network Utility Maximization (NUM) is an approach for resource allocation among multiple agents such that the total utility of all the agents (network utility) is maximized. In its simplest form, NUM solves the following optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{\boldsymbol{a}}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{K}U_{i}(a_{i})

\displaystyle\mbox{subject to}\hskip 11.38109pt\sum_{i=1}^{K}a_{i}\leq Q

where
U_{i}(\cdot)
denotes the utility of agent
i
, variable
\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{K})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}
denote the allocated resource vector, and
Q\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is amount of resource available. Utilities define the agents’ satisfaction level, which depends on the amount of resources they are allocated. A resource could be bandwidth, power, or rates they receive. Since the seminal work of
Kelly 1997
, there has been a tremendous amount of work on NUM and its extensions. NUM is used to model various resource allocation problems and improve network protocols based on its analysis. We refer the readers to
Palomar and Chiang 2006
and
Palomar and Chiang 2007
for an informative tutorial and survey on this subject.

The nature of utility functions is vital in the analysis of the NUM problem and assumed to be known or can be constructed based on the agent behavior model and operator cost model. However, agent behavior models are often difficult to quantify. Therefore, we consider the NUM problem where the utilities of the agent are unknown and stochastic. The earlier NUM problems considered deterministic settings. Significant progress has been made to extend the NUM setup to consider the stochastic nature of the network and agent behavior
(
Yi and Chiang 2008
)
. For both the static and stochastic networks, the works in the literature often assume that the utility functions are smooth concave functions and apply Karush-Kuhn-Tucker conditions to find the optimal allocation. However, if the utility functions are unknown, these methods are useful only once the utilities are learned. Many of the NUM variants with full knowledge of utilities aim to find an optimal policy that meets several constraints like stability, fairness, and resource
(
Neely 2010
;
Eryilmaz and Koprulu 2017
;
Sinha and Modiano 2018
)
. In this work, we only focus on resource constraint due to limited divisible resource (bandwidth, power, rate).
Fig.

4.8
depicts the Stochastic Network Utility Maximization problem.

Figure 4.8
:
Stochastic Network Utility Maximization problem where the resources are allocated among multiple agents such that the total average utility of all the agents (network utility) is maximized.

Since learning an arbitrary utility function is not always feasible, we assume the utilities belong to a class of ‘threshold’ type functions. Specifically, we assume that each agent’s utility is stochastic with some positive mean only when it is allocated a certain minimum resource. We refer to the minimum resource required by an agent as its ‘threshold’ and the mean utility it receives when it is allocated resource above the threshold as its ‘mean reward.’ Thus the expected utility of each agent is defined by two parameters – a threshold and a mean reward. Such threshold type utilities correspond to hard resource requirements. For example, an agent can transmit and obtain a positive rate (reward) only if its power or bandwidth allocation is above a certain amount. In each round, the operator allocates a resource to each agent and observes the utilities the agent obtains. The goal of the operator is to allocate resources such that the expected network utility is maximized. We pose this problem as a Censored Semi-Bandits problem in the reward maximization setting, where the operator corresponds to a learner, agents to arms, and utilities to rewards. The learner’s goal is to learn a policy that minimizes the difference between the best achievable expected network utility with full knowledge of the agent utilities and that obtained by the learner under the same resource constraint with the estimated utilities of agents.

4.6.1
CSB setup for Reward Maximization

The CSB problems that are considered in
Section

4.2
works only in the loss setting. Now we extend the CSB setup to reward maximization setting, where the optimal allocation can be computed as follows:

\boldsymbol{a}^{\star}\in\arg\!\max_{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{i}\geq\theta_{i}\right\}}.

The interaction between a learner and the environment that governs rewards for the arms is as follows: In the round
t
, the environment generates a reward vector
(X_{t,1},X_{t,2},\ldots,X_{t,K})\in\{0,1\}^{K}
, where
X_{t,i}
denotes the true reward for arm
i
in round
t
. The sequence
(X_{t,i})_{t\geq 1}
is generated IID with the common mean
\mathbb{E}\left[X_{t,i}\right]=\mu_{i}
for each
i\in[K]
. The learner selects a feasible allocation
\boldsymbol{a}_{t}=\{a_{t,i}:i\in[K]\}
and observes reward vector
X_{t}^{\prime}=\{X^{\prime}_{t,i}:i\in[K]\}
, where
X_{t,i}^{\prime}=X_{t,i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{t,i}\geq\theta_{i}\right\}}
and collects reward
r_{t}(\boldsymbol{a}_{t})=\sum_{i\in[K]}X_{t,i}^{\prime}
. A policy of the learner is to select a feasible allocation in each round based on the observed reward such that the cumulative reward is maximized. The performance of a policy that makes allocation
\{\boldsymbol{a}_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
in round
t
is measured in terms of expected regret for
T
rounds given by

\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{R}_{T}]=T\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{x^{\star}_{i}\geq\theta_{i}\right\}}-\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{i=1}^{K}Y_{t,i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{x_{t,i}\geq\theta_{i}\right\}}\right].

A good policy must have sub-linear regret, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]/T\rightarrow 0
as
T\rightarrow\infty
. Next, we define the notion of treating a pair of thresholds for the given reward vector and resource to be ‘equivalent.’

Definition 3

(Allocation Equivalent)
.

For fixed reward vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and amount of resource
Q
, two threshold vectors
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
are allocation equivalent if the following holds:

\max_{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{i}\geq\theta_{i}\right\}}=\max_{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{i}\geq\hat{\theta}_{i}\right\}}.

4.6.2
Algorithms for Network Utility Maximization

We first focus on the special case of the network utility maximization problem where
\theta_{i}=\theta_{s}
for all
i\in[K]
. We develop an algorithm named Network Utility Maximization with the Same Threshold (
NUM-SK
). This algorithm is adapted from
CSB-SK
for the reward maximization setup. There are two major differences: 1) the feedback (reward) is only observed when the resource allocation is more than a certain threshold, and 2) a sample for the mean reward estimate is drawn from the beta distribution. Similarly, we develop an algorithm named Network Utility Maximization with the Multiple Threshold (
NUM-MK
), which is adapted from
CSB-MK
to the reward maximization setup.

Next, we will give the regret upper bounds for

and

. For simplicity of discussion, we assume that arms are indexed according to their decreasing mean rewards, i.e.,
\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2},\ldots,\geq\mu_{K}
, but the algorithms are not aware of this ordering. We refer to the first
M
arms as

M
arms. For a instance
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\theta},C)
and any feasible allocation
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}
, we define the sub-optimality gap as
\Delta_{a}=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\big(\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{i}^{\star}\geq\theta_{i}\right\}}-\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{i}\geq\theta_{i}\right\}}\big)
. The maximum and minimum regret incurred in a round is
\nabla_{\max}=\max\limits_{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{a}}
and
\nabla_{\min}=\min\limits_{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{a}}
, respectively. Now we will give regret bound of
NUM-SK
.

NUM-MK

\varepsilon

Theorem 5
.

Let
\mu_{K}\geq\varepsilon>0
,
\mu_{M}>\mu_{M+1}
,
W_{\delta}={\log(\log_{2}(K)/\delta)}/{\log(1/(1-\varepsilon))}
, and
T>W_{\delta}\log_{2}{(K)}
. Then with probability at least
1-\delta
, the expected regret of
NUM-SK
is upper bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq W_{\delta}\log_{2}{(K)}\Delta_{\max}+O\left((\log T)^{{2}/{3}}\right)+\mbox{$\sum_{i\in[K]\setminus[M]}$}\frac{(\mu_{M}-\mu_{i})\log{T}}{d(\mu_{i},\mu_{M})}.

As we are in the reward setting, the regret bound of MP-TS can be used as it is. The remaining proof follows similar steps as the proof of
Theorem

1
.

Let
\nabla_{i,\min}
be the minimum regret for superarms containing arm
i
and
K^{\prime}
be the maximum number of arms in any feasible resource allocation. We redefine
W_{\delta}=\log(K\log_{2}(\lceil 1+Q/\gamma\rceil)/\delta)/\log(1/(1-\varepsilon))
. Now we are ready to state the regret bound of
NUM-MK
.

Theorem 6
.

Let
\gamma>0
,
\mu_{K}\geq\varepsilon>0
, and
T>KW_{\delta}\log_{2}\left(\lceil 1+Q/\gamma\rceil\right)
. Then with probability at least
1-\delta
, the expected regret of
NUM-MK
is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\leq{KW_{\delta}\log_{2}\left(\lceil 1+Q/\gamma\rceil\right)\Delta_{\max}}+O\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}\frac{\log^{2}(K^{\prime})\log T}{\nabla_{i,\min}}\right).

Since we are in the reward setting, the regret bound of combinatorial bandits algorithm CTS-BETA can be used as it is. The remaining proof follows similar steps as the proof of
Theorem

2
.

Anytime Algorithms for reward maximization setting.

For simplicity, consider the reward setting with a single threshold. When the allocated resource exceeds the arm’s threshold, the learner may continue to observe sample values of
0
due to the stochastic nature of reward generation. Thus, the learner needs to observe enough samples to be confident that the resource allocated is above the threshold. To decide how much is enough, the learner needs to know
T
so that exploration and exploitation can be well balanced. However, note that this issue does not arise in the loss setting; if the learner continues to observe a sample
0
, there is no need to increase the allocation further, and the learner can continue the same resource allocation on the arm. The same argument applied if the learner has to start by allocating the higher amount of resources and keep decreasing it until it goes below the threshold.

4.6.3
Experiments

We evaluate the performance of
NUM-SK
and
NUM-MK
empirically on three synthetically generated instances. In instance
1
, the threshold is the same for all arms, whereas, in instances
2
and
3
, thresholds vary across arms. We ran the algorithm for
T=10000
rounds in all the simulations. All the experiments are repeated
100
times, and the regret curves are shown with a
95\%
confidence interval. The vertical line on each curve shows the confidence interval. The following empirical results validate sub-linear bounds for our algorithms. The details about the problem instances are as follows:

Instance
1
(Identical Threshold):
It has
K=50,Q=20,

\theta_{s}=0.7,\delta=0.1
and
\varepsilon=0.1
. The mean reward of arm
i\in[K]
is
0.25+(i-1)/100
.

Instance
2
(Different Thresholds):
It has
K=5,Q=2,

\delta=0.1,

\varepsilon=0.1
and
\gamma=10^{-3}
. The mean reward vector is
\boldsymbol{\mu}=[0.9,

0.89,0.87,0.6,0.3]
and the corresponding threshold vector is
\boldsymbol{\theta}=[0.7,0.7,0.7,0.6,0.35]
.

Instance
3
(Different Thresholds):
It has
K=10,Q=3,

\delta=0.1
,
\varepsilon=0.1
and
\gamma=10^{-3}
. The mean reward vector is
\boldsymbol{\mu}\hskip-0.56905pt=\hskip-0.56905pt[0.9,0.8,0.42,0.6,0.5,0.2,0.11,0.7,0.3,0.98]
and the corresponding threshold vector is
\boldsymbol{\theta}=[0.6,0.55,0.3,

0.46,0.34,0.2,0.07,0.3,0.25,0.8]
.

We considered two different reward distributions of arms: 1) Bernoulli, where the rewards of arm
i
are Bernoulli distributed with parameter
\mu_{i}
, and 2) Uniform, where the rewards of arm
i
is uniformly distributed in the interval
[\mu_{i}-0.1,\mu_{i}+0.1]
. For any continuous reward distribution with support in
(0,1]
, the value of
W_{\delta}
is set to
1
because the reward is observed with probability
1
when the allocated resource is above its threshold on any arm. For the Bernoulli distribution, the value of
W_{\delta}
is
38
for instance
1
,
62
for instance
2
and
69
for instance
3
. Hence, we observe less regret for uniformly distributed rewards than Bernoulli distributed rewards. This difference is more significant when the arms have different thresholds.

Experiments with the same threshold:

We perform two different experiments on problem instance
1
using
NUM-SK
. First, we varied the amount of resources
Q
while keeping other parameters unchanged. With more resource, the learner can allocate resource to more arms. Hence learner can observe rewards from more arms in each round, which leads to faster learning and low cumulative regret, as shown in Fig. (
4.9(b)
) for the uniformly distributed rewards. For the uniform distribution we use binarization trick
(
Agrawal and Goyal 2012
)
to apply
NUM-SK
: when a real-valued reward
X_{t,i}\in(0,1]
is observed, the algorithm is updated with a fake binary reward that is drawn from Bernoulli distribution with parameter
X_{t,i}
, i.e.,
X_{t,i}^{f}\sim Ber(X_{t,i})\in\{0,1\}
. The different amount of resource has different optimal allocation and sub-optimality gap. Hence with large
W_{\delta}
value for Bernoulli distributed rewards, we may not observe similar behavior (less regret with more resource) as shown in Fig. (
4.9(a)
).

(a)

Bernoulli Distributed Reward

(b)

Uniform Distributed Reward

Figure 4.9
:
Comparing regret of
NUM-SK
v/s amount of resource for problem instance
1
with Bernoulli and uniformly distributed reward.

Second, we varied the threshold
\theta_{s}
while keeping other parameters unchanged. As a smaller threshold allows the allocation of resource to more arms, we observe that a smaller threshold leads to faster learning due to more feedback. These trends are shown in Fig. (
4.10(a)
) and (
4.10(b)
) for Bernoulli and uniformly distributed rewards, respectively.

(a)

Bernoulli Distributed Reward

(b)

Uniform Distributed Reward

Figure 4.10
:
Comparing regret of
NUM-SK
v/s different values of same threshold for problem instance
1
with Bernoulli and uniformly distributed reward.

Experiments with different thresholds:

We evaluate the performance of
NUM-MK
on problem instances
2
and
3
. We varied the amount of resources
Q
while keeping other parameters unchanged. As the thresholds are different across arms, an increase in the resource may lead to a selection of a different set of arms leading to different sub-optimality gaps. Hence, it does not show the same behavior (less regret with more resource) as observed for the same threshold.

(a)

Bernoulli Distributed Reward

(b)

Uniform Distributed Reward

Figure 4.11
:
Comparing regret of

v/s amount of resource for problem instance

with Bernoulli and uniformly distributed reward.

:

v/s amount of resource for problem instance

with Bernoulli and uniformly distributed reward.

But we observe that the allocation equivalent is learned faster as the reward of more arms can be observed simultaneously with more resource. These observations are shown in Figs. (
4.11(a)
) and (
4.11(b)
) generated on instance
2
for Bernoulli and uniformly distributed rewards on instance
2
, and same is repeated in Figs. (
4.12(a)
) and (
4.12(b)
) on instance
3
.

We run experiment
500
times for uniformly distributed rewards (Figs. (
4.11(b)
) and (
4.12(b)
)) on instance
2
and
3
as confidence intervals overlapped for
100
runs.

4.7
Appendix

Missing proofs from Section
4.3

See
1

Proof.

Proof.
The case
\left\lfloor Q/\theta_{s}\right\rfloor\geq K
is trivial. We consider the case
\left\lfloor Q/\theta_{s}\right\rfloor<K
. By definition
M=\min\{\left\lfloor Q/\theta_{s}\right\rfloor,K\}
. We have
M\leq Q/\theta_{s}
and
\theta_{s}\leq Q/M\doteq\hat{\theta}_{s}
. Hence
\hat{\theta}_{s}\geq\theta_{s}
. Therefore,
\hat{\theta}_{s}
fraction of resource allocation for an arm has same reduction in the mean loss as
\theta_{s}
. Further, in both the instances
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\theta_{s},Q)
and
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\hat{\theta}_{s},Q)
the optimal allocations incur no loss from the
t
op-
M
arms and the same amount of loss from the
b
ottom-
(K-M)
arms. Hence the mean loss reduction for both the instances is same. This argument completes the proof of first part. As
M\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
and
\hat{\theta}_{s}\leq Q
, the possible value of
\hat{\theta}_{s}
is only one of the elements in the set
\Theta=\{Q/K,Q/(K-1),\cdots,Q\}
.
∎

See
2

Proof.

Proof.
When
\hat{\theta}_{s}<\theta_{s}
, it is possible that no loss is observed for
W_{\delta}
consecutive rounds that leads to incorrect estimation of
\theta_{s}
. We want to set
W_{\delta}
in such a way that the probability of occurring such event is upper bounded by
\delta
. This probability is bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\text{No loss is observed on $Q/\hat{\theta}_{s}$ arms for $W_{\delta}$ consecutive rounds at $\hat{\theta}_{s}<\theta_{s}$ (underestimate)}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\prod_{i>K-Q/\hat{\theta}_{s}}^{K}(1-\mu_{i})^{W_{\delta}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{\big(as $(1-\mu_{i})$ is the probability of not observing loss for arm $i$\big)}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\prod_{i>K-Q/\hat{\theta}_{s}}^{K}(1-\varepsilon)^{W_{\delta}}\hskip 22.76219pt\mbox{\big(as $\varepsilon\geq\mu_{i},~\forall i\in[K]$\big)}

\displaystyle\qquad=(1-\varepsilon)^{\frac{QW_{\delta}}{\hat{\theta}_{s}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq(1-\varepsilon)^{W_{\delta}}.\hskip 14.22636pt\text{\big(as $\hat{\theta}_{s}\leq Q$\big)}

Since we are using binary search and the set
\Theta
has
K
elements, the algorithm goes through at most
\log_{2}(K)
underestimates of
\theta_{s}
. Let
I
denote the set of indices of these underestimates in
\Theta

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\text{No loss is observed for consecutive $W_{\delta}$ rounds at any underestimate of $\theta_{s}$}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{P}\left\{\text{No loss is observed for consecutive $W_{\delta}$ rounds at the underestimate $\Theta(i)$}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq(1-\varepsilon)^{W_{\delta}}\log_{2}(K).

As we are interesting in bounding the probability of making mistake by
\delta
, we get,

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)^{W_{\delta}}\log_{2}(K)\leq\delta

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)^{W_{\delta}}\leq\delta/\log_{2}(K).

Taking log on both side of above equation, we get

\displaystyle W_{\delta}\log(1-\varepsilon)\leq\log(\delta/\log_{2}(K))

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle W_{\delta}\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\right)\geq\log(\log_{2}(K)/\delta)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle W_{\delta}\geq\frac{\log(\log_{2}(K)/\delta)}{\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\right)}.

We set

W_{\delta}=\frac{\log(\log_{2}(K)/\delta)}{\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\right)}.

(4.4)

Hence, the minimum rounds needed to find a threshold that is an allocation equivalent with probability of at least
1-\delta
is
W_{\delta}\log_{2}(K)
.
∎

See
1

Proof.

Proof.
This result is an extension of Proposition 1 in
Verma et al. 2019a
to the case where
\theta_{s}\leq Q
instead of
\theta_{s}\leq 1
.
Let
\pi^{\prime}
be a policy on
P:=(\boldsymbol{\mu},\theta_{s},Q)\in\mathcal{P}_{s}
. The regret of policy
\pi^{\prime}
on
P
is given by

\mathfrak{R}_{T}(\pi^{\prime},P)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{t,i}<\theta_{s}\right\}}-\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a^{\star}_{i}<\theta_{s}\right\}}\right),

where
\boldsymbol{a}^{\star}
is the optimal allocation for
P
. Consider
f(P)=(\boldsymbol{\mu},m)\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{MP}}
where
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is same as in
P
and
m=K-M
, where
M=\min\{\lfloor Q/\theta_{s}\rfloor,K\}
. The regret of policy
\pi
on
f(P)
is given by

\mathfrak{R}_{T}(\pi,f(P))=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\sum_{i\in M_{t}}\mu_{i}-\sum_{i>K-M}^{K}\mu_{i}\right),

where
M_{t}
is the superarm played in round
t
. Recall the ordering
\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\mu_{K}
. It is clear that
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a^{\star}_{i}<\theta_{s}\right\}}=\sum_{i>M}^{K}\mu_{i}
.
Let
C_{t}
be the set of arms where no resources are allocated by policy
\pi^{\prime}
in round
t
. Since, loss only incurred from arms in the set
C_{t}
, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{t,i}<\theta_{s}\right\}}=\sum_{i\in C_{t}}\mu_{i}
. By definition, the policy
\pi
selects superarm
M_{t}=C_{t}
in round
t
, i.e., set of arms returned by policy
\pi^{\prime}
for which no resourced are applied. Hence
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{t,i}<\theta_{s}\right\}}=\sum_{i\in M_{t}}\mu_{i}
. This establishes the regret of policy
\pi^{\prime}
on
P
is same as regret of policy
\pi
on
f(P)
and hence, we get
\mathfrak{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\text{MP}})\leq\mathfrak{R}(\mathcal{P}_{s})
.
Similarly, we can also establish the other direction of the proposition and get
\mathfrak{R}(\mathcal{P}_{s})\leq\mathfrak{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\text{MP}})
. Thus we conclude that
\mathfrak{R}(\mathcal{P}_{s})=\mathfrak{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\text{MP}})
.
∎

We need the following results to prove the
Theorem

1
.

Theorem 7
.

Let
\hat{\theta}_{s}
be allocation equivalent of
\theta_{s}
for instance
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\theta_{s},Q)
. Then, the expected regret of
CSB-SK
for
T
rounds after knowing the allocation equivalent is upper bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]\leq O\left((\log T)^{{2}/{3}}\right)+\sum_{i\in[M]}\frac{(\mu_{i}-\mu_{M+1})\log{T}}{d(\mu_{M+1},\mu_{i})}.

(4.5)

Proof.

Proof.
As
\hat{\theta}_{s}
is the allocation equivalent of
\theta_{s}
, the instances
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\theta_{s},Q)
and
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\hat{\theta}_{s},Q)
have the same minimum loss. After knowing the allocation equivalent, the CSB problem with the same threshold is equivalent to solving an MP-MAB instance (
Proposition

1
). Hence, we can directly apply Theorem 1 of
Komiyama et al. 2015
to obtain the regret bounds by setting
k=K-M
and noting that we are in the loss setting and incur regret only when an arm
i\in[M]
is in selected superarm.
∎

Theorem 8
.

With probability at least
1-\delta
, the expected cumulative regret of
CSB-SK
is upper bounded as

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]\leq W_{\delta}\log_{2}{(K)}\nabla_{\max}+O\left((\log T)^{{2}/{3}}\right)+\sum_{i\in[M]}\frac{(\mu_{i}-\mu_{M+1})\log{T}}{d(\mu_{M+1},\mu_{i})}.

Proof.

Proof.
The regret of
CSB-SK
can be divided into two parts: regret before knowing allocation equivalent and after knowing it. The threshold estimation completes in at most
W_{\delta}\log_{2}{(K)}
rounds and returns a threshold which is an allocation equivalent with the probability of at least
1-\delta
. The maximum regret incurred for estimating allocation equivalent is
W_{\delta}\log_{2}{(K)}\nabla_{\max}
. The regret incurred after knowing allocation equivalent is given by
Theorem

7
. Thus the expected regret of
CSB-SK
is the sum of regret incurred in the two parts and holds with the probability of at least
1-\delta
.
∎

We are now ready to give the proof of
Theorem

1
.
See
1

Proof.

Proof.
The regret bound follows from Theorem
8
by setting
\delta=T^{-(\log T)^{-\alpha}}
and unconditioning the expected regret incurred after knowing the allocation equivalent in
CSB-SK
.
∎

See
2

Proof.

Proof.
Assigning
\theta_{i}
fraction of resources to an arm
i
reduces the total mean loss by amount
\mu_{i}
. Our goal is to allocate resources such that total mean loss is minimized, i.e.,
\min\limits_{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}}\sum_{i\in[K]}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{i}<\theta_{i}\right\}}
. observe that the maximization version of same optimization problem is
\max\limits_{\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}}\sum_{i\in[K]}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{i}\geq\theta_{i}\right\}}
which is exactly same as solving a 0-1 knapsack with capacity
Q
where item
i
has value
\mu_{i}
and weight
\theta_{i}
.
∎

See
3

Proof.

Proof.
Let
L^{\star}=\left\{i:a_{i}^{\star}<\theta_{i}\right\}
and
r=Q-\sum_{i:a_{i}^{\star}\geq\theta_{i}}\theta_{i}
. If resource
r
is allocated to any arm
i\in L^{\star}
, minimum value of mean loss will not change as
r<\min_{i\in L^{\star}}\theta_{i}
. If we can allocate
\gamma=r/K
fraction of
r
to each arm
i\in K
, the minimum mean loss still remains the same. If estimated threshold of every arm
i\in K
lies in
[\theta_{i},\left\lceil\theta_{i}/\gamma\right\rceil\gamma]
then using Theorem 3.2 of
Hifi and Mhalla 2013
,
KP(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\theta},Q)
and
KP(\boldsymbol{\mu},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},Q)
has the same optimal solution because of having the same mean loss for both the problem instances.
∎

See
4

Proof.

Proof.
For any arm
i\in[K]
, we want
\hat{\theta}_{i}\in[\theta_{i},\left\lceil\theta_{i}/\gamma\right\rceil\gamma]
. As
\theta_{i}\in(0,Q]
, we can divide interval
[0,Q]
into a discrete set
\Theta\doteq\left\{0,\gamma,2\gamma,\ldots,Q\right\}
and note that
|\Theta|=\left\lceil 1+{Q}/{\gamma}\right\rceil
. As search space is reduced by half in each change of
\hat{\theta}_{i}
, the maximum change in
\hat{\theta}_{i}
is upper bounded by
\log_{2}|\Theta|
to make sure that
\hat{\theta}_{i}\in[\theta_{i},\left\lceil\theta_{i}/\gamma\right\rceil\gamma]
. When
\hat{\theta}_{i}
is underestimated and no loss is observed for consecutive
W_{\delta}
rounds, a mistake happens by assuming that current allocation is an overestimate. We set
W_{\delta}
such that the probability of estimating wrong
\hat{\theta}_{i}
is small and bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\text{No loss is observed for consecutive $W_{\delta}$ rounds when $\hat{\theta}_{i}$ is underestimated}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad=(1-\mu_{i})^{W_{\delta}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{\big(as $(1-\mu_{i})$ is the probability of not observing loss at arm $i$\big)}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq(1-\varepsilon)^{W_{\delta}}.\hskip 19.34787pt\text{\big(since $\forall i\in[K]:\mu_{i}>\varepsilon$\big)}

Since we are doing binary search, the algorithm goes through at most
\log_{2}(|\Theta|)
underestimates of
\theta_{i}
. Let
I
denote the set of indices of these underestimates in
\Theta

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\text{No loss is observed for consecutive $W_{\delta}$ rounds when $\hat{\theta}_{i}$ is underestimated}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{P}\left\{\text{No loss is observed for consecutive $W_{\delta}$ rounds when $\hat{\theta}_{i}$ is underestimated}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq(1-\varepsilon)^{W_{\delta}}\log_{2}(|\Theta|).

Next, we will bound the probability of making mistake for any of the arm. That is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\exists i\in[K],\hat{\theta}_{i}\in\Theta:\text{No loss is observed for consecutive $W_{\delta}$ rounds when $\hat{\theta}_{i}$ is underestimated}\right\}

\displaystyle~~\leq\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mathbb{P}\left\{\exists\hat{\theta}_{i}\in\Theta:\text{No loss is observed for consecutive $W_{\delta}$ rounds when $\hat{\theta}_{i}$ is underestimated}\right\}

\displaystyle~~\leq K(1-\varepsilon)^{W_{\delta}}\log_{2}(|\Theta|).

As we are interested in bounding the above probability of making a mistake by
\delta
for all arms, we have the following expression,

\displaystyle K(1-\varepsilon)^{W_{\delta}}\log_{2}(|\Theta|)\leq\delta

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)^{W_{\delta}}\leq\delta/K\log_{2}(|\Theta|).

Taking log both side, we get

\displaystyle W_{\delta}\log(1-\varepsilon)\leq\log(\delta/K\log_{2}(|\Theta|))

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle W_{\delta}\log\left({1}/{(1-\varepsilon)}\right)\geq\log(K\log_{2}(|\Theta|)/\delta)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle W_{\delta}\geq\frac{\log(K\log_{2}(|\Theta|)/\delta)}{\log\left({1}/{(1-\varepsilon)}\right)}.

As
|\Theta|=\left\lceil 1+Q/\gamma\right\rceil
, we set

W_{\delta}=\frac{\log(K\log_{2}\left\lceil 1+Q/\gamma\right\rceil/\delta)}{\log\left({1}/{(1-\varepsilon)}\right)}.

(4.6)

Therefore, the minimum number of rounds needed to find a threshold
\hat{\theta}_{i}
for an arm
i
, which is an element of allocation equivalent vector with the probability of at least
1-\delta/K
is upper bounded by
W_{\delta}\log_{2}\left\lceil 1+Q/\gamma\right\rceil
.

Since
n
is the number of different thresholds, there are
n
different groups of arms where group
G_{i}
consists of arms having the same estimated threshold
\hat{\theta}_{i}
in the estimated allocation equivalent vector. We divided the number of rounds to know allocation equivalent into two parts. The first deals with the maximum number of expected rounds needed to find
n
thresholds. In comparison, the second part deals with finding the good thresholds for remaining arms using known thresholds.

Let consider the worst case where only one threshold is estimated at a time. Then the maximum expected rounds needed to estimate threshold associated with
G_{i}
is
W_{\delta}\log_{2}\left\lceil 1+Q/\gamma\right\rceil
. Using this fact with definition of
A_{\theta_{n}}
, the maximum expected rounds needed to estimate
n
thresholds is
W_{\delta}\sum_{i\in A_{\theta_{n}}}\log_{2}\left\lceil 1+Q/\gamma\right\rceil
. Once all
n
thresholds are known then the threshold for any arm
k
in remaining arms with non-zero mean loss need to search over
n
possible values of thresholds and hence the expected number of rounds needed to its estimate is
W_{\delta}\log_{2}(n+1)
. Therefore, the maximum number of rounds needed to estimate threshold for all arms in
A_{\theta_{n}}^{c}
is
W_{\delta}\sum_{k\in A_{\theta_{n}}^{c}:\mu_{k}\neq 0}\log_{2}(n+1)
which further upper bounded by
W_{\delta}K\log_{2}(n+1)
. With this argument, the proof is complete.
∎

Equivalence of CSB with different thresholds and Combinatorial Semi-Bandit

In stochastic Combinatorial Semi-Bandits (CoSB), a learner can play a subset of
K
arms in each round, also known as superarm, and observes the loss from each arm played
(
Chen et al. 2013
;
Chen et al. 2016
;
Wang and Chen 2018
)
. The size of a superarm can vary, and the mean loss of a superarm only depends on the mean of its constituent arms. The goal is to select a superarm that has the smallest loss. A policy in CoSB selects a superarm in each round based on past information. The performance of a policy is measured in terms of regret, defined as the difference between cumulative loss incurred by the policy and that incurred by playing an optimal superarm in each round. Let
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathcal{I})\in[0,1]^{K}\times 2^{[K]}
denote an instance of CoSB, where
\boldsymbol{\mu}
denote the mean loss vector and
\mathcal{I}
denotes the set of superarms. Let
\mathcal{P}_{d}\subset\mathcal{P}
denote the set of CSB instances with different thresholds. For any
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\theta},Q)\in\mathcal{P}_{d}
with
K
arms and known threshold
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, let
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathcal{I})
be an instance of CoSB with
K
arms and each arm has the same Bernoulli distribution as the corresponding arm in the CSB instance. Let
\mathcal{P}_{\text{CoSB}}
denote set of resulting CoSB problems and
g:\mathcal{P}_{d}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\text{CoSB}}
denote the above transformation.

Let
\pi
be a policy on
\mathcal{P}_{\text{CoSB}}
. The policy
\pi
can also be adapted for any
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\theta},Q)\in\mathcal{P}_{d}
with known
\boldsymbol{\theta}
to decide which set of arms are allocated resource as follows: In round
t
, let information
(C_{1},Y_{1},C_{2},Y_{2},\ldots,C_{t-1},Y_{t-1})
collected from a CSB instance, where
C_{s}
is the set of arms where no resource is applied and
Y_{s}
is the samples observed from these arms, is given to
\pi
which returns a set
C_{t}
. Then all arms other than arms in
C_{t}
are given resource equal to their estimated good threshold. Let this policy on
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\theta},Q)\in\mathcal{P}_{d}
is denoted as
\pi^{\prime}
. Similarly, a policy
\beta^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{P}_{d}
can be adopted to yield a policy for
\mathcal{P}_{\text{CoSB}}
as follows: In round
t
, the information
(M_{1},Y_{1},M_{2},Y_{2},\ldots,M_{t-1},M_{t-1})
, where
M_{s}
is the superarm played in round
s
and
Y_{s}
is the associated loss observed from each arms in
M_{s}
, collected on an CoSB instance is given to the policy
\beta^{\prime}
. Then the policy
\beta^{\prime}
returns a set
M_{t}
where no resources has allocated. The superarm corresponding to
M_{t}
is then played. Let this policy on
\mathcal{P}_{\text{CoSB}}
be denoted by
\beta
. Note that when
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is known, the mapping is invertible.
Our next result gives regret equivalence between the CoSB problem and the CSB problem with the known thresholds.

See
3

Proof.

Proof.
Let
\pi^{\prime}
be a policy on
P:=(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\theta},Q)\in\mathcal{P}_{d}
. The regret of policy
\pi^{\prime}
on
P
is given by

\mathfrak{R}_{T}(\pi^{\prime},P)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{t,i}<\theta_{i}\right\}}-\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a^{\star}_{i}<\theta_{i}\right\}}\right),

where
\boldsymbol{a}^{\star}
is the optimal allocation for
P
. Consider
g(P)=(\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathcal{I})\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{CoSB}}
where
g:\mathcal{P}_{d}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\text{CoSB}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is the same as in
P
and
\mathcal{I}
contains all superarms (set of arms) for which resource allocation is feasible. The regret of policy
\pi
on
g(P)
is given by

\mathfrak{R}_{T}(\pi,g(P))=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big(l(M_{t},\boldsymbol{\mu})-l(M^{\star},\boldsymbol{\mu})\big),

where
M_{t}
is the superarm played in round
t
,
M^{\star}
is optimal superarm, and
l
returns mean loss for given superarm.
The outcome of
l(M,\boldsymbol{\mu})
only depends on mean loss of constituents arms of the superarm
M
. In our setting,
l(M,\boldsymbol{\mu})=\sum_{i\in M}\mu_{i}
where
M=\left\{i:a_{i}<\theta_{i}\right\}
for allocation
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}
. It is clear that
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a^{\star}_{i}<\theta_{i}\right\}}=l(M^{\star},\boldsymbol{\mu})
. Let
C_{t}
be the set of arms where no resource is allocated by
\pi^{\prime}
in round
t
. Since, loss is only incurred for arms in the set
C_{t}
, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{t,i}<\theta_{s}\right\}}=\sum_{i\in C_{t}}\mu_{i}
. By definition the policy
\pi
selects superarm
M_{t}=C_{t}
in round
t
, i.e., set of arms returned by
\pi^{\prime}
for which no resourced are applied. Hence
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{t,i}<\theta_{s}\right\}}=\sum_{i\in M_{t}}\mu_{i}
. This establishes the regret of
\pi^{\prime}
on
P
is same as regret of
\pi
on
g(P)
and hence,
\mathfrak{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\text{CoSB}})\leq\mathfrak{R}(\mathcal{P}_{d})
.
Similarly, we can establish the other direction of the proposition and get
\mathfrak{R}(\mathcal{P}_{d})\leq\mathfrak{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\text{CoSB}})
. Thus we conclude
\mathfrak{R}(\mathcal{P}_{d})=\mathfrak{R}(\mathcal{P}_{\text{CoSB}})
.
∎

Let
\nabla_{\max}
,
\nabla_{\min}
,
\nabla_{i,\min}
, and
K^{\prime}
be the same as in Section
4.3.2.2
. Let
k_{\star}
be the minimum number of arms in the optimal superarm and
\mathcal{M}
be the set of all feasible superarms. As it is not possible to sample
\hat{\mu}_{i}(t)
to be precisely the true value
\mu_{i}
using Beta distribution, we need to consider the
\eta
-neighborhood of
\mu_{i}
, and such
\eta
term is common in the analysis of most Thompson Sampling algorithms (see
Perrault et al. 2020
;
Wang and Chen 2018
for more details). We need the following results to prove
Theorem

2
.

Theorem 9
.

Let
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
be allocation equivalent of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
for instance
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\theta},Q)
. Then, the expected regret of
CSB-MK
in
T
rounds after knowing the allocation equivalent is upper bounded by

16\log_{2}^{2}(16K^{\prime})\sum_{i\in[K]}\frac{\log\left(2^{K^{\prime}}|\mathcal{M}|T\right)}{\nabla_{i,\min}}+\nabla_{\max}(K+1)+\frac{4K(K^{\prime})^{2}\nabla_{\max}}{\left(\nabla_{\min}-2(k_{\star}^{2}+1)\eta\right)^{2}}+\nabla_{\max}\frac{C}{\eta^{2}}\left(\frac{C^{\prime}}{\eta^{4}}\right)^{k_{\star}},

where
C
,
C^{\prime}
are two universal constants, and
\eta\in(0,1)
is such that
\nabla_{\min}-2(k_{\star}^{2}+1)\eta>0
. Further, the expected regret of
CSB-MK
in
T
rounds is also upper bounded by
O\left(\sum\limits_{i\in[K]}\frac{\log^{2}(K^{\prime})\log T}{\nabla_{i,\min}}\right)
.

Proof.

Proof.
Once the allocation equivalent of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is known, the CSB problem is equivalent to a Combinatorial Semi-Bandit problem (from
Proposition

3
). Now the proof of
Theorem

9
follows by verifying Assumptions
1-3
in
Perrault et al. 2020
for the Combinatorial Semi-Bandit problem and applying their regret bound. Assumption
1
states that the agent has access to an oracle that can compute the optimal superarm. Whereas, Assumption
3
states that the losses of arms are bounded and mutually independent. It is clear that both of these assumptions hold for our setting. We next proceed to verify Assumption
2
. For fix allocation
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}
, the mean loss incurred from loss vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is given by
l(M,\boldsymbol{\mu})=\sum_{i\in M}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}
where
M=\left\{i:a_{i}<\hat{\theta}_{i}\right\}
. For any two loss vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle l(M,\boldsymbol{\mu})-l(M,\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in M}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{i}<\hat{\theta}_{i}\right\}}\left(\mu_{i}-\mu_{i}^{\prime}\right)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{$\Bigg($as $\sum_{i\in M}\mu_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mu_{i}\mathds{1}_{\left\{a_{i}<\hat{\theta}_{i}\right\}}\Bigg)$}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{K}\left(\mu_{i}-\mu_{i}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{K}|\mu_{i}-\mu_{i}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=B\parallel\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}\parallel_{1}

where
B=1
. After knowing the allocation equivalent, the allocation to each arm remains the same in each round (
\hat{\theta}_{i}
is given to each arm
i\in[K]\setminus A_{t}
). Thus we are solving a Combinatorial Semi-Bandit with parameter
B=1
. By using Theorem
1
in
Perrault et al. 2020
, we get the desired bounds.
∎

Theorem 10
.

With probability at least
1-\delta
, the expected cumulative regret of
CSB-MK
is upper bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{T}\right]\leq\frac{\log(K\log_{2}\left(\left\lceil 1+\frac{Q}{\gamma}\right\rceil\right)/\delta)}{\log(1/(1-\varepsilon))}

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{i\in A_{\Theta_{n}}}{\log_{2}\left(\left\lceil 1+\frac{Q}{\gamma}\right\rceil\right)}+K{\log_{2}(n+1)}\right]\nabla_{\max}+

\displaystyle\qquad O\left(\sum\limits_{i\in[K]}\frac{\log^{2}(K^{\prime})\log T}{\nabla_{i,\min}}\right).

Proof.

Proof.
The first term of expected regret is due to the estimation of allocation equivalent. It takes
T_{\theta_{n}}
rounds to complete, and
\nabla_{\max}
is the maximum regret that can be incurred in any round. Then the maximum regret due to threshold estimation is bounded by
T_{\theta_{n}}\nabla_{\max}
(replace
T_{\theta_{n}}
by its value). The remaining term in regret corresponds to the expected regret incurred after knowing the allocation equivalent, that is upper bounded by
Theorem

9
.
∎

Let
W_{\delta}
be the same as in Section
4.3.2.2
. We are now ready to give the proof of
Theorem

2
.
See
2

Proof.

Proof.
The regret bound follows from Theorem
10
by setting
\delta=T^{-(\log T)^{-\alpha}}T
and unconditioning the expected regret incurred after knowing the allocation equivalent in
CSB-MK
.
∎

Missing proofs from
Section

4.4

See
5

Proof.

Proof.
Let
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{P}
be the independent Bernoulli random variables where
X_{i}
has mean
\mu_{i}
. The samples from all random variables are observed at the same time. Let
R_{W}
is a random variable that counts the number of rounds needed to observe a sample of ‘
1
’ for any of
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in[P]}
. First, we compute
\mathbb{P}\left\{R_{W}=w\right\}
, i.e.,

\mathbb{P}\left\{R_{W}=w\right\}=\Pi_{i\in[P]}(1-\mu_{i})^{w-1}\left(1-\Pi_{i\in[P]}(1-\mu_{i})\right).

The previous results follows from the fact that there a sample of ‘
1
’ is not observed for any of
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in[P]}
in the first
w-1
rounds and a sample of ‘
1
’ is observed for at least one of the random variable in the
w^{th}
round. The expectation of
R_{W}
is given as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[R_{W}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=1}^{\infty}w\mathbb{P}\left\{R_{W}=w\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=1}^{\infty}w\Pi_{i\in[P]}(1-\mu_{i})^{w-1}\left(1-\Pi_{i\in[P]}(1-\mu_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(1-\Pi_{i\in[P]}(1-\mu_{i})\right)\sum_{w=1}^{\infty}w\Pi_{i\in[P]}(1-\mu_{i})^{w-1}.

Let
\bar{p}=1-\Pi_{i\in[P]}(1-\mu_{i})
, we have

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{E}\left[R_{W}\right]

\displaystyle=\bar{p}\sum_{w=1}^{\infty}w(1-\bar{p})^{w-1}

\displaystyle=\bar{p}\left[\frac{d}{d\bar{p}}\sum_{w=1}^{\infty}-(1-\bar{p})^{w}\right]

\displaystyle=\bar{p}\left[\frac{d}{d\bar{p}}\left(\frac{-1}{\bar{p}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\bar{p}\left(\frac{1}{\bar{p}^{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{\bar{p}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\Pi_{i\in[P]}(1-\mu_{i})}.

CSB-SU
starts equal resources to all
L=K
arm. When a loss is observed for any of the arms, it implies that current resource allocation is a underestimate of threshold and then resources are equally allocated among
L=K-1
arms. Let
T_{\theta}(L)
denote the number of the rounds needed to observe a loss when
L
arms are allocated resources. By taking top
L
arms in each round, the upper bound on expected value of
T_{\theta}(L)
is given as:

\mathbb{E}\left[T_{\theta}(L)\right]\leq\frac{1}{1-\Pi_{i\in[K]/[K-L]}(1-\mu_{i})}.

Note that
M
is the number of arms in the optimal allocation. Consider all wrong values of
L\in\{M+1,M+2,\ldots,K-1,K\}
, the upper bound on expected number of rounds needed to reach to correct allocation, i.e.,
(Q/M)
is given as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[T_{\theta_{s}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{L=M+1}^{K}\mathbb{E}\left[T_{\theta}(L)\right]

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{E}\left[T_{\theta_{s}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{L=M+1}^{K}\frac{1}{1-\Pi_{i\in[K]/[K-L]}(1-\mu_{i})}.

∎

Now, we need the following results to prove the regret bound of
CSB-SU
.

Proof.

Proof.

can be divided into two parts: regret before knowing allocation equivalent and after knowing it. The first part of regret bound is the expected regret incurred while estimating allocation equivalent, which is
\mathbb{E}\left[T_{\theta_{s}}\right]\nabla_{\max}
. The second part of regret is due to the MP-MAB algorithm (MP-TS) and is given by
Theorem

7
.

Proof.

Proof.
The expected number of rounds needed to observe a loss from an under-allocated arm with non-zero mean loss are
1/\mu_{i}
(by Geometric distribution). When a loss is observed for an arm,
CSB-DU
increments resources by
\gamma
amount for that arm. In worse case,
\theta_{i}
or more resources are allocated only after
\left\lfloor\theta_{i}/\gamma\right\rfloor
number of increments in resource allocation for the arm
i
. Therefore, the expected number of rounds needed to estimate
\hat{\theta}_{i}\in[\theta_{i},\left\lceil\theta_{i}/\gamma\right\rceil\gamma]
are
\left\lfloor\theta_{i}/\gamma\right\rfloor(1/\mu_{i})
.

Let consider the worst case where only one threshold is estimated at a time. Then the maximum expected rounds needed to estimate all thresholds are
\sum_{i\in[K]:\mu_{i}\neq 0}\left\lfloor{\theta_{i}}/{\gamma}\right\rfloor\left({1}/{\mu_{i}}\right)
. With this argument, the proof is complete.
∎

Remark:

can estimate thresholds of multiple arms by starting with the same allocation of resources to all arms. Hence the number of rounds needed for finding allocation equivalent might be very small in practice than given in

where the worst case is considered.

, the regret of

and the fact that

.
